  - **Two or more image-library threads for WebP**, for the decode, or for the whole conversion: 0.5 ms for the decode of a 1024 x 640 picture (5.3 ms against 4.8 ms) and none for a 640 x 640 grey one, 2 to 16 percent slower WebP pictures other than the grey drawing with alpha; a third thread helps that drawing (21.6 ms against 23.5 ms) and costs the interface (25.6 ms against 24.6 ms).
  - **Reading the encoded picture in the other order, or turning the image library's operation cache off**: no change.
  - **Re-attaching a JPEG's density from the rounded figure the library reports**: writes 94.000 where the PNG route writes 94.005, so the files differ.
  - **Encoder settings that are faster with the same or better quality on these pictures** (AVIF superblock 64, WebP effort 3) are measured in the pull request and left to a decision: they change the files.
- **Verified by**: `tests/image-avif-direct-handoff.test.ts` (which PNG files the encoder may read as they are, chunk by chunk; the file the encoder is given is the source; the AVIF is the one the encoder writes for that file, run on the source with the recorded arguments; the regular route gives the same bytes, also for 16-bit colour and 16-bit grey pictures whose alpha plane is fully opaque or one step below (the encoder's PNG reader drops a fully opaque plane exactly as the regular route does); a failing encoder is run again on the decoded pixels; an encoder that writes nothing is not; the working directory and the process of an abandoned run are gone), `tests/image-decoded-source-blocks.test.ts` (packet, background, density and stripping: read once, same bytes, and the packet and the density in the output read by `exiftool`), `tests/image-16bit-jpeg-class.test.ts` (the class of a 16-bit colour PNG, the equality with the 8-bit PNG of the nearest levels, resize, background and translucent alpha), `tests/image-header-reads.test.ts` (one header read), `tests/image-threads.test.ts` (the JPEG lease), and the `image` parity rows.
- **Revisit when**: the reference tools change how they read the PNG, a WebP pipeline gets a measured gain from threads, or the AVIF encoder offers a way to read pixels from a pipe without a file.

## 37. Real sample files for every input format

- **Status**: Implemented (issue #780: the format samples and the coverage test, then the evaluation-size sets, the alias rule and the regression corpus), decided 2026-10-16 (QA decision: the sample sets are completed before more speed work); the regression corpus decided 2026-10-17.
- **Decision**:
  - Every input format the registry accepts has at least three real sample files in the corpus manifests (`bench/corpus/remote-manifest.json`, `bench/realworld/manifest.json`, `tests/fixtures/raw/*.json`), or an entry in `bench/realworld/format-coverage-exceptions.json` that states why no further public sample with a usable licence exists and lists the sources searched. `tests/corpus-format-coverage.test.ts` fails for a format with neither, and for an entry whose format has since reached three samples.
  - A real sample is a file published by somebody other than this project. Files that a test or a generator of this repository writes do not count.
  - An extension alias counts the samples of its canonical format (`FORMAT_ALIASES` in `bench/realworld/format-coverage.ts`: `jfif` for `jpg`, `djv` for `djvu`, `tbz` and `tbz2` for `tar.bz2`, `tz` for `tar.z`, `midi` for `mid`, `zstd` for `zst`, `ndjson` for `jsonl`, `latex` for `tex`, `htm` for `html`, `mpeg` for `mpg`, `3gpp` for `3gp`, `weba` for `webm`, `text` for `txt`, `mts` for `m2ts`). The registry has no alias relation, so the table is written by hand and the test checks it: both ids exist, no chain, and the registry accepts the specification signature of the canonical format under the alias extension. Formats that merely share a container (Dolby Vision, an audiobook, an XVID stream) are not aliases. `pdfa` is left out on purpose: the registry rejects the signature of a PDF declared as `pdfa`, which is a finding and not a pair to hide.
  - The evaluation-size sets of the thin families (video, camera RAW, audio and PDF stress files) are split by what judges them. A quick, representative subset per content class goes to the parity sample sets (`bench/corpus/remote-manifest.json`), where the reference tool judges every row; the remainder goes to the real-world corpus, which finds crashes, hangs and bad output. A new parity sample gets a content class of its own and is never added to a class that has baseline rows (a class row is the mean of its samples, so one more sample would move the recorded mean): video samples share the five classes because their class rows are BD-rates against an encoder run with the same arguments, which are 0 by construction. The pull request subset (`QUICK_PUBLIC_SUBSET`) is unchanged, so the new samples are measured by the nightly run, and their rows have no baseline entry until a CI report is added with `--baseline-from-report` (the command refuses a report without a workflow run, so a laptop or a VM cannot supply them). HDR video, containers, encrypted and unreadable PDF files, and the audio formats go to the real-world corpus, because the video rows encode an 8-bit lossless copy of an 8-bit sequence and the encrypted class of the PDF rows is a name the runner tests for.
  - A file's licence is read from the publisher's own list where there is one (the camera sample archive gives the licence and the SHA-256 of every file), not from the statement of the source: 146 of its files are CC BY-NC-SA, not CC0, and one of them was recorded as CC0. Only licences that allow commercial use are accepted, because the service is commercial: Creative Commons NonCommercial (NC) and NoDerivatives (ND) files are excluded from both manifests, whatever the use. The one parity file and the seven real-world files of that kind (all from that archive) were replaced by CC0 files of the same maker and format; where no small CC0 file exists (Hasselblad 3FR, 39 MB and larger, Kodak `.raw`) the format stays on the exceptions list with that reason. 
  - The standard evaluation sets whose terms allow research and development use stay (QA decision 2026-10-17): the EBU SQAM tracks ("not for commercial purposes other than as a R&D tool"), the Kodak Photo CD pictures, the NTIA/ITS sequences ("research purposes") and the Silesia parts whose terms the corpus page does not give. They are for internal testing only: fetched at run time by digest, never committed, shown, redistributed or shipped, and marked as such in both PROVENANCE files. No file of them is in the repository (checked: the repository holds no sample, only the manifests). Why: they are the sets that codec and converter evaluations use, including for commercial products, and the terms permit research and development use, which a conformance and quality benchmark is. Trade-off: the corpus depends on the hosts and the publishers' terms, and a use outside internal testing (shipping a file, a public demo, a published sample table with the pictures) is not covered. Alternative rejected: replacing them with permissive-only sets, which would drop the sets the published results and the reference tools are compared on. Revisit when: a publisher changes a term, or a file of such a set is to be redistributed or shipped.
  - New samples go to the real-world corpus: pinned by origin, size and SHA-256, never committed, fetched when a run needs them, with the licence statement of their source in `bench/realworld/PROVENANCE.md`. Sources are chosen for a stated licence and a pinned address (a commit, a release tag, an archive that keeps old versions); where the publisher states no licence the files are fetched for conversion testing only. Files from exploit databases, fuzzer outputs and files of unknown licence from government forms are not used; the PDF files that the qpdf and PDF.js projects keep for damaged structure (a looping page tree, a wrong cross-reference table) are regression tests of error handling, not fuzzer outputs, and are used, while files named for a fuzzer or a CVE are not.
  - The nightly real-world run converts every sample through three of its format's targets. A crash, hang or bad output of a new sample fails the night unless it is listed in `bench/realworld/known-failures.json` with the issue that tracks the fix.
  - **Regression corpus at the scale of the document engines** (QA decision 2026-10-17). About 20,000 office and PDF files (`bench/realworld/regression-manifest.json`: 19,996 files, 23.5 GB) are drawn from the public government documents corpus (1,000 archives of 1,000 files) and from the PDF files of the CC-MAIN-2021-31 web crawl (40 archives sampled), stratified by format, producing application and size class. The producer is read from the file itself (PDF Producer or Creator, the application name of the OLE SummaryInformation stream, `Application` of the OOXML `docProps/app.xml`) and recorded in the manifest. The government corpus is published as freely redistributable to the best of the publisher's knowledge; the crawl corpus is under the Common Crawl terms of use and the terms of the digital corpora project. Both are used for internal testing only, like the research-term sets above: fetched at run time by digest, never committed, shown, redistributed or shipped.
  - **The corpus is stored nowhere.** The repository holds the manifest only (one file per line, 3.4 MB). A shard reads each file from its archive with range requests, checks the digest, converts it and deletes it, in batches of 200 files; no Actions cache holds the corpus. A file whose bytes differ from the manifest, or that the host does not serve, fails the run as an unavailable file and does not hide the results of the others.
  - **The draw is by stratum, not in proportion to the population.** The budget of each format (and for PDF of each source) is shared equally between its strata; a stratum smaller than its share is taken whole. Rare producers and large files therefore stay in, where a proportional draw would drop them. Files above 8 MiB, empty files, files the real-world corpus already holds, names that mark an exploit, fuzzer or malware sample, crawl PDFs that the corpus's own metadata flags as carrying JavaScript, and, after reading the bytes, files with a Visual Basic project or a PDF script or launch action are left out. UNSAFE-DOCS and any malicious-file or fuzzing corpus are not used; they belong to the pre-launch security batch (#761).
  - **Schedule.** On Sunday the whole corpus runs in 16 shards. On the other six days a seventh of it runs in 4 shards, chosen by the date (the hash of the file's id modulo 7 against the day number modulo 7), so seven nights cover it once; each night also runs a canary (1,352 files today: one per format and producer class, 1,325 of the classes being PDF producers) and every file that failed before (`bench/realworld/regression/failed-before.json`, extended by `merge --record-failures` in a reviewed commit). One job per file, to one target chosen by the hash of the id, so a file keeps its pair when the corpus grows or runs in part.
  - **Judged like the real-world corpus, and against the last full run.** Crash, hang, bad output and refusal as there; in addition, a job that was `ok` in the last full run on `main` (its result set is kept as the `regression-baseline` artifact for 90 days) and is not now is a regression and fails the run, even when no reference tool can open the file. Known failures are issue-linked entries of `bench/realworld/regression/known-failures.json`, as for the real-world corpus; one that explains a regression keeps the run green.
- **Why the regression corpus is right in practice**: the open document engines test their import and export against corpora of tens of thousands of documents (about 80,000 for the office suite's runs), and the text extraction and PDF libraries run more than a terabyte of government and web-crawl files, previous release against release candidate. A reader written against a few thousand files passes them and fails on the next producer's output; the differential run needs no reference, so it covers the files that no reference tool can open, which are the ones where a regression goes unseen. The commercial services publish no test sets, so there is nothing to copy; the public corpora are the nearest equivalent.
- **Why it is right in practice**: published evaluations of converters and decoders use corpora of files from many producers, because a format reader written against one producer's files passes its own tests and fails on the next application's output. Format-detection and conversion test suites of mature open-source projects therefore keep one example file per producer and version, and track files they cannot handle in a list instead of deleting them. A count per format that a test checks stops the corpus from drifting back to the formats that are easy to find.
- **Trade-offs**: the nightly corpus grows by about 600 files (about 370 MB across the ten shard caches, and about ten percent more jobs); some hosts (a mirror, a sample collection) may move a file, which fails the run until the file is re-pinned. A format with fewer than three public files stays on the exceptions list with its reason, and the third sample of a rare format is often a test file of a library rather than a document from a user. The producer of a file is recorded where the publisher states it but not enforced: whether a second producer exists is a finding of the search. The new parity samples add about 20 minutes to the nightly quality parity job measured on a 4-vCPU Linux machine (the 20 PDF files take 4 minutes, the 14 audio tracks 4, the 4 video sequences 6, the 6 camera files 6; measured as the per-file time of a run of 54 PDF files, 40 tracks, 14 sequences and 25 camera files that took 11, 12, 20 and 23 minutes), on a job that took 18 to 57 minutes over the last two weeks and is cut at 60; the job should be split by family (a workflow change that this decision does not make). The pull request quality job is unchanged apart from running once for every family, because a change of `remote-manifest.json` maps to all of them.
- **Trade-offs of the regression corpus**:
  - **Transfer and runner time.** A full run reads about 23.5 GB from the publisher and takes 16 jobs of about 26 minutes of conversion (sized from a trial of 3,600 jobs, 18 % of the corpus, on the CI runner: 3.65 s a job on 3 workers, against the 3.1 s that the times of the real-world run give for this mix; the stratified draw takes more large files than that corpus does, and one PDF in the trial took 7 minutes alone); a night takes 4 jobs of about 21 minutes. Hosted runners of a public repository are free; the weekly run adds about seven hours of runner time.
  - **Sources and terms.** The corpus depends on the hosts and the publishers' terms, and a use outside internal testing (a shipped file, a public sample table) is not covered. A host that moves a file fails the run until the file is re-pinned.
  - **The government corpus has few OOXML files** (the sources hold 162 docx, 37 xlsx and 204 pptx files within the size cap), so those strata are small; its office files are the 2003 formats (doc, xls, ppt, pps). Producers rarer than about one file in a thousand of their format may not be in the pool the draw is made from.
  - **The baseline is up to a week old.** A regression introduced on Monday is reported every night until the next Sunday run records it; by then it has to be fixed or listed.
  - **A web crawl can hold a hostile PDF** that the checks above do not find; the converters run sandboxed and the tooling that builds the manifest only reads bytes.
  - **The size cap of 8 MiB** leaves out the largest presentations (about 7 % of the government corpus's ppt files), which the real-world corpus and the stress sets cover.
- **Alternatives rejected**:
  - **Generating samples with the converters' reference tools**: a file we wrote tests our own producer; the point of the rule is inputs we did not write.
  - **Committing the files**: several sources state no licence or restrict redistribution, and the repository would grow by hundreds of megabytes.
  - **A minimum of 10 samples per format**: the rarer formats (Palm eBook variants, StarOffice generations, Real audio) have two or three public files in total.
  - **Adding every sample to the parity quality families**: each sample would need an oracle and a baseline row, and the nightly job has no room for them; the real-world run needs neither and finds crashes, hangs and wrong outputs on all of them. The parity sets get a small representative subset per class and the real-world corpus the rest.
  - **More samples in an existing class**: a class row is the mean over its samples, so a sample added to `encrypted`, `tagged` or a camera maker's class would move a recorded value without any change in the product.
  - **The full VCTK and LibriSpeech archives as the speech set**: the VCTK archive is a 11.7 GB ZIP64 file that the range reader of the fetcher does not read, and LibriSpeech is a gzip-compressed tar that cannot be read in ranges; the clean test set of the Voice Bank database (the same recordings, 154 MB, CC BY 4.0) is a plain ZIP.
  - **A cache of the regression corpus in Actions**: it would be 10 GB or more against a limit of 10 GB for the whole repository; the files are read again by range each time.
  - **A draw in proportion to the population**: it would follow the producer mix of the web and drop the rare applications, where readers fail.
  - **Building the previous `main` in every job for the differential**: it doubles the set-up of each shard; the result set of the last full run costs one artifact download and is the same comparison.
  - **Running the whole corpus every night**: about five hours of runner time a night for little new information; a seventh and the canary find a regression within a week and the canary within a day.
  - **A differential against the reference tools**: they cannot open many of the files, and a file they cannot open is exactly the case the differential run is for.
  - **The SafeDocs stressful PDF corpus**: it states no licence, warns that it may hold malicious files and is published as six tar archives of 31 GB.
  - **The EBU tracks in the real-world corpus**: the host answers parallel range requests with HTTP 429 and the real-world fetcher does not wait for `Retry-After` as `bench/corpora.ts` does, so a shard would fail on a rate limit.
- **Verified by** (regression corpus): `tests/regression-corpus-producer.test.ts` (producers read from PDF, OLE and OOXML files built by independent writers), `tests/regression-corpus-manifest.test.ts` (the stratified draw against hand-worked allocations and the manifest file), `tests/regression-corpus-schedule.test.ts` (the nights, the canary and the job plan, with buckets computed independently), `tests/regression-corpus-differential.test.ts` and `tests/regression-corpus-merge.test.ts` (the differential judge, the gate, the summary), `tests/regression-corpus-judge.test.ts` (range reads from a local server, deletion after judging, unavailable files), `tests/regression-corpus-build.test.ts` and `tests/regression-corpus-candidates.test.ts` (the manifest build rules), `tests/regression-corpus-workflow.test.ts` (schedule, shards, permissions, no Actions cache) and `tests/regression-corpus-manifest-real.test.ts` (the committed manifest: sources, exclusions, no overlap, shard sizing).
- **Verified by**: `tests/corpus-format-coverage.test.ts` (the counting rule on synthetic manifests, the alias table against the registry and the signatures of the canonical formats, then the real manifests against the registry, the exceptions list and the provenance notes), a run of every new parity sample and every new real-world file on Ubuntu 24.04 in a Linux VM (the parity verdict of each family and the real-world gate with the committed known failures), `tests/realworld-corpus.test.ts` (manifest validation, plan and gate), and the nightly real-world run.
- **Revisit when**: a format on the exceptions list gets a public sample, the registry gains a format, or a source stops publishing a pinned file, a shard of the regression corpus gets within 15 minutes of the job limit, the first full runs show that the baseline age hides regressions, or a source of OOXML and OpenDocument files with producer variety becomes available.

## 38. Pictures the image library cannot read: decoders, signatures and refusals

- **Status**: Implemented (issue #783, image rows), decided 2026-10-17 (QA decision: every format the registry accepts converts its real files, or is refused with a typed answer that names a real limit of the file).
- **Decision**:
  - The image library (libvips through `sharp`) has no loader for PSD, XCF, TGA, PCX, DDS, the PNM family and JPEG 2000, its libheif has no HEVC decoder (every HEIC file failed with `bad seek`, the message of the sequential read that follows the failed plugin load; the image library says the same for a file path as for a buffer, so the way the bytes are handed over is not the cause: the cause is the missing HEVC decoder, named in the line after the seek messages), and its libtiff is built without old-style JPEG compression. These sources are decoded by a command-line decoder to a lossless PNG (a Deflate TIFF for a file with several pages) and the PNG goes through the same pipeline as any other picture, so resize, colour handling and every target behave as for a PNG:
    - **ImageMagick** (`magick`, or `convert` of version 6) reads PSD (the merged image), XCF (the layers drawn on the canvas), TGA, PCX, DDS, PNM, PBM, PGM, PPM, HEIC and HEIF (libheif with its libde265 module), and TIFF files whose directory declares Compression = 6.
    - **OpenJPEG** (`opj_decompress`) reads JP2 files and bare JPEG 2000 codestreams. ImageMagick's own coder is not used for them: it takes the size of a picture whose reference grid has an offset as the grid size less twice the offset, so a codestream that starts at (150, 300) of a 2742 x 2244 grid came out 2442 x 1644 instead of 2592 x 1944 (ITU-T T.800 B.2 defines the size as the grid less its offset; the reference decoder and a third decoder agree).
  - Both are unmodified separate programs, run in the process sandbox with no network, a time limit, a resident-memory limit, an output-size limit and a private 0700 directory, on files with fixed names and with an argument vector that holds no request text. ImageMagick is told the coder on the command line (`psd:in[0]`), so it never guesses the format from the content, and it runs under a policy of the repository (`docker/imagemagick/policy.xml`, named by `MAGICK_CONFIGURE_PATH` on every run, root-owned and read-only in the worker image at `/etc/easyconvert/imagemagick`, the same file in CI): every coder and delegate denied and `@` file lists denied, then only PSD, XCF, TGA, PCX, DDS, PNM, PBM, PGM, PPM, PAM, HEIC and HEIF allowed to read and TIFF, PNG and INFO to read and write, with limits on width, height (100,000 pixels), area, memory, map, disk (2 GiB, the budget of one job), list length, threads and time, so a future source without a coder prefix still cannot reach MVG, MSL, URL, text or delegate coders (without the policy directory the conversion is refused with HTTP 503). The pixel cache that spills past the memory limit is written inside the job directory (`MAGICK_TEMPORARY_PATH`) and removed with it, and the size probe may write at most 1 MiB; a warning is a failure (`-regard-warnings`), so a file that ends early is refused instead of answered with the part that was read (the libtiff coder is the exception: libtiff warns about every private tag it does not know). The declared size is read from the header first (`-ping`, or the SIZ segment for JPEG 2000) and held to the input pixel limit before any pixel is decoded; the sum over all pages is held to it too.
  - Without the program the conversion fails with HTTP 503 (`EngineUnavailableError`), like the RAW decoder; there is no second decoder to fall back to. A file the decoder cannot read fails with HTTP 422 (`Invalid image: the <format> picture could not be decoded`); the decoder's own words go to the log, not to the client.
  - The XCF canvas is taken from the file header (the canvas size, then the layers drawn on it). ImageMagick's canvas is not used: for GIMP 3 files (XCF version 19 and later) it takes the first layer's size and crops every other layer (a 1900 x 550 logo came out 1000 x 525 with a layer missing).
  - A HEIC or HEIF picture is turned once: libheif applies the rotation and mirroring of the file (`irot`, `imir`, `clap`), and the EXIF orientation of a HEIF file is not applied on top of them (ISO/IEC 23008-12 6.5.10), so the orientation of an EXIF chunk the decoder wrote is set to 1 before the picture goes on. A HEIC file written with both an `irot` property and EXIF orientation 6 (what libheif's own encoder writes) comes out upright, 30 x 60 and not 60 x 30.
  - Signatures (the spoof check): a gzip member (RFC 1952 header: deflate method, no reserved flag) is accepted under `.svgz` when its inflated head opens with an `svg` root element (the next item), and the conversion inflates it with a bound and refuses a stream that is not an SVG; a PostScript file is accepted under `.ai` when its first 8 KiB carry the Illustrator prolog (`%%Creator: Adobe Illustrator`, an `%AI<n>_` comment or the `Adobe_Illustrator_AI<n>` procedure set; PDF-compatible files already passed) and is converted as PostScript; the HEIF structural brands `mif2` and `mif3` (the minimized image file) join `mif1` and `msf1`; an Ultra HDR image, which is a JPEG with a gain map, is accepted under the `ultrahdr` target name, which the real-world run had called a bad output for every RAW and DNG file converted to it. The conversion of an `.svgz` file still refuses a stream that is not an SVG document (the check is `isSvg` of the inflated bytes, a typed 422), as a second line behind the signature check, which refuses a gzip of a PNG, of text, of a page or of a program first. Every other combination stays refused: a gzip stream or a PostScript file without the prolog under another image name, an ISO base media file with a video brand under a HEIF name, and bytes that are not a JPEG under `ultrahdr`.
  - Signatures of other kinds (the spoof check; issue #783, signature rows). The check refuses a file whose content is another kind than its name says, and accepts every content variant of the kind it names. The variants follow the open signature databases (shared-mime-info, libmagic's `Magdir`, and Tika's media type definitions), and where those and the format specifications differ the specification decides.
    - **Compressed XML** (`.svgz`, `.zabw`): two checks. The registry (browser components and edge engines import it, so it carries no decoder) takes the name only for a well-formed gzip member (RFC 1952 header: deflate method, no reserved flag) and refuses every other bytes, text and unrecognised content included. The server side gate (`assertNotSpoofedFile` of `src/lib/security/file-guard.ts`, which every upload path, the worker and the conversion entry point call) then inflates the head of the member with Node's zlib and requires the document's root element, `svg` or `abiword`, after a byte order mark, white space, a declaration, comments and a doctype with its internal subset. A gzip of a page, of another XML document, of a picture, of a program or of noise is refused, as is an SVG under `.zabw` and an AbiWord document under `.svgz`. The inflate is bounded: the member is read in slices that double from 64 bytes, each with a 64 KiB output cap and `Z_SYNC_FLUSH`, and the longest slice whose output fits is the head, so a stream that expands past the cap (a 50 MiB run of zeros is tested) is never inflated further and a window that ends inside the stream gives the bytes decoded so far. A stream that is not valid gzip is refused. The gzip names (`.gz`, `.tgz`) still take any member. No new dependency.
    - **Raw AAC** (`.aac`, `.adts`): ADTS frames (a 12-bit sync word with layer bits 00), ADTS frames behind an ID3v2 tag (the frame after the tag, whose size is read from the synchsafe field and the footer flag, is what decides), an ADIF header (which has no signature the sniff names, so it is not refused; a text file that opens with the word `ADIF`, such as an amateur radio log, is not taken for AAC), and an audio MP4 file (major brand `M4A ` or `M4B `, which is what such a file is when it is saved as `.aac`). An ID3 tag that runs past a full 64 KiB window (cover art) cannot be followed, so it is accepted under the AAC names on its well-formed header alone; a file shorter than the window that ends inside or right after its tag has no frame and is refused; an ID3 file whose frames are MPEG audio stays MP3, and a program, a picture or a RIFF WAVE file is refused. This is the one place where the check accepts on a header it cannot read past: a file that opens with a well-formed ID3v2 header whose tag is longer than the window is accepted as AAC with no frame in view, as `.mp3` already accepts any ID3 file.
    - **DivX and Xvid** (`.divx`, `.xvid`): a RIFF container of form type `AVI `, which is what these files are (the codec is MPEG-4 Part 2 inside AVI). A RIFF WAVE or WebP file, a picture or a program under those names is refused. A DivX file in another container (the real file `LMLM4_CIFat30fps.divx` is an MPEG-4 elementary stream with MP3 audio, which no signature names) was never refused and is not now.
    - **Compound-file documents** (`.sdw`, `.sdc`, `.sda`, `.shw`, `.qpw`): the OLE2 compound file signature. The kind is told apart by the CLSID of the root storage in shared-mime-info and Tika, but the StarOffice 3.1 files of the corpus (`Writer_3.1.sdw`, `Calc_3.1.sdc`, `Draw_3.1.sda`) carry other CLSIDs than the 5.2 files that those databases list, the Corel Presentations and Quattro Pro 9 files carry CLSIDs that they do not list, and the root directory of a larger file lies past the header window, so the container signature is what the check can rely on. These formats exist only as compound files, so bytes without the signature (a page, a program, a ZIP package, text) are refused, which the check does not do for formats that also have a text form.
    - **Order of the signatures**: a fixed signature (a DOS or Windows program, an ELF program, a Parquet file, PNG, PDF and the like) wins over an ISO base media box whose `ftyp` type sits at byte 4, so a program or a Parquet file with `ftyp` there is refused under `.mp4` (it used to be read as video). A format that is only a gzip member of XML (`.svgz`, `.zabw`) or only a compound file is refused when the bytes sniff as anything else or as nothing.
    - **HEIF**: the AVC image brands `avci` and `avcs` (ISO/IEC 23008-12) join the HEVC and structural brands; any other video brand is still refused under the HEIF names.
  - Formats the decoders cannot read in full are refused with the typed 422 and are not removed from the registry: DDS pixel formats that ImageMagick does not read (a DX10 uncompressed RGBA surface on version 7.1.2; the three real DDS files of the corpus on version 6.9.12, which reads the surfaces written from the format document), HEIF files of the minimized `mif3` kind and of the uncompressed codec with row alignment on libheif 1.17, and a PCX file that ends inside its pixels.
- **Why it is right in practice**: the reference service converts these formats with ImageMagick (its public formats list names PSD, XCF, TGA, HEIC and SVGZ as converted by its image engine), so these files convert there and the registry advertises them here. OpenJPEG is the reference implementation of JPEG 2000 and the decoder every mature toolkit links. Reading the header before the pixels and refusing a truncated file are what the image library already does for the formats it reads (its default is to fail on a warning).
- **Trade-offs**: one more process per conversion of these formats. Measured on Ubuntu 24.04 (ImageMagick 6.9.12, libheif 1.17, OpenJPEG 2.5): starting ImageMagick and decoding a small PCX file takes 4 ms, a 1280 x 720 HEIC file 0.19 s, and OpenJPEG decodes a 2592 x 1944 JPEG 2000 file in 2.3 s (its own time). The worker image gains about 15 MB of packages (ImageMagick without its `-extra` coders, so no SVG, EXR, DjVu, WMF or Pango; the libde265 module; the OpenJPEG tools). HEVC is a patent-encumbered codec, as it already is in the video path (`ffmpeg`), and its decoder is the distribution package. The decoded PNG is written once and read once (fastest zlib level, no row filter). A policy file read after ours would win over it, so the worker image overwrites the distribution's own file (`/etc/ImageMagick-7/policy.xml`, from `imagemagick-7-common`, the only policy file the Debian 13 packages ship) with ours, and its build fails unless `scripts/verify-imagemagick-policy.sh` finds that ImageMagick lists no policy file but ours and that one, and that neither allows anything ours does not. On the Ubuntu test image, where the file is not overwritten, the distribution's file only denies (URL and HTTP delegates). The worker image installs the libheif module of one codec only, `libheif-plugin-libde265` (HEVC), because `--no-install-recommends` leaves out the others (AV1, JPEG 2000, AVC and the encoders); the image build prints the installed libheif packages so that an extra one is seen. The PCX, DDS and PNM readers are ImageMagick's, so a quirk of its readers is ours; the tests judge them against pictures written from the format documents, with known pixels.
- **Alternatives rejected**:
  - **Loaders inside the image library** (libheif with an HEVC decoder, ImageMagick or OpenJPEG loaders in libvips): the prebuilt `sharp` binaries cannot be extended without building and shipping our own libvips, and an HEVC decoder linked into the application is a licence and patent question that a separate process keeps outside it.
  - **Writing the decoders in the application** (PNM, TGA, PCX, DDS, PSD and XCF readers): format code to maintain, with corner cases that ImageMagick has years of reports on, for formats whose reference engine is ImageMagick.
  - **ImageMagick for JPEG 2000**: wrong size for offset grids (above).
  - **`heif-dec` instead of ImageMagick for HEIC**: one more package for the same libheif; ImageMagick is needed for the other formats anyway and is the reference engine.
  - **Falling back to a second decoder** when the first fails (ffmpeg reads the DDS formats that ImageMagick 6 does not, and the other way round for DX10 files): a chain of decoders per format hides which one produced a picture; the typed refusal names the limit.
  - **An ICC-managed conversion of CMYK PSD files**: ImageMagick converts to sRGB without the profile, as the reference does; not changed here.
- **Verified by**: `tests/spoof-check-variants.test.ts` (the real files of the compressed XML, AAC, DivX and compound-file rows from the corpus, each also read by libmagic's `file` where it is installed; headers and gzip members written from the layouts in the specifications; the refusals of every kind above; the head inflater against Node's zlib as the oracle), `tests/image-format-signatures.test.ts` (the three signatures and the Ultra HDR name, on real files and on headers written from the specifications, with the refusals), `tests/image-external-decode.test.ts` (TGA, PCX, PNM, PSD and DDS written from the format documents with known pixels, real XCF files against the canvas their headers state, truncated, wrong and oversized files, the missing engine), `tests/image-jpeg2000-decode.test.ts` (lossless files written by OpenJPEG's encoder decoded to the pixels that went in, with an offset and at 16 bits, real files against the reference decoder), `tests/image-heif-decode.test.ts` (real HEIC and HEIF files against libheif's own decoder, JPEG, AVIF and GIF of each against it by PSNR over the colours on white with a floor of 30 dB where 32.8 dB was the least measured, the rotated file, the EXIF chunk rewrite), `tests/image-magick-policy.test.ts` (the rules `-list policy` reports on ImageMagick 6.9 and 7.1, coders the route never calls refused: MVG, MSL, text, label, canvas, `@` lists and URLs, the width limit, where the policy is looked for), `tests/image-magick-policy-image.test.ts` (the Dockerfile step as text, and the verification script against listings of right and wrong images), `tests/svgz-payload.test.ts`, `tests/image-old-style-jpeg-tiff.test.ts` (the directory walk, and the real file against the JPEG stream it carries decoded by the image library's JPEG decoder without libtiff: PNG and TIFF within 50 dB, the targets that encode again within 40 dB, where 53.7 dB and 43.3 dB were the least measured; on Ubuntu 24.04 with ImageMagick 6.9.12 and libheif 1.17 with its libde265 module, and on macOS with ImageMagick 7.1.2), a Linux real-world run of the affected sources on Ubuntu 24.04 (ImageMagick 6.9.12, libheif 1.17), and the image parity rows.
- **Revisit when**: a worker image carries a libheif that reads `mif3` or row-aligned uncompressed HEIF, ImageMagick's DDS reader gains the DX10 formats, a conversion of these formats needs a faster path than a process, or the prebuilt image library gains HEVC and JPEG 2000 loaders.

## 39. Layer cache of the worker image in the container registry

- **Status**: Implemented (epic #763, row "Actions cache over its 10 GB limit"), decided 2026-10-17 (QA decision).
- **Decision**:
  - **The layer cache of the CI `container` job** (`Dockerfile.worker`, all layers, `mode=max`) is kept in the container registry of the repository, in a package named after it: `ghcr.io/<owner>/<repo>-worker:buildcache`, lower case. It no longer uses the Actions cache.
  - **Only a push to `main` writes it.** The job logs in to the registry with the workflow token (`packages: write`, granted to this job alone) on pushes only, and only a push passes `cache-to`. A pull request, including one from a fork, reads the cache and cannot change it, so a pull request cannot put layers into the cache that later builds trust.
  - **The job runs on `main` when a file that changes the image changes** (`CONTAINER_PATHS`, which includes `ci.yml` and the actions), so the cache is written by the merge that changes the image, and at no other time. A push that changes none of them leaves the cache as it is.
  - **A cache that is not there is a build without it**, not a failure: a step asks the registry for the cache manifest and passes `cache-from` only when it answers. This covers the first run, and a package that is still private (an anonymous pull is refused).
  - **The Actions cache stays** for the dependency and tool installs and the public parity samples. The regression corpus (entry 37) uses none: it is read by byte range and deleted after each file.
- **Why**:
  - On 2026-10-17 the repository held 10.47 GB in 237 Actions cache entries, over the 10 GB limit. Eviction is least recently used across every cache of the repository, so the image layers (182 of the entries, 5.4 GB, `buildkit-blob-*` and their index entries) pushed out the sample, tool and dependency caches that other jobs depend on, and those jobs fetched everything again.
  - A registry is where layered images keep their cache in practice: it is not counted against the Actions budget, every branch and pull request can read what `main` wrote, and nothing evicts it by use. Writing it from `main` only keeps the cache the product of reviewed, merged files.
- **Trade-offs**:
  - **The package must be public for pull requests to read it.** Package visibility is a setting of the owner of the repository, not something a workflow sets; until the package is public (or when a fork's token cannot read it) pull requests build without the cache, which is slower but correct. The first write happens when this change merges.
  - **`mode=max` keeps every layer**, so the package grows with each change of the image. Old versions have to be pruned from the package's version list from time to time.
  - **The cache is refreshed only when the image paths change on `main`**. A pull request whose base is older than the last such change still reads a cache that is newer than its base, which is harmless for a content-addressed layer cache.
  - **The cache exporter's compatibility with the registry is exercised by the first write**, not by this change's tests.
- **Alternatives rejected**:
  - **Raising the Actions cache limit**: a paid feature.
  - **`mode=min` in the Actions cache**: keeps only the final layers, but every pull request still writes its own entry, so the growth that caused the eviction stays.
  - **A local cache directory saved with the Actions cache action**: the same budget.
  - **No cache**: the container job rebuilds the whole image on each pull request that touches an image path, and its 20-minute limit is close without it.
- **Verified by**: `tests/container-ci-job.test.ts` (the cache strings of the build step, the login step and its condition, the permission, the lower-case name by running the step with a stand-in `docker`, and the container job running on a push that changes the image or `ci.yml`), and the first push to `main` after the merge, which writes the package.
- **Revisit when**: the package storage grows beyond what the repository may use for free, the registry stops accepting the cache format, or the container job needs to run on every push.

## 40. Formats only the office suite reads, templates, refused Word documents and XPS text

- **Status**: Implemented (issue #783, office, legacy document and e-book rows), decided 2026-10-17 (QA request in issue #783: every format the registry accepts converts its real files, or is refused with a typed answer that names a real limit of the file; a conversion never returns the bytes of a binary file read as text).
- **Decision**:
  - The office suite (LibreOffice) is the reader of the formats no in-process reader covers: Works (`wps`), WordPerfect (`wpd`), OpenOffice.org 1.x (`sxw`, `sxi`, `sxd`), StarOffice (`sdw`, `sdc`, `sda`), Lotus Word Pro (`lwp`), AbiWord (`zabw`), Quattro Pro (`qpw`), Numbers, Publisher (`pub`), and the Sony BBeB (`lrf`), Palm database (`pdb`) and RocketBook (`rb`) e-books. The in-process engine never sees their bytes: the converter that used to take them for UTF-8 text produced garbage for some targets (`.wps` to text, `.wpd` to PDF, `.xps` to HTML all "succeeded") and an untyped error for the others. Without the office suite the answer is an unavailable engine (HTTP 503); a file it cannot read, or stops on, is a typed `ConversionFailedError` that names the reason.
  - The office suite writes the target itself when it has the filter: PDF, the Office formats of the application that opened the source (docx, odt, rtf for a word processor; xlsx, xls, ods for a spreadsheet; pptx, odp for a presentation), plain text for a word processor and the e-book readers, and page images through its PDF and Poppler. For every other target it writes an interchange file and the in-process converter of that format finishes: `docx` for html, markdown and EPUB, `xlsx` for CSV, plain text for the targets the in-process engine writes from text only (Palm family, Sony and Open eBook, XPS, Hangul; a test pins this set against the registry: they are the targets offered for a text file and not for a Word document). An archive target (`zip`) packs the file as it is and needs no interchange file. The result is what the same conversion of a native file of the interchange format gives; the output path of the request is never used for the intermediate file. The suite's text export is asked for UTF-8 without a byte order mark, as the in-process text engine writes it.
  - A Word, Excel or PowerPoint template or slide show is the file layout of its plain format under another extension, and is read by the reader of that format: `dot` as `doc`, `xlt` as `xls`, `pps` and `pot` as `ppt`, `ott` as `odt`, `otp` as `odp`. They are listed in one table (`OFFICE_FORMAT_ALIAS`), the office suite opens the file under its own name.
  - A Word, Excel or PowerPoint file that the strict in-process reader refuses as malformed (`LegacyOfficeFormatError`, a corrupt compound file) is tried by the office suite before the refusal stands, when the file starts like a binary Office document (the OLE2 compound file signature, or the start of a Word 5.0 for Macintosh file): the suite reads damaged files the way the application that wrote them does, and the Word 6 and Macintosh Word 5 files that real collections carry under `.doc`. An HTML, RTF or text body under one of these names is not handed over, because the suite would load it without the staged HTML path and its image fetch guard (a `file:` image link would be embedded from the worker filesystem); the reader's refusal stands for it. The text of a Word document and the targets written from text come from the suite's text export; the others from its `docx`, `xlsx` or `pptx`. If the suite cannot read the file either, the reader's own refusal is the answer.
  - Two defects of the Word reader are fixed, because they made 39 of the 47 refused Word files of the regression-corpus trial unreadable: the streams of an embedded object (`ObjectPool/<id>/WordDocument`, `1Table`) replaced the document's own streams of the same name in the compound file reader, so the reader parsed the object (a root-level stream now always wins over a nested one of the same name); and the piece that holds the end of the main story and goes on into the footnote story was taken for a break of the piece table (the next piece is now expected where the previous piece ends, not where the main story ends).
  - A file the office suite itself stops on is answered with a typed refusal that says so: "Unspecified Application Error" or a fatal signal in its stderr. The Word report `gd-579820.doc` (embedded Excel objects) crashes LibreOffice 24.2 in the text layout (`SwTextFrame::Format`) for every office target and every fresh process (three runs in a standalone profile, one through the pool, which loses the shared process); the same file converts with LibreOffice 26.8. The crash was in the `.doc` to `.docx` row; the same file still converts to text and HTML, which the in-process reader writes.
  - Draw documents are not presentations. `sxd` is a drawing; LibreOffice has no export from Draw to an Impress format, so `sxd` to `odp` and `pptx` are withdrawn (they never worked) and `sxd` offers PDF and the page images, as `odg` does. The StarOffice drawing `sda` is the same case (its import opens Draw, `--convert-to odp` has no export filter, and a `pptx` written with the Impress filter holds no slide), so `sda` to `odp` and `pptx` are withdrawn and `sda` offers PDF and the page images. Once its signature passed the spoof check these StarOffice, AbiWord and Quattro Pro files reached the in-process text extractor, which returned the compound file as mojibake text for `txt` and `csv` targets; they are office-suite sources, written through `docx` (documents) or `xlsx` (spreadsheets) for the targets the suite does not write. Corel Presentations (`shw`) has no import filter in LibreOffice (it prints the file as binary text), so its `pdf` and `pptx` stay typed refusals and only `zip` converts.
  - XPS and OpenXPS have no renderer in the worker image, so only their text is read: the glyph runs of the fixed pages (ECMA-388), grouped into lines by baseline, in reading order, with the space that a style change leaves out put back from the measured advance of the run, and right-to-left runs read from the right. A package is read under limits: every part is read once (a package that names a document or a page twice is refused as corrupt), at most 1,000 documents and 10,000 pages, 64 MiB for one part and one 256 MiB inflate budget for all parts together, and the job signal is checked before each part, so a small package cannot make the reader work for hours. `xps` to text and HTML work; `xps` to PDF is withdrawn (and `oxps` to PDF), like the page images and vector formats of the reference list that have no engine. A text-only PDF would drop every picture and drawing without saying so.
  - RocketBook (`rb`) files are read by libe-book in LibreOffice 26.8 but not by LibreOffice 24.2 (its filter list has no RocketBook type, and neither of the two real files loads). Where the filter is missing the answer is a typed refusal that names the missing import filter. The registry keeps the format.
  - `pot` to XPS is withdrawn with the other presentation-to-XPS pairs, because it has no engine.
  - **Lotus Word Pro and the OpenDocument spreadsheet template** (follow-up row of issue #783): `lwp` is a Word Pro document that no in-process reader covers (the text path refused every real file as binary), so it is an office-suite source like `wps` and `wpd`: the suite writes PDF, `doc`, `docx`, `odt`, `rtf` and text, the page images come from its PDF, and HTML and Markdown come from its `docx`. Without the suite the answer is the unavailable engine. `ots` is the file layout of `ods` under another mimetype, so it joins `ott` and `otp` as an alias: the suite opens it under its own name, and the in-process spreadsheet reader reads its cells when the suite is missing (before, the CSV of a template was the package bytes read as text, and PDF failed untyped). The StarOffice formats `sdw`, `sdc` and `sda` were routed through the suite by the first change of this entry and are covered by the same real-file tests.
  - **Pairs opened because the suite writes them directly** (the same row): `wps` and `wpd` to `doc`, `rtf`, `jpg` and `png`; `numbers` to `pdf`, `jpg` and `png`; `pps` to `ppt`, `jpg` and `png`. Each was withdrawn only because the registry had not been told that the writer application of the source writes them: the suite saves a word processor's file as Word 97-2003 or RTF, a spreadsheet's as PDF, a presentation's as PowerPoint 97-2003, and the page images come from the suite's PDF like those of `docx` and `ppt`. They were opened after the real files passed the checks below, and without the suite they are an unavailable engine (503), like the other pairs only the suite writes (a slide show to `ppt` reaches the reader of `ppt` as the pair `ppt` to `ppt`, which is now listed with the pairs only the suite writes). `numbers` to `doc`, `ppt` and `tsv` stay withdrawn: a spreadsheet has no export filter for a text document or a presentation.
  - **WPS Presentation (`dps`) is not routed**: no public file of the format exists (a search of the test data of LibreOffice, Apache POI and Tika, the Open Preservation Foundation corpus and a code search for the extension found only other formats), and routing it to a reader without a single real file would be a guess about the container; the typed refusal stays and the format keeps its coverage exception.
- **Why it is right in practice**: the reference service converts Works, WordPerfect, Publisher and OpenDocument templates with the office suite, and the Palm, Sony and RocketBook e-books with calibre; the office suite's importers are the document-format libraries that LibreOffice depends on, so they are already in the worker image. Reading a format with the application's own importer rather than treating bytes as text is what every converter does; reading templates and slide shows as the plain format is how the same package families are already handled (`dotx`, `ppsx`, macro-enabled files). The second reader for refused documents follows the rule that a strict parser is a quality gate, not a verdict: both readers are open source, run in the same sandbox, and the refusal stands when neither reads the file.
- **Trade-offs**: a conversion through an interchange file costs one more office-suite run (0.3 to 1.9 s on a warm pool, measured on Ubuntu 24.04) and the fidelity of the interchange format: the CSV of a Numbers file carries the display of the cells that LibreOffice writes into the workbook (a cell shown with no decimals is written without them), as the CSV of any workbook does. A refused Word document that only the office suite reads costs its run time, and a document it crashes on stays refused. The plain-text export of a Word document leaves out page headers, text frames and footnotes (as the in-process reader does). RocketBook files need an e-book converter this worker does not have; LibreOffice 26.8 has the filter, so the answer changes with the image. HTML, Markdown and EPUB of a word-processor source come from its `docx`, whose reader reads body text, tables, lists and images; text in frames and shapes that the suite's `docx` export leaves out is not in them.
- **Alternatives rejected**:
  - **Reading the bytes as text and refusing the binary ones** (the old behaviour): a bad answer for the formats whose bytes happen to pass the check.
  - **calibre in the worker image** for the e-books: GPL-3.0 and a large Python and Qt distribution; LibreOffice already reads Sony, Palm and Plucker files, and only RocketBook is missing. Offered to QA with the size and licence instead of added.
  - **MuPDF for XPS** (the reference tool for XPS pages): AGPL-3.0 and a new engine; offered to QA with the size and licence. A drawing renderer written for XPS (paths, fonts with obfuscated TrueType resources, brushes, images) is a project of its own.
  - **A text-only PDF of an XPS document** and **an in-process text-only `.docx` for a Word file LibreOffice crashes on**: both drop what the file shows without saying so.
  - **Retrying the crash with a fresh process or another filter option**: three fresh processes and the pool all stop at the same place, and a profile that turns the converters of embedded Office objects off (tried once) fails the same way.
  - **Mapping every office-suite failure to a refusal**: only the load failures, the empty outputs and the crash signatures are; a timeout stays a timeout (`CpuTaskTimeoutError`) and an unavailable sandbox stays unavailable.
- **Verified by**: `tests/office-suite-real-samples.test.ts` (a real file of each format to every target the registry advertises, with a test that fails for a target without a conversion: the words of the office suite's PDF or CSV export of the same file read by pdftotext, phrases of each document pinned as literals, and the e-book outputs read back by calibre and by the MobileRead layout of a MOBI book), `tests/doc-refusals-real-samples.test.ts` (four Word documents of the regression corpus, compared with the words of their printed PDF and pinned phrases), `tests/doc-embedded-objects.test.ts` (an embedded Word object and a piece that runs past the main story, written with the independent Word builder and compared with the office suite's text), `tests/xps-reader.test.ts` (three real XPS files against MuPDF's text, reading order, gaps and right-to-left runs on packages written from the specification), `tests/office-suite-typed-failures.test.ts` (the missing-engine answer through the dispatcher, the crash and load-failure messages with the real stderr, the routing tables), a Linux real-world run of the 48 files of these formats on Ubuntu 24.04 (LibreOffice 24.2): 302 jobs before (111 converted, 33 refused, 158 crashes) and 301 after (281 converted, 20 refused, all of them RocketBook, no crash, hang or bad output; the real-world gate passes with the three known-failure entries of these rows removed), and the 47 Word documents of the regression-corpus trial that the reader refused: all convert to text now (39 in process, 8 through the office suite), with 99.97 % of the words of the office suite's text export on average and none below 95 %. For the follow-up row: four Lotus Word Pro files (the LibreOffice import test data that pass import) and the OpenDocument spreadsheet template are pinned in the real-world manifest; the suite reads each lwp file (exit code 0; 2, 1, 1 and 22 pages) and the words of its PDF are found in the 8-bit text of the file itself (92 %, 92 %, 80 % and 62 % of the words: the rest are words the file stores in pieces or other code pages), the pinned phrases of three files are bytes of the file; the opened pairs are checked on real files by `tests/office-suite-real-samples.test.ts` (a Word or PowerPoint 97-2003 output is a compound file with its `WordDocument` or `PowerPoint Document` stream and is read back by the suite, an RTF output is read back by the suite, a page-image output has one image for every page of the suite's PDF, the aspect ratio of that page and is not one flat colour), with the first page of the `wps` and `wpd` images also read by an OCR tool.
- **Revisit when**: the worker image carries an e-book converter or a LibreOffice with the RocketBook filter, an XPS renderer is approved, LibreOffice no longer crashes on the embedded-object report, or a registry format that only the office suite reads gets an in-process reader, or a real WPS Presentation file is published (route `dps` and open its pairs after the same checks). The in-process fallback for an OpenDocument template writes the package of the template for the `ods`, `odt` and `odp` targets (the suite writes a document); revisit it if the fallback is ever the main path.

## 41. Archive sources: package payloads, links, disk images, lzip and lzop

- **Status**: Implemented (issue #783, archive rows), decided 2026-10-17 (QA request in issue #783: every archive source the registry accepts converts its real files to the archive targets, or is refused with a typed answer that names a real limit of the file).
- **Decision**:
  - **A package converts to the files it installs.** An rpm holds a cpio and a deb a tar (`data.tar`), usually compressed. The conversion extracts that payload and packs its files; the wrapper stream is never the output. 7-Zip lists a package either as its files (an uncompressed cpio) or as one stream, so the stream is read as the archive it is, through the same listing, vetting and extraction as an upload of that archive. A layer 7-Zip leaves compressed is decompressed first (gzip, bzip2 and xz with 7-Zip, Zstandard in process, at most three layers), and the size and ratio caps are always measured against the package the caller uploaded, never against an inner layer. A selection of entries (`entries`) applies to the payload files and matches a name with or without the `./` the package tools write. A package that installs nothing converts to an empty archive. An rpm 7-Zip lists no entries for at all (the payload of rpm 4.12 and later with stripped cpio headers keeps its file names in the rpm header) is a typed `UnreadableArchiveError`, never an empty archive.
  - **Links in packages and disk images.** For `rpm`, `deb`, `dmg`, `img`, `iso` and `cpio` sources, symbolic links are left out of the conversion and reported in `skippedLinks`, unless the caller sets `skipLinks` explicitly (`false` keeps the refusal). Every real package and installer image has some, so refusing the archive refused nearly all of them. A link is never created in the output and never followed: the tree 7-Zip wrote is checked with `lstat` before anything is packed, and an entry that is written through a link the archive also holds is still refused. All other sources keep the opt-in `skipLinks`.
  - **Hard links keep their content.** A hard link between two files of the same archive (a cpio writes them for every package that has some, and a tar entry of type hard link names an earlier entry) is extracted, and each name is packed with the content, as the distribution unpackers install both names. The extraction accepts it only when the target is a regular entry of the same listing with a plain relative name, and the extracted tree is checked again: every name of an inode with several links must be inside the extraction directory, otherwise the archive is refused. Each name counts toward the entry-count, size and ratio caps, so many links to one large file cannot multiply it past them. A hard link to anything else is a link entry, left out and reported.
  - **Permission bits.** An archive may store a file or directory with no permission bits (the `shadow` files of a distribution's `setup` package). The extraction gives the owner read access to files and full access to directories, so the file can be packed and the directory removed. Setuid, setgid and sticky bits are always cleared: a converted archive extracted by a privileged user must not create a set-user-ID file. The mode in the output is the nine stored permission bits plus those owner bits.
  - **One budget, one deadline and at most three compression layers per package.** The bytes a package holds on disk at once (its own extraction, the layers being read, the extracted files and a selected copy) are charged against the bound a single-stream archive gets, and a layer that has been removed is credited back, so the peak counts and layers that each fit cannot add up past it; every layer is removed as soon as the layer inside it exists (an rpm's own extraction stays until its payload is known to be one); every 7-Zip step gets what is left of one deadline; and a payload still compressed after the third layer is refused without a fourth decode.
  - **lzip (`lz`) and lzop (`lzo`, `tar.lzo`, `tzo`) are read in process.** 7-Zip opens neither. The lzip reader locates the members from the sizes in their trailers and checks each member's CRC-32 and size; the lzop reader decodes LZO1X blocks (every method an lzop 1.x writes) and verifies the header, compressed-block and decompressed-block checksums the file declares. Both write into a buffer bounded by the archive size and ratio caps and refuse trailing data. They follow the published format descriptions only: no lzop or lzo source is used, and the `lzop` and `lzip` programs run only in tests, as the oracle.
  - **What stays refused, and why.** Each is an `UnreadableArchiveError` (HTTP 4xx) whose message names the limit. An archive that is one part of a multi-volume set (the listing says an entry continues in the next volume or began in the previous one) says which part it is and asks for all parts as one archive, because its data is incomplete. A classic HFS volume (the `BD` signature at byte 1024) says so, since 7-Zip reads HFS+, FAT, ext, NTFS and others but not that. A disk image 7-Zip cannot open at all says it holds no file system that can be read. An entry 7-Zip reports a data error or a failed checksum on is named in the message, and the archive is refused whole, as any damaged archive is. Output past 32 MiB that expands more than 100:1 stays refused under [10. Archive bomb limits](#10-archive-bomb-limits); a smaller one converts. A rar needs a 7-Zip build with the RAR decoders (the pinned upstream build of the worker image has them; the distribution package does not).
- **Why**:
  - Converting a package to another archive means converting what it installs. The unpackers of the distributions (`rpm2cpio` with `cpio`, `dpkg-deb -x`) give the file tree; the compressed stream, or an `ar` archive of a control and a data member, is an implementation detail no one asks a converter for. The reference service converts these formats with its archive tool to the same targets, and a result that is one `.cpio` file in a zip is not a conversion.
  - Links are part of what these archives are made of; what is unsafe is following one, writing through one, or recreating one on the user's machine. Leaving them out and saying so keeps the conversion useful and the extraction inside its directory. Hard links inside one archive are the only link kind whose target the listing names and the tree check proves, which is why only those keep their content.
  - lzip and lzop are small, fully specified formats; a decoder that checks every length and checksum is a few hundred lines, with no new dependency, and 7-Zip cannot read them.
- **Trade-offs**:
  - Symbolic links are lost in the converted archive (a `.app` bundle or a shared-library directory loses its version links). Preserving them in tar, zip and 7z would keep the layout, but an archive that holds a link followed by a file written through it is the standard way to write outside an extraction directory, and the converter would hand that archive on to its user.
  - A package's payload is on disk twice at the peak (the decompressed stream and the extracted tree being written from it), so a package whose payload is more than half the bound is refused although each layer alone is under it. A Zstandard payload is held in memory while it is decoded, within the same cap.
  - HFS+ images hold extended attributes and resource forks as alternate streams, which 7-Zip hands out as files named `<file>:<attribute>`; they are left out and counted in one warning ([56](#56-archive-rows-of-the-real-file-run-stripped-rpm-payloads-alternate-streams-named-volumes-and-methods-lzip-and-lzop-listings)).
  - The lzop reader refuses the multi-part and filter variants and the old header layouts (before version 0x0940) that no current lzop writes.
- **Alternatives rejected**:
  - **Converting the wrapper** (the cpio or the `ar` members) as before: the files are not reachable in the result.
  - **Keeping symbolic links in the output**: see the trade-off; a tar or zip with links is also not extractable on every platform.
  - **Following links inside the extraction directory**: a link to a link to a directory is the first step of every escape; no resolution is attempted.
  - **A cpio and tar reader in process** for the payloads: it would duplicate the vetting the 7-Zip pipeline already has and drop the hard-link and mode handling.
  - **Linking the `lzo` library or shelling out to `lzop`**: the library is GPL and the program a separate GPL tool; the format is simple enough to decode in a few hundred lines.
- **Verified by**: `tests/archive-package-sources.test.ts` (rpm and deb with xz, Zstandard, gzip and uncompressed payloads, a symbolic link and a hard link, built by rpmbuild and dpkg-deb, to every target; the installed files compared with what `rpm2cpio` with `cpio` and `dpkg-deb -x` install; empty packages; entry selection; a macOS disk image with links; hostile debs and a cpio with a traversal name, an absolute name, a device file, a write through a link, a hard link to a file outside the package and a ratio bomb; an rpm with stripped headers), `tests/archive-lzip-lzop-sources.test.ts` (files written by lzip and lzop, hand-assembled LZO1X blocks for every instruction, damaged and truncated files, the tools compressing generated data of every size and match distance, tarballs, bombs), `tests/archive-extraction-safety.test.ts` (hard-link acceptance and the tree check, owner access), `tests/archive-source-listing-keys.test.ts` (the worker route of the probe packages), `tests/registry-engine-conformance.test.ts` (the pairs of `lz`, `lzo`, `tar.lzo` and `tzo` are no longer inconclusive), and a Linux run of the 26 real-world files of these rows with the pinned 7-Zip: 20 convert to every target (four of the five rpm files, all four deb files, all three lzip files, both lzop files, both RAR files that are not a volume part, the two dmg files with links, all three CAB files), with the outputs compared file by file with `rpm2cpio` with `cpio`, `dpkg-deb`, `lzip`, `lzop`, `unrar` and, for the dmg files, the volume mounted by the disk utility; the other six stay typed refusals for the reasons above (the stripped rpm, the first part of a multi-volume rar, three classic HFS disk images, the HFS+ image with the data error), each now answered with a message that names the limit.
- **Revisit when**: users need symbolic links kept (the output would be a tar or 7z with links, for the caller to extract at their own risk), a reader for classic HFS is added, or the ratio floor of [10. Archive bomb limits](#10-archive-bomb-limits) changes.

## 42. Visio, CorelDRAW and metafile sources through the office suite, DXF page clips, and the formats with no reader

- **Status**: Implemented (issue #783, vector row), decided 2026-10-17 (QA request in issue #783: every format the registry accepts converts its real files, or is refused with a typed answer that names a real limit of the file).
- **Decision**:
  - **Visio (`vsd`), CorelDRAW (`cdr`), Windows metafiles (`emf`, `wmf`) and Computer Graphics Metafiles (`cgm`) are read by the office suite** (LibreOffice): Draw for the first four, Impress for CGM. It imports them with the Document Liberation libraries (libvisio, libcdr) and its own metafile filters, the libraries that Inkscape also reads Visio and CorelDRAW files with. They are office-suite-only sources like `pub` and `sxd` ([40](#40-formats-only-the-office-suite-reads-templates-refused-word-documents-and-xps-text)): a missing office suite is an unavailable engine (HTTP 503), and a file the importer cannot read or stops on is a typed refusal that names the format. EMF, WMF and CGM were output formats only; they are now sources too. A clear-text CGM (the `BEGMF` encoding) stays with the in-process parser, since the office suite imports the binary encoding only; the two are told apart by the first bytes.
  - **PDF is the one interchange.** The office suite writes the PDF (`draw_pdf_Export`, `impress_pdf_Export` for CGM); PNG, JPEG, SVG, EPS and PostScript come from that PDF through the existing page chain (Poppler renders the pages, `pdftocairo` writes the SVG, `pdftops` the PostScript and EPS). DXF, EMF and WMF come from the SVG that `pdftocairo` draws for each page, through the DXF writer and the metafile encoders. A drawing of several pages becomes a PDF with every page, one file per page in a ZIP, and one PostScript file with every page. The 14 pictures of `circles.cgm` are 14 pages, as the file's own BEGIN PICTURE elements count.
  - **Targets, by what real files convert to.** `vsd`: `pdf`, `png`, `svg`, `eps`, `ps`, `dxf`, `emf`, `wmf`. `cdr`: `pdf`, `png`, `svg`, `eps`, `ps`. `emf`, `wmf`, `cgm`: `pdf`, `png`, `jpg`, `svg`, `eps`, `ps`. DXF, EMF and WMF are withdrawn where a real file of the format cannot be written as vector geometry: the page of `pb-poi.cdr` holds a picture and the pages of the metafile samples hold a picture (`testEMF.emf`, `testWMF.wmf`, `computer_mail.emf`) or a clip region that is not the page (`sine_wave.emf`, every CGM file), and the writers refuse both rather than drop them. The raster formats of the reference list for EMF (AVIF, BMP, GIF, ICO, PSD, TIFF, WebP) are not offered yet.
  - **A clip path that is one axis-aligned rectangle covering the page clips nothing and is dropped; every other clip stays refused.** The page of an office-suite PDF is clipped to its own size, and Poppler writes that as a `clip-path` in the SVG, which made every Draw page unwritable as DXF or metafile. The geometry reader accepts a clip whose single `<rect>` or short `<path>` child (at most 512 characters) is four axis-aligned corners that, drawn under the current transform, reach within one point (96/72 device pixels) of every side of the page. Poppler's clip for a LibreOffice page falls short of the page by 0.59 to 0.76 point (right edge 0.76, bottom 0.59, top 0.46, left none, measured on the real samples), which is 1.01 pixel in the SVG of Poppler 26 (sized in points) and 0.76 in that of Poppler 24 (sized in pixels). A smaller rectangle, a rectangle short on one side by more than the point, a rounded one, a curve, several shapes, a clip or shape with a `transform`, `style`, `class`, `clip-path` or `clipPathUnits` attribute, a clip on the root element and any reference to a missing clip path are refused as before. The shape of a clip is read once per clip id however many elements refer to it, and charged once to the vertex budget, so a page of many elements under one clip costs no more than the elements.
  - **EMF and WMF from these sources** are the in-process encoders' output for the SVG page; the PDF chain accepts them as targets only for these sources (the registry offers them for no other PDF source).
  - **The text `<svg` in the bytes of a file named `.vsd`, `.cdr`, `.emf`, `.wmf` or `.cgm` no longer makes it convertible.** The in-process engine used to cut an embedded SVG out of any file of these names and convert that; a real file of these formats does not hold its drawing as SVG, so every real file was refused while a renamed SVG converted. The signature check still refuses a renamed SVG at the door.
  - **Each of these five sources must open with the signature of its own format before it reaches the office suite**, because the suite sniffs the content and would open a Word document or a page of HTML under any of these names as what it is. EMF: header record type 1 and ` EMF` at byte 40. WMF: the Aldus placeable key, or the standard header of nine words. CGM: the binary BEGIN METAFILE command (a first 16-bit word of 0x0020 to 0x003F) or the clear-text `BEGMF` command, which the in-process parser reads. Visio: a compound file whose directory names the `VisioDocument` stream (a file that fills the whole 64 KiB header window without the name in it is taken on its container signature, since its directory may lie beyond). CorelDRAW: a RIFF file whose form type starts with `CDR` or `cdr`, or a ZIP package that holds `content/riffData.cdr` or `content/root.dat` (libcdr's own test), where a package that runs past the bytes read counts when its first entry is in a CorelDRAW folder (`content/`, `metadata/`, `color/`). Forcing the import filter by name for these sources (`--infilter`) is left to the row that does it for every office source (#761).
  - **DWF, Sketch (`sk`) and sK1 (`sk1`) stay refused, with the reason.** The worker includes no reader of DWF 6 (W2D) or DWFx files, Sketch files or sK1 files (the office suite imports none of them). The only open-source DWF reader found, an MIT-licensed W2D and DWFx parser that writes SVG, is a pre-alpha first released in 2026; it is evaluated on the real samples as a separate change before the worker relies on it. The vector editor dropped its Sketch and sK1 import in its 1.0 release, and the remaining converter of those formats is packaged for an end-of-life language runtime and has had no release since 2021. Every target answers with a typed refusal that says so, never with a drawing synthesised from a placeholder. The registry keeps the formats and their targets, so the refusal is what a caller sees. The `<svg` cut-out that `sk` and `sk1` had accepted any file through is gone.
  - **The worker image carries the Draw application** (`libreoffice-draw-nogui`); the image had Writer, Calc and Impress only.
- **Why it is right in practice**: the reference service converts Visio and CorelDRAW files with a vector editor that reads them through libvisio and libcdr, and the metafiles with the same editor, so the same libraries decide what a drawing looks like, and LibreOffice's own test data holds the files that exercise them. LibreOffice and its page chain are already in the worker, with the sandbox, the limits and the typed failures of [40](#40-formats-only-the-office-suite-reads-templates-refused-word-documents-and-xps-text); a second engine would add a process, a licence and an image layer for the same import code. One interchange keeps one rendering path: the PNG, SVG, PostScript and DXF of a drawing show what its PDF shows. Dropping a clip that clips nothing loses no geometry, which is why it is safe where dropping any other clip is not.
- **Trade-offs**: what the importers do not draw is not drawn. Text set on a path stays straight and larger than in CorelDRAW (the badge `pb-poi.cdr` keeps its words but not their curve), and the objects of the right column of `fdo65220-2.cdr` are missing from the render while CorelDRAW's own page thumbnail in the file shows them; the reference tool shows the same page, because it reads the file with the same library. A Visio file converts every page (the reference tool converts the first). The page of a Visio drawing is the page size the file declares. A metafile is a small picture: the office suite puts it on an A4 page (or on the 793.7 x 446.5 pt page of a CGM) and the reference tool crops the page to the drawing, so a converted EMF or WMF has white margins the reference output does not. SVG text, and the text of a DXF or a metafile, is glyph outlines, because it comes from the PDF. The page clip tolerance of one point would also drop a clip that cuts a sliver of the page edge that wide; an office-suite page has nothing drawn there. A conversion costs one office-suite run (the targets of each file took 1 to 3 s in total on a macOS developer machine; no Linux run was made). A `.wmf` file with a picture and a `.cdr` file with a picture convert to PDF, images, SVG and PostScript but not to DXF, EMF or WMF; the registry offers those targets only for `vsd`, where all four real files convert.
- **Alternatives rejected**:
  - **Inkscape in the worker** (the reference tool): GPL-3.0, a large GTK and Python distribution for importers that LibreOffice already carries. It runs only as a test oracle on CI and on a developer machine, as a separate program; it reads no CGM and two of the three EMF files.
  - **In-process Visio, CorelDRAW and metafile readers**: large, partly undocumented binary formats; the libraries took years to cover them, and a partial reader would draw some files wrong and call it done.
  - **Draw's own PNG and SVG export** (`draw_png_Export`, `draw_svg_Export`): the PNG covers the first page only and neither shares the PDF rendering path; the page chain handles every page and every target the same way.
  - **Clipping the geometry by the clip rectangle** for the pages the writers refuse: it would unlock the metafile pages with a clip region, but needs a polygon clipper in the geometry reader; recorded as a follow-up, not decided here.
  - **Keeping the SVG cut-out for files that hold the text `<svg`**: it answers with a drawing for a file that is not of the format.
  - **Withdrawing DWF, Sketch and sK1**: the reference service offers them, and a typed answer that names the missing reader tells the caller what is missing.
- **Verified by**: `tests/vector-sources-real-samples.test.ts` (four Visio, two CorelDRAW, three EMF, three WMF and three CGM files of the real-world corpus converted to every target the registry offers: the page count and size read by pdfinfo and pinned, the pictures of the PDF against the bitmap records that the EMF and WMF files themselves hold and the pictures that a CGM file declares by its own BEGIN PICTURE elements, phrases pinned as literals and read by pdftotext, the PNG, JPEG, SVG and PostScript pages compared with the PDF page by SSIM with ghostscript drawing the PostScript, the DXF read by ezdxf and its extents holding every inked pixel of the page, the EMF and WMF drawn again by the office suite, every letter of Inkscape's PDF of the same file in the converter's PDF and the first pages alike, and the colours of the CorelDRAW file with no text compared with the page thumbnail CorelDRAW stored in the file; a renamed SVG refused for all five sources; `sk` and `sk1` refused; one fact per sample that no phrase or second reader judges, read from the records of the file itself (the rounded rectangle of a WMF record against the ink, the background colour of a binary CGM, the pictures of a CGM that draw something, the characters of a CGM's text);
