- **Revisit when**: the compression ratio allowance changes or lbzip2 matches `bzip2 -9`'s block layout on run-heavy data (raise the bound, to every size); the CI runner rows still fall below 1.0 with the pinned 7-Zip (then profile on x64, where the nightly `xml` xz row was 24 ms behind); `unshare` gets cheaper or a persistent sandbox is adopted (the start is then the first thing left to remove from the xz and 7z rows); or `lbzip2` stops being available for the worker's base image (return the small tarballs to 7-Zip).

## 51. HTML to PDF: what a page names that the PDF cannot carry

- **Status**: Implemented (HTML to PDF rows of issue #783, epic #760), decided 2026-10-19 (QA request); extended 2026-10-20 (QA request) to tables inside table cells and rows taller than a page on the in-process route.
- **Decision**:
  - **A page is converted, not refused, for what it names and the PDF cannot carry.** The staging for LibreOffice writes only three kinds of reference, each checked as it is written: an `<a href>` that is `http`, `https`, `mailto` or a `#fragment`; an `<img src>` that is base64 PNG, JPEG or GIF data that decodes (after the loader of entry 6 has embedded the images the page names); and CSS that names embedded raster images or fragments only. Nothing else is written, so nothing else can reach the renderer, and nothing is fetched for it.
  - **Links that go elsewhere lose their target and keep their text.** A relative link, a `javascript:`, `file:`, `ftp:` or protocol-relative one, and a `data:` link is not a link in the PDF. A relative link has no document to be relative to, and a `file:` link in a PDF would point into the reader's disk.
  - **Style sheets, backgrounds and CSS pictures that name a file or an address are left out.** That is a `<link rel="stylesheet">`, a `background` attribute, and a `<style>` element or `style` attribute with a `url()` or `@import` that is not an embedded raster image: the element or attribute is not written at all, because the part of the CSS that is safe cannot be told from the rest without a full CSS parser. Scripts, icons, `<base>`, `<meta>` and form targets render nothing and are dropped without a warning, as before.
  - **Content LibreOffice would not draw here is left out whole.** That is SVG, canvas, video, audio, form controls (`input` other than hidden, `select`, `textarea`), frames and framesets with a source, objects, embeds and applets, each with its fallback content. A page that is only a frameset becomes a valid one-page PDF without text.
  - **The in-process renderer (no LibreOffice, or an orientation requested) leaves out the same elements with the same warning.** It no longer answers 503 for form controls, frames, framesets, SVG, canvas, media, objects, embeds and applets. One definition decides what is left out (`isUndrawable` in `html-omitted-resources.ts`), and both routes use it.
  - **The in-process renderer draws a table inside a table cell.** A cell holds text, pictures and tables in order; the inner table is laid out in the width of the cell, by the same rules, and may hold tables of its own down to 32 levels. A page nested deeper is refused with the limit named (`HTML tables nested more than 32 levels deep cannot be drawn ...`), as is a table whose columns cannot all be at least 24 pt wide in the width left, counting the tables inside it (`a table with 4 columns and the tables inside it need 768pt but the page width is 741pt`). The 450 saved pages of the public government corpus nest tables up to 10 deep (one page holds 145 tables).
  - **A table row taller than a page continues on the next page.** A row that fits a page is moved to the next page whole, as before (a break between rows is preferred). A row taller than a page, or a group of rows tied by a merged cell that is, is cut where the page ends: each cell goes on from its next line, with its border and its padding drawn again on every page, and the row after it starts below the last piece. A picture is not cut: it is scaled to fit the page between its margins, as a picture outside a table already is.
  - **Merged cells (`rowspan`) are placed as the HTML table model places them.** The in-process renderer used to give a cell that merges downward one grid position only, so the cells below it shifted left under it. A cell now covers its columns in the rows below, a `rowspan` is cut at the last row (`rowspan="0"` spans every row left, 65,534 is the most), and a merged cell and the rows under it stay on one page unless they are taller than one.
  - **A table that holds a table, and a table inside one, takes its column widths from its content; other tables keep equal columns.** Each column has a least width (24 pt, or what the tables inside it need), a minimum (its widest word, at most 96 pt, since a longer word is broken) and a maximum (its longest line); the width of the table is shared as CSS 2.1 section 17.5.2.2 describes for automatic layout: the maximums grown to fill the width when they fit, otherwise the minimums plus a share of what is left in proportion to how much more each column wants, and when not even the minimums fit, the least widths plus a share. A picture asks for no minimum (it is scaled to its column).
  - **`<xmp>` is drawn as preformatted text**, its content literal, as the HTML rendering rules ask. MathML is still refused by name.
  - **Nothing is fetched or reported for a subtree no route draws.** The image loader skips the content of `script`, `noscript`, `template`, `noembed` and `noframes` and of every element left out above, so a picture in a `<noscript>` fallback or inside an `<object>` costs no request and gets no "left out the image" line. Both renderers and the loader build their lists from the one set of never-drawn elements, so they cannot drift.
  - **Two warnings report it, after the image warnings of entry 6.** `Left out elements that cannot be drawn (N): <input> x 31, <select> x 2.` counts the elements by tag. `Left out references that are not loaded or followed (N): "other.html" (<a href>), "style.css" (<link href>), ...` names each distinct reference and where the page held it, up to 10, then `and K more`.
  - **`requireResources` is about images only.** A page with a link, style sheet or form that the PDF cannot carry is converted with the warnings, with or without it; an image that cannot be loaded is still a 400.
  - **Data that claims to be something it is not is still a 400**: image data that does not decode or is no PNG, JPEG or GIF (in `<img>` and in CSS), CSS with a `data:` URI that is no image, and CSS that cannot be vouched for (escapes outside comments, `<`, characters outside ASCII and strings, a character reference in a style sheet). A backslash inside a CSS comment is no escape (the comment is replaced by a space before LibreOffice sees it), so a sheet with the comment form of the "backslash hack" is read like any other.
- **Why**:
  - **A saved page almost always names something it cannot have.** Of the 97 HTML pages of the real-world sample that go to PDF, 82 were refused, each for the first thing it named, and the same pages converted to Word, ODF, Markdown and the ebook formats. Measured by what the 97 pages hold (a page holds several): 73 a link that is not kept, 48 a style sheet by address, 42 a script by address, 37 a form control, 20 a background picture, 19 an image-map link, 10 CSS with a picture or import by address, 4 an object, embed or applet, 2 a frameset. After the change all 97 convert on macOS with image loading on and off, and 96 on Linux with LibreOffice 24.2: the one page that is left (an API reference of 145 tables) sends LibreOffice's HTML layout into a loop at a page break, with or without this staging (the original file does it too), the worker stops it at its timeout, and the in-process renderer, which could not draw nested tables then, converts that page since the extension below.
  - **Converters that print a page do not stop for a resource they cannot fetch.** A browser prints a page whose style sheet or script is missing, and an office suite opens a page with broken links; the page is shown without what is missing. A refusal that quotes the first reference blocks the user without telling them that the page can be converted without it.
  - **A warning tells the caller what the PDF lacks**, as entry 9 asks, in the two kinds of loss a reader can act on: elements that are not drawn and references that are not followed.
  - **The renderer's input stays the checked tree.** Making the checks part of the write (and not a pass over the whole tree before it) removes the refusals for references in elements that are not written anyway (scripts, templates, SVG, frames), and leaves the three places a reference can reach unchanged.
  - **Tables inside tables are how saved pages are laid out.** On the in-process route (no office suite, or an orientation asked for) 224 of the 450 saved pages were refused and 226 converted: 206 were refused for a table in a table cell and 18 for a row taller than a page (the first reason met). Now 447 convert. The 3 left are two pages with an `<img>` that has no `src` (a typed 400 that predates this) and one page whose tables need 768 pt of a 741 pt text width. The page counts of the pages that converted before are unchanged.
  - **The text of the page is kept, in the order of its cells.** Read back with `pdftotext`, the 447 converted pages hold 9,977,941 letters and digits of the text parse5 finds in them outside what is left out on purpose (elements with `display: none` or `hidden`, `select`, `textarea`, `button`, `object`, `applet`, `embed`, media, frames and SVG), and none is missing. A word in a column too narrow for it is broken, as the renderer already broke long words. Where a row is cut, the cells go on page by page, so each cell's lines read in order but the cells alternate by page.
  - **A row taller than a page is cut, not scaled or clipped.** CSS Fragmentation Level 3 (section 4.4) allows a break inside a table row when no break between rows fits, and lets a layout slice only content that has no break point of its own; the open page-layout engine whose table code was read lays out each cell with the room left and resumes it on the next page from a resume point kept per cell, and gives a first row that is taller than the page the whole page rather than dropping it. The office suite (26.8, measured) does the same: a contents column beside a 5-page article (corpus page `govdocs1-000057`, 1,913 words) is split at line boundaries over 5 pages with every word present and the cell background drawn on each page, and a cell of 400 lines gives 8 pages; a 3,000 px picture in a cell is scaled to the page. The in-process route in portrait gives 5, 3 and 22 pages for corpus pages `govdocs1-000057`, `-000440` and `-000726`, as the office suite does, 4 for `-000060` as it does, and 3 for `-000070` where it gives 4 and loses 2,439 of the 5,732 letters and digits of the page.
  - **Equal columns cannot hold a layout.** Nesting two columns halves the width each time; with equal columns inside tables, 45 of the 450 pages were still refused as too narrow (2 to 14 columns in a cell of a few dozen points). Content widths remove them, and fit what CSS 2.1 prescribes; only tables with a table inside, or inside one, change, so a table of data keeps its look.
- **Trade-offs**:
  - **A form is not drawn.** The labels and text around the controls stay; the controls and their values do not. The other HTML targets do not draw them either.
  - **A sheet or style attribute with one reference loses all its rules.** Dropping a whole `<style>` element for one `background: url(...)` is a visible loss on a page that is styled by it. The alternative needs a CSS parser that can cut one declaration out, which is a change of its own, and a half-checked sheet is the risk the checks exist to avoid.
  - **Relative links die.** The page's own address is not known, so they cannot be resolved; a page that sets an absolute `<base href>` still has its relative images loaded (entry 6), but its relative links are left out.
  - **The in-process renderer still refuses what it would have to guess at.** MathML has no drawing there and answers 503 with the element named; so do an image inside a preformatted block, an image that is not PNG or JPEG, a table nested deeper than 32 levels, and a table that cannot be set in the width left (24 pt per column at the least); an `<img>` with no `src` is a typed 400. A table is as wide as its text column: a layout wider than the page is squeezed, not cut off or shrunk.
  - **A table is not shrunk to its content.** Tables fill the width they are given, as before, with or without a table inside: a browser would make an automatic-width table as wide as its content when that is narrower. Filling keeps the borders aligned with the page and with the pages that converted before.
  - **A cut row costs lines at the page end.** A cell that has a picture or a nested row next in line that does not fit leaves the room under it empty on that page; and the lines of a cut cell are set by the text shaper (the same line breaking as complex scripts), while a cell that fits a page keeps the writer it always had, so the line breaks of a cut cell can differ from the same text in a cell that is not cut.
  - **Merged cells now move text.** 9 of the 226 pages that converted before use `rowspan` in a way that moved cells (100 of the 450 pages have a `rowspan`): they are laid out in the right columns where their cells were shifted, with the same text and the same page counts. The other 217 are byte for byte what they were.
  - **Work is bounded by the input.** A cell is measured once, at its width (no layout is repeated per level); the column widths of a table are measured once and kept; a merged cell is cut at the rows that exist; a table is refused at 24 pt per column before any row is placed, and a table with merged cells above 1,000 columns before its grid is made; of tables nested one in another, 34 are refused by the depth limit, 60 by the width their least widths need, and 400 by the parser (elements deeper than 256 levels), each in under 10 ms; a table of 20,000 rows takes 1.3 s against 1.2 s before, and 2,000 rows tied by one merged cell convert in 0.3 s.
- **Alternatives rejected**:
  - **Keeping the 400** (the previous behaviour): it made 85% of the sampled pages unconvertible to PDF for references that could not have been followed anyway.
  - **Fetching style sheets, scripts or frames**: gives a full browser engine's attack surface to a document converter, for styles that LibreOffice reads only in part. Entry 6's revisit condition stays.
  - **Resolving relative links against the document's `<base>`**: a link in a PDF that points to a remote site chosen by the page, only because of a `<base>`, adds a second place that chooses a link target for little gain.
  - **Writing form controls as LibreOffice form fields**: an interactive field in a PDF is a different document, and the controls do not carry values a reader can use.
  - **One warning per reference**: a page with 100 links would drown the others; entry 9 caps warnings at 50 lines plus a count.
  - **Scaling a tall row to fit one page**: a row of five pages of text would shrink to a fifth of its size, below reading size; the office suite does not do it for text either.
  - **Clipping a tall row at the page end**: it loses text, which the other targets (and the office suite) keep.
  - **Handing every page with nested tables to the office suite**: the in-process renderer is the route when the office suite is absent or an orientation is asked for, and it answered 503 for 224 of 450 pages; one page of the corpus (145 tables) sends the office suite's HTML layout into a loop at a page break, so a second route that works is the only answer for it.
  - **Equal columns inside nested tables**: 45 of 450 pages refused as too narrow; see above.
  - **Shrink-to-fit tables (width of the content)**: changes the look of pages that already convert, for no gain in the text kept.
- **Verified by**: `tests/html-pdf-inprocess-omissions.test.ts` (a page with a form, a frame and inline SVG, a page that is only a frameset and a landscape request converted on the in-process route, read by `pdftotext` and `pdfinfo` with the warning lines exact; MathML still refused with the element named; a local server counts the requests of the loader for images inside never-drawn subtrees on both routes: one request, for the visible picture); `tests/html-pdf-external-resources.test.ts` (real pages of the corpus and pages derived from them, through real LibreOffice, read by `pdftotext`, `pdfinfo`, `pdfimages` and the link annotations of the PDF; the renderer's input with `file:`, relative-traversal, `javascript:`, protocol-relative and `data:` links, pictures and style sheets; a private-address image; `HTML_IMAGE_FETCH=off`; `requireResources`); `tests/html-native-staging.test.ts` (the bypass matrix still ends in a refusal or a document without the reference, the CSS refusals above, the elements left out); `tests/html-external-images*.test.ts`; and the real-world gate, where the 97 HTML pages to PDF were 15 converted and 82 refused and are now 96 or 97 converted (above). The 15 pages that converted before are staged byte for byte as they were. `tests/html-pdf-nested-tables.test.ts` (tables nested in a cell, merged cells in a table and in a nested table, content widths, the depth limit at 33 and 34 tables and a nesting of 400, a thousand-column cell, 65,534 and 0 as `rowspan`, two thousand rows tied by one cell, `<xmp>`; a row of three pages cut line by line with the rectangles of its two cells on every page read by pdf-lib, a row cut inside a nested table, a picture of 3,000 px scaled to the page; eight corpus pages with the letters and digits of parse5's reading of the page found in the PDF; the positions come from `pdftotext -bbox-layout`, the page counts from `pdfinfo` and the size of the picture from `pdfimages -list`). Over the 450 corpus pages with `orientation`: 226 converted before and 447 after, 0 converted before and refused after, 217 of the 226 byte for byte (after qpdf normalisation) and the other 9 with `rowspan` moved cells and kept their page counts, 0 letters or digits missing from the 447 (outside what is left out on purpose); the document parity family (quality, Linux) has 302 rows on both sides and none moved.
- **Revisit when**: callers ask for the controls of a form to be drawn, for style sheets by address, or for links resolved against a base; or the in-process renderer is the route most pages take, or a page needs a table wider than its text column to be drawn at its width (a landscape page for the table, or a scale), or the nesting limit of 32 or the 24 pt column floor refuses a page that callers need.

## 52. Picture rows left by the real-file run: DX10 DDS surfaces, TIFF directories out of order, libheif and ImageMagick limits of the CI runner

- **Status**: Implemented (issue #783, image rows), decided 2026-10-19 (QA request in issue #783: a real file converts, or is refused with a typed answer that names a real limit of the file; found by the nightly real-file run 39107567130).
- **Decision**:
  - **DDS files whose pixel format is an uncompressed 8-bit four-channel DXGI format are read through the legacy A8R8G8B8 pixel format.** `DXGI_FORMAT_R8G8B8A8_TYPELESS`, `_UNORM`, `_UNORM_SRGB` and `B8G8R8A8_TYPELESS`, `_UNORM`, `_UNORM_SRGB` in a two-dimensional texture lose their 20-byte DX10 header; the header gets the A8R8G8B8 masks (red `0x00ff0000`, green `0x0000ff00`, blue `0x000000ff`, alpha `0xff000000`), and red and blue are exchanged in the bytes of the red-first formats. The rewrite runs in process on the bytes before ImageMagick reads them under the same policy; the policy file is not changed (`DDS` was already an allowed coder). The sRGB variants name how the values are meant to be read; they are not converted. BC1 to BC7, volume textures and every other DXGI format are passed on as they are.
  - **A TIFF directory is put in order before the image library reads it.** The entries of every directory are sorted by tag (stable) and an entry whose 12 bytes repeat the entry before it is dropped; the rest of the file is not touched. The warning level of the decoder stays at "warning is a failure": a repeat with another value, a missing strip, a cut file and every other warning or error still refuse the file. Only classic TIFF (magic 42) is read; BigTIFF and bytes that are no TIFF pass unchanged. The walk over the directory chain stops at a directory it has read, and when the directories read would take more bytes than the file has (a loop, or directories that start a few bytes apart and each claim 65535 entries, took 69 s for a 768 kB file when only the directory count was bounded); a directory whose tags already rise is passed without allocating.
  - **HEIF `mif3` and uncompressed images with row-aligned tiles get no code change.** The worker's ImageMagick 7 and libheif (Debian 13: libheif 1.23.4, ImageMagick 7.1.1-43) read both and give the pixels libheif's own decoder writes. The CI runner's libheif 1.17 does not read them, and its answer stays the typed refusal. The same holds for the BC7 DDS file (ImageMagick 6 on the runner has no BC7 reader; ImageMagick 7.1.1 reads it). `bench/realworld/known-refusals.json` lists these three files and `pil-01r_00.pcx` with the reason, and the corpus report shows their refusals in a table of their own ("Known refusals, explained, not gating") instead of among the unexplained ones. The gate does not change: the refusals still count in the refusal rate of their pair.
  - **`pil-01r_00.pcx` stays a typed refusal.** Its header gives 8 bits, 1 plane and 12 bytes per line for a window 88 pixels wide, and the run-length stream ends before 88 x 52 samples exist. Pillow reports the file as truncated, ImageMagick 6 and 7 report an unexpected end of file. It is a buffer-overrun test file of Pillow's; it is refused and not guessed at.
- **Why**:
  - **Measured on Debian 13 (arm64) with the packages the worker image installs, and on the CI runner's ImageMagick 6.9.12 unpacked from the Ubuntu 24.04 archive** (`pil-argb-32bpp_mipmaps-1.dds` is a DX10 R8G8B8A8_UNORM file by its header, whatever its name says):

    | File | ImageMagick 7.1.1-43 before | ImageMagick 6.9.12 before | After (both) |
    |---|---|---|---|
    | `pil-dxgi_format_r8g8b8a8_unorm_srgb.dds` | refused ("image type not supported") | refused | the pixels Pillow decodes |
    | `pil-argb-32bpp_mipmaps-1.dds` | the pixels Pillow decodes | refused | the pixels Pillow decodes |
    | `pil-dxgi_format_bc7_unorm_srgb.dds` | the pixels Pillow decodes | refused | unchanged (BC7 needs ImageMagick 7) |
    | `libheif-lightning_mini.heif`, `libheif-uncompressed_comp_rgb_tiled_row_tile_align.heif` | equal to `heif-dec` of libheif 1.23.4 (difference 0) | not applicable (libheif 1.17 on the runner refuses) | unchanged |

    The bundled image library reads none of the DDS, PCX and HEIF files (unsupported format, or a libheif without the HEVC and uncompressed decoders).
  - **Why red and blue are exchanged instead of describing the red-first bytes by masks.** The same legacy header with red-first masks gave the right picture on ImageMagick 7.1.1-43 and a picture with red and blue exchanged on 7.1.2-29, so the masks are not read alike across releases; the A8R8G8B8 layout is read alike by 6.9.12, 7.1.1 and 7.1.2 (the existing hand-written A8R8G8B8 file test passes on all three). The worker image takes the distribution's ImageMagick, so a security update must not change a picture.
  - **Why the TIFF file is repaired and the fail-on level is not lowered.** `wc-psalm-44.tiff` carries the Orientation tag twice (both entries say 1) in its first directory. libtiff warns "tags are not sorted in ascending order" for any entry that is not above the one before it, a repeat included (`TIFFReadDirectoryCheckOrder`). The image library cannot tell one warning from another, so lowering its fail-on level to "error" would let every warning through (a strip past the end of the file is one). Putting the directory in order removes this warning alone, costs a copy of the file only when a directory needs it, and leaves the decision on every other warning where it was. Pillow reads the file, and the pixels its colour transform gives from the embedded profile equal the converted pixels to within one level.
  - **Why the CI-runner rows are listed and not fixed.** The nightly judges files with the tools of Ubuntu 24.04; the worker image runs Debian 13. The Ubuntu 24.04 archive has no ImageMagick 7 (checked in its package index) and carries libheif 1.17, and building them in the job would make the nightly judge a binary that neither ships nor is the distribution's. Judging the corpus in a Debian 13 container would fix all four rows at once but changes every job of the nightly (the image, the cache, the oracles' versions) for four files. The list is one file of four entries and one table in the report; the tests state the same split: where the installed libheif or ImageMagick reads the file the conversion has to equal the independent decode, where it cannot the answer has to be the typed refusal.
- **Trade-offs**:
  - **A copy of the DDS surface** (and a byte exchange for red-first formats) for DX10 files: the file is held in memory once more, for 4 bytes per pixel up to the input pixel limit.
  - **A TIFF whose directory is repeated with another value is still refused**, as is any other file libtiff warns about; a viewer that guesses would open some of them.
  - **The known-refusals list can go stale**: an entry for a file that converts on the runner is not noticed by the report. The test checks that every entry names a corpus file; it does not check that the refusal still happens.
  - **HEIF and BC7 files of the corpus stay refused in the nightly** until the runner's tools change; the worker is covered by the tests that run on any machine whose libheif and ImageMagick read them.
- **Alternatives rejected**:
  - **A decoder of the uncompressed DDS formats in the code base**: the header rewrite reuses the coder under its policy and its limits; a second reader of the same bytes is more code to keep right.
  - **Describing red-first bytes by masks**: read in opposite orders by two ImageMagick 7.1 releases (measured).
  - **Lowering the TIFF fail-on level, or an allow-list of warning texts**: the warning text cannot be seen from the image library, and a lower level hides warnings that mean a damaged file.
  - **Dropping every repeated tag, or keeping the last**: which of two different values is meant is not known.
  - **Reading `pil-01r_00.pcx` with the byte count of the row recomputed from the width**: the stream is shorter than any such reading needs; the part decoded would be an invention.
  - **A Debian 13 container for the nightly, or a source build of ImageMagick 7 in the job**: see the reason above; revisit below.
- **Verified by**: `tests/image-dds-dx10.test.ts` (the R8G8B8A8_UNORM_SRGB and R8G8B8A8_UNORM files equal to the SHA-256 of the pixels Pillow 11.1.0 decodes, derived files with the DXGI format changed to TYPELESS and UNORM_SRGB, and with red and blue exchanged for the B8G8R8A8 formats, the rewritten header field by field, files that stay as they are, BC7 either converting to Pillow's pixels or refused with the typed answer by what the installed ImageMagick reads), run on ImageMagick 7.1.1-43, 7.1.2-29 and 6.9.12; `tests/image-tiff-tag-order.test.ts` (both pages of the real file against Pillow, the first page through its embedded profile within one level, all targets, a directory in descending order, an identical repeat, a conflicting repeat still refused, a cut file still refused, the same bytes returned when nothing needs repair); `tests/image-old-style-jpeg-tiff.test.ts` (the directory reader after it moved); `tests/image-heif-decode.test.ts` (the two libheif files equal to libheif's decoder where it reads them, the typed refusal where it does not); `tests/image-external-decode.test.ts` (the PCX file refused with the facts of its header); `tests/realworld-corpus.test.ts` (known refusals explained, listed files exist in the manifest).
- **Revisit when**: the CI runner's image gains ImageMagick 7 or libheif 1.19 (remove the four entries and run the tests that now take the converting branch); the nightly moves to a Debian 13 container; a real file with a repeated TIFF tag of a different value or a BigTIFF with out-of-order tags is reported; or a DXGI format outside the six (packed 16-bit, BC6H) turns up in files users send.

## 53. PDF page trees that loop, and page deletion inside the document

- **Status**: Implemented (PDF operation rows of issue #783, epic #760), decided 2026-10-19 (QA request).
- **Decision**:
  - **A page tree that lists one of its own nodes again is mended before a PDF is compressed.** When the PDF page tool cannot list the pages of the document, the product reads its object graph and removes every `/Kids` entry that names a `/Pages` node already placed in the tree (a node that lists itself, a ring of nodes, a node two parents list: the first parent in reading order keeps it). A `/Pages` node left without a page is dropped from its parent, and the `/Count` of every node whose pages changed is the number of pages below it. The pages, their content and every other object are untouched: the PDF tool writes the change as an update of the damaged objects. A document it lists, or a tree with nothing to mend, is not rewritten.
  - **The mended document is what the profile works on.** Every profile (`web`, `print`, `archive`, `max`) starts from it, so the page of a looping file is rewritten like any page and the result passes `qpdf --check`. When the profile gains nothing, the mended document is returned (`optimized: false`), not the damaged input.
  - **Watermark, merge, protection and unlock mend a looping tree too.** Watermark and merge read the document in the process, and a tree that loops ends there in a stack overflow of the parser: only after that failure is the tree mended and the document read again (a document the parser reads takes no extra process; a tree that is merely too deep is not changed by the mend, so the first failure stays). Merge mends each input on its own, after that input's own failure, so a looping file next to healthy ones keeps all its pages in the order given. Protection and unlock do not fail on a loop (the PDF tool encrypts and decrypts without reading the pages), so there is nothing to wait for: the tree is mended on the decrypted bytes, before protection encrypts them again and after unlock decrypts them, so no plain copy is written where protection was asked for. Unlock mends only a document it decrypted; an unencrypted file is still returned as it is.
  - **The page operations mend a looping tree too, after the PDF tool refuses it.** Extract, delete, reorder, rotate and split run as before; only when the run fails with `Loop detected in /Pages structure` is the tree mended the same way and the run repeated once on the mended document. A document the tool reads takes no extra process, and its output is byte for byte what it was. A loop that cannot be mended (an export past its limit) keeps the tool's refusal. The edit gate (`openPdfForEditing`) is not the place: a probe there would cost every operation a process to learn what a failed run already says. The mend lives in one function (`withMendedPageTree`) that every operation but protection and unlock calls with its own test for the refusal.
  - **The walk is bounded.** A node is placed once, when the first node that lists it is read, so the work is the number of objects in the file however the references loop (a mesh in which 300 nodes list each other, or a 50,000-node chain, is read once per node), and the walk keeps its own stack, so a deep chain cannot exhaust the process stack. A tree deeper than the PDF tool's own nesting limit stays a refusal of that tool.
  - **Deleting pages leaves the document where it is.** `qpdf --pages . 1-z,x<N>` removes the pages from the document itself, as extract and reorder already do: the catalog entries (language, open action, outlines, names), the form and the structure of the pages that stay are kept, and the form fields and objects that belong only to the deleted pages go with them. The earlier route, a selection written through `--split-pages`, copied the pages into a new document: it lost the catalog entries and copied a damaged form again for every field. The page count is read beside the run, and a specification that covers every page is still refused.
- **Why**:
  - **The reference engines hide the loop by losing the page.** For `pdf-qpdf-pages-loop.pdf` and `pdf-pdfjs-Pages-tree-refs.pdf` the PDF page tool reports `Loop detected in /Pages structure`, and Ghostscript's pdfwrite, the reference for compression, cannot reach the page (`Couldn't get page info`, `page not found`) and writes a blank page, which passes the structure check by having nothing in it. The product's lossless rewrite kept the page and the loop, and failed the check (1 failure against 0). A document that keeps its text and passes the check is better than either, and the text is the part of the file a user needs.
  - **Mending beats stopping at the first loop.** Poppler and PDF.js read these files: the page is there and only one edge of the tree is wrong, so the repair removes that edge and nothing else. Giving the Ghostscript step the mended document also keeps it from replacing a larger damaged file by a smaller one with blank pages, which the size check would take for a gain.
  - **Delete was 3.7% larger than the same selection by the reference** (26,830 bytes against 25,870 for page 2 of `pdf-qpdf-form-errors.pdf`) because the copy path rebuilt the form: the output held 14 widget annotations and 66 objects where the in-document selection holds 11 and 55. It also lost `/Lang` and `/OpenAction`, which the in-document selection keeps for the pages that stay.
- **Trade-offs**:
  - **One more process for a damaged file.** The probe that asks the PDF tool for the pages costs about 3 ms per compression; a document that has a loop costs two more runs (export of the objects, update), whose export is as large as the object list of the file.
  - **A large object export is not mended.** The export is read only up to 32 MiB (plus the 1 MiB qpdf may print besides): on the 43 PDFs of the pdf-ops corpus it is 0.5 to 3.4 times the file, at most 2.4 MB, and packed object streams can expand a hostile file far further. Past the limit the document is returned as it is and the later steps answer as they would without the repair.
  - **A page listed twice is not changed.** Only a repeated `/Pages` node is a loop; a page object two nodes list is read by the PDF tool as a copy and passes the check.
  - **Delete reads the page count in a second process, run at the same time as the edit**, so a wall time of the longer of the two; on a single busy core it is up to 3 ms more, and the launcher counts two programs for a delete, not one.
  - **A delete of a tagged or bookmarked document is larger than before.** The structure tree, outlines and names are kept, so the output equals the reference's size and is up to a third larger than the earlier route's (a 7-page government form, page 2 deleted: 603,173 bytes against 448,649; the reference writes 603,173). The seven delete size rows of `bench/baseline.json` that had recorded the smaller output are updated to the reference's size.
  - **Outline items of a deleted page stay.** Deleting inside the document keeps the outline and the named destinations, as the reference does, and an item that pointed at a deleted page points at nothing; the earlier route dropped the outline, the named destinations and the open action of every document.
  - **A looping file costs the page operations three more processes**: the failed run, the export and the update, then the run again (the probe of the mend adds about 3 ms). A healthy file costs nothing more.
  - **Protection and unlock ask about the loop beside their qpdf run.** Protection runs the encryption and the PDF tool's page listing on the same file at the same time; unlock runs the listing on the encrypted file, with the same password (on standard input, as the decryption has it), beside the decryption. Each stops the other when one fails, so a document that is fine waits for one run (a second process, about 4 ms of CPU, not of wall time: unlock of a healthy encrypted 3-page file took 17.9 ms against 18.0 ms before). Only when the listing reports the loop is anything done after the run: protection discards its encrypted result, mends the tree and encrypts the mended bytes again; unlock mends the decrypted bytes. A loop is recognised in the whole output of the PDF tool, not its first line (it warns about a repeated or untyped page before it refuses the loop), by one matcher that the page operations, protection and unlock share; a mend that follows such a report does not list the pages a second time. A listing that fails for another reason changes nothing, and an unencrypted file is returned by unlock as it is, with no question asked. A looping file costs the export and the update besides. The first version of unlock listed the pages after the decryption, which made a healthy unlock about a third slower in the CI speed comparison.
- **Alternatives rejected**:
  - **Returning Ghostscript's output for a looping file**: matches the reference, which keeps neither the text nor the page.
  - **Rebuilding the whole tree as one flat `/Pages` node**: it would also mend a tree that is merely deep, but it rewrites every page's `/Parent` and the inherited resources, and the page tree of a file without a loop would change for no reason.
  - **Always running the object export before compression**: reads the file twice for the files that need nothing.
  - **Counting the pages of the result after the delete**: a second process after the first, on every delete.
  - **Dropping a deleted page's form fields in the product**: the PDF tool already removes the fields and widgets of the pages it drops when the selection is made inside the document.
- **Verified by**: `tests/pdf-ops-damaged-samples.test.ts` (the three public files, read back by `qpdf --check`, Poppler's text and page count, and the object graph of `qpdf --json`, against the same selection written by the `qpdf` command line); `tests/pdf-ops-looping-tree.test.ts` (extract, delete, reorder, rotate and split of a hand-built three-page looping file and of the two public looping files, read back by `qpdf --check`, `qpdf --show-npages`, Poppler's page count, rotation and text against the pages written into the file or listed by `qpdf --json`); `tests/pdf-page-tree.test.ts` (object lists worked out on paper, loops of one, three and 50 nodes, a mesh of 300 nodes, a 50,000-node chain, a layered tree with 20^10 paths); `tests/pdf-page-ops.test.ts`; the same file for watermark (its text on every page by Poppler), protection (`qpdf --check` and `--json` with the open password, the encryption asked for), unlock of an encrypted copy, and merge of a looping file with a healthy one in both orders and of an encrypted looping file; and the `pdf-ops` parity rows `pdf-qpdf-pages-loop.pdf->compress`, `pdf-pdfjs-Pages-tree-refs.pdf->compress` and `pdf-qpdf-form-errors.pdf->delete`.
- **Revisit when**: the PDF page tool mends a looping tree itself, the in-process parser guards against loops (the stack-overflow test of watermark and merge can go), or the probe of protection and unlock shows in their timings.

## 54. VP9 for jobs too big for the media time limit, and audio files with no samples

- **Status**: Implemented (issue #783, media rows), decided 2026-10-19 (QA request in issue #783: a real file converts, or is refused with a typed answer that names a real limit of the file; found by the nightly real-file run).
- **Decision**:
  - **VP9 keeps the documented encoder setting (good deadline, cpu-used 2, row multithreading, four tile columns) for every job that fits the media time limit, and uses the realtime deadline at cpu-used 5 (same threading) for a job that does not.** A job is estimated as its frames times its picture size, divided by 15 megapixels per CPU second (measured for the whole conversion, tone mapping and decoding of a 10-bit source included) and by two cores. When that time is more than half of the job's own stage limit (`3 x seconds + 60`, at most the stage limit, which is 180 s unless the job or the server sets a shorter one), the realtime deadline is used. The constant-quality value (CRF 30 by default) does not change. The rule applies to VP9 in WebM, MP4 and Matroska, when the picture reaches the encoder at the source's own size and frame rate; a request that scales, crops or changes the frame rate, a two-pass encode (it needs the good deadline) and a source whose frame count or length is not stated keep the good deadline.
  - **The frame count of a plain MP4 is read from its movie header** (the sample table of the video track), so planning still costs no prober process; other inputs take the frame rate and length from the one probe the conversion already makes.
  - **HDR10 video to WebM stays tone mapped to SDR (8-bit BT.709).** Keeping the HDR picture means VP9 profile 2 with 10-bit samples and PQ, BT.2020 tags; it is not offered (see below).
  - **An audio file without samples is refused for `.aac`, `.m4a`, `.mp3`, `.opus` and `.ogg` with a typed 422 (`EmptyAudioInputError`): "The audio file holds no audio samples, so there is nothing to write as a .<target> file that a player or reader can open".** The check costs no process for a normal conversion. After the run it looks only at an output of zero bytes, or at a target of those five whose output is under 8 KB, and then reads at most one packet from the start of the input's audio streams with ffprobe (the input has to hold an audio stream at all); an input that has packets stays a success (a small real file) or, for zero bytes, the plain error it was. Both engines (in-process and worker) use the same check, whatever the source format. WAV and FLAC targets, which hold a stream of zero frames as a file readers open, are not changed.
  - **A stream of fewer than 16 samples converts to FLAC, whether the file is `.flac`, `.ogg` with `audio.codec: "flac"` or Matroska with FLAC audio.** FFmpeg 9.0 opens its FLAC encoder with the length of the first frame it is given, so a stream shorter than FLAC's minimum block of 16 samples stopped it with `[flac] invalid block size: N` and wrote nothing (6.1 and 7.1 take the same stream). A transcode whose log shows that line is started again once with the encoder's block size stated (`-frame_size:a 4096`, the size of the default block; a stream shorter than a block is one frame whatever the size), and the file is the FLAC of every sample, read back sample for sample. The `.ogg` target itself writes Vorbis, with the quality scale of entry 3, and takes a stream of one sample on every build that has the encoder.
- **Why**:
  - **The good deadline does not get cheaper with cpu-used.** On the 30 s, 1080p, 60 fps HDR10 clip of the corpus (1,797 frames) encoded from tone-mapped lossless frames on four cores (Ubuntu 24.04 VM, FFmpeg 6.1.1 with libvpx-vp9, CRF 30):

    | Deadline, cpu-used | Wall s | CPU s | File MB | SSIM | PSNR dB |
    |---|---|---|---|---|---|
    | good 2 (documented) | 61.8 | 208.8 | 20.7 | 0.9968 | 47.9 |
    | good 3 | 58.0 | 187.0 | 21.3 | 0.9966 | 47.7 |
    | good 4 | 55.7 | 179.9 | 21.4 | 0.9966 | 47.5 |
    | good 5 | 64.1 | 182.5 | 21.3 | not measured | not measured |
    | realtime 3 | 79.9 | 263.9 | 19.9 | 0.9967 | 47.7 |
    | realtime 4 | 31.7 | 98.7 | 26.7 | 0.9967 | 47.8 |
    | **realtime 5 (chosen)** | **16.9** | **55.4** | **26.3** | **0.9959** | **47.3** |
    | realtime 6 | 14.8 | 47.6 | 29.4 | 0.9958 | 47.2 |
    | realtime 8 | 12.7 | 39.6 | 34.0 | 0.9953 | 46.9 |

    The encoder's own search stays at its full cost at every good-deadline speed, so a higher cpu-used buys at most 10 percent; the realtime deadline at 5 costs a quarter of the CPU. The auto-alt-ref, lag-in-frames and temporal-dependency switches of a one-pass constant-quality encode write files of the same size to the byte, with the same scores, at the same cost (measured), and frame-parallel only changes how the file can be decoded.
  - **The whole conversion, before and after** (the clip as the real-file run converts it, default options; the limit is 150 s):

    | Run | Before (good 2) | After (realtime 5) |
    |---|---|---|
    | one job on four idle cores | 79.2 to 80.6 s, 20.7 MB, SSIM 0.9968 | 27.5 to 29.3 s, 26.3 MB, SSIM 0.9959 |
    | three jobs at once on four cores (the nightly shard) | all three stop at the 150 s limit (`SandboxedTimeoutError`) | 67.9, 70.5 and 70.6 s |

    All 1,797 frames, 1920x1080, `yuv420p`, BT.709 tags (tv range) and a full decode without error are the same before and after.
  - **Why the quality is comparable.** At the same CRF the realtime encode scores 0.0009 lower SSIM and 0.6 dB lower PSNR against the lossless tone-mapped frames (both above 47 dB, where the difference is not visible) and writes a file 27 percent larger. The size is the price; the picture is what the constant quality asked for. CRF is not raised to claw the size back, because that would lower the picture of the jobs that are already the largest.
  - **Why a rule on the job and not a new default.** The good deadline at cpu-used 2 is the setting the project documents and compares with the reference encoder (the video parity rows use the same arguments), and every short clip fits the limit with it; a faster default would give up compression on all of them for the sake of the few that do not fit. The estimate uses two cores because a worker runs several jobs at once and the nightly run puts three on a four-core runner, which is how the clip timed out although it takes 80 s alone.
  - **Why HDR is not kept in WebM.** The VP9 authors' HDR encoding guidance is profile 2, `yuv420p10le`, PQ transfer (16), BT.2020 primaries (9) and matrix (9), limited range, with about 25 to 30 percent more bitrate than SDR; FFmpeg cannot write mastering-display and content-light metadata into WebM (it only passes metadata that is already there; the guidance adds it with a separate Matroska tool). The same clip kept in HDR at the good deadline took 174.5 s and 326 CPU seconds on the idle VM, which is past the 150 s limit by itself, and scored SSIM 0.980 against the HDR source. Tone mapping to SDR was already the behaviour (the BT.2390 curve by default, `toneMap` in the request) and finishes inside the limit.
  - **Why `.aac` refuses and not writes an empty file.** An AAC file (ADTS, ISO/IEC 14496-3) is a series of frames that each carry their own header; it has no file header, so zero frames are zero bytes. FFmpeg writes 0 bytes for the empty WAV with the ADTS muxer and with `-c:a aac`, `file` calls the result "empty", and ffprobe and ffmpeg refuse it ("End of file"). An empty output is not a file of the target type, and the cause is the input, which the refusal names.
  - **Why `.m4a`, `.mp3`, `.opus` and `.ogg` refuse too.** Measured with FFmpeg 9.0 (Mac) and 6.1.1 (Linux VM) for the empty WAV: `.m4a` 257 to 258 bytes (a `ftyp` and an empty `mdat`, no `moov`, no stream: ffprobe lists nothing and ffmpeg has no stream to decode), `.mp3` 260 bytes (an ID3 tag and one padding frame; ffprobe and ffmpeg fail with "Failed to find two consecutive MPEG audio frames"), `.opus` 135 to 137 bytes and `.ogg` 160 bytes with FLAC or 2,636 bytes with Vorbis (the header pages only; both readers stop at "End of file" because they need a first audio page). A reader-valid empty file exists for none of them from FFmpeg: the one candidate, an `.m4a` written with `-movflags +empty_moov` (737 bytes, ffprobe shows the AAC stream and decoding succeeds), is a fragmented MP4 with no fragment, not the progressive file an `.m4a` is expected to be, and is not used. A file that no reader opens, reported as a success, is worse than a named refusal, and the cause is the input.
  - **The FLAC in the `.ogg` row was FFmpeg's choice for a build with no Vorbis encoder.** The measurement that raised the row (a 1-sample WAV to `.ogg`, "invalid block size") ran FFmpeg directly on the Mac build, where the Ogg muxer's default audio codec is FLAC because libvorbis is absent; the conversion itself refuses `.ogg` there with an engine-unavailable answer, and with libvorbis a WAV of 1, 2, 15, 16 or 17 samples converts on 6.1.1 (Linux VM, CI), 7.1.5 (the worker image's version, a static build) and 9.0.2, and ffprobe and ffmpeg read the file (a Vorbis stream whose last page states a length of the input). The FLAC failure is real for `.flac` and for FLAC inside Ogg or Matroska, on 9.0 only.
  - **FLAC can hold the stream.** RFC 9639 lets the last block of a stream be shorter than the minimum, and the reference encoder writes a one-sample file (STREAMINFO block size 4096, one frame of one sample) that its test mode, ffprobe and ffmpeg read; FFmpeg's encoder is what refuses it, not the format. So the file exists and the answer is a file, not a refusal.
- **Trade-offs**:
  - **A job past the estimate gets a file about a quarter larger** than the good deadline would write at the same CRF, in exchange for finishing: 150 s limit, 27.5 s alone.
  - **The estimate is a model.** It was measured on one clip and one machine class (arm64 VM, FFmpeg 6.1.1); a host that is faster or has more free cores than two will switch some jobs to realtime that would have fitted (the cost is the size above), and a slower one can still time out for a job just under the line. The numbers are constants in `media-vp9-effort.ts`.
  - **Requests that scale, crop or change the rate are not estimated.** A 4K source scaled to 1080p keeps the good deadline although its decoding and scaling are the size of the source.
  - **An input that FFmpeg turns into a small output costs one ffprobe.** An output under 8 KB for those five targets (a very short clip) reads one packet of the input to tell it from an empty one; larger outputs, and every other target, are never probed.
  - **Short outputs of other targets are not changed.** On FFmpeg 9.0.2 a WAV of 1 to 15 samples converted to `.opus` is written without an audio page and ffprobe cannot open it, where 6.1.1 and 7.1.5 write 313 to 328 bytes that open; and `.aac` of such a WAV is one valid ADTS frame that FFmpeg's probe does not recognise on any of the three builds (it needs consecutive frames: "Invalid data found"). The check of this entry tells an empty input from a short one by the input's packets and does not read the output.
- **Alternatives rejected**:
  - **cpu-used 3 to 5 at the good deadline for everything**: saves 10 percent at most (table), and moves the parity rows away from the documented setting.
  - **A bigger time limit**: the tier ceiling is 180 s; the clip needs 80 s alone and 180 s or more with two other jobs, so the ceiling would have to rise past what a request may hold a worker for.
  - **Splitting the tone mapping and the encode into two processes**: saves the 16 s of filtering at most (FFmpeg 6.1 runs filters and encoder in one thread) and adds a pipe and a second process to every HDR job.
  - **AV1 (SVT-AV1) for long jobs**: much faster at the same quality, but it changes the codec of the output; the caller asked for VP9 by taking the WebM default.
  - **An empty `.aac` file**: see above. **An `.m4a` with an empty track instead**: another format than the one asked for.
  - **Padding a short stream with silence to 16 samples:** the file would be longer than the audio it came from.
  - **The in-process FLAC encoder for a short `.flac`:** it writes a one-sample file, but not FLAC inside Ogg or Matroska, and two routes for one target.
  - **Passing the block size on every FLAC encode, or estimating the length from a probe first:** the first changes the block size of every file (4096 where the default is 4608 at 44.1 kHz) and the second adds a process to every FLAC conversion; the log of the run that fails is the evidence, and it costs the conversions that do not fail nothing.
- **Verified by**: `tests/media-vp9-effort.test.ts` (the deadline for nine workloads with the sizes the measurements were taken at, the monotone rule, the arguments of a built 1080p60 clip at 3 s and 30 s for WebM, MP4 and Matroska, the requests that keep the good deadline including both passes of a two-pass encode, the frame count of a plain MP4 against ffprobe's packet count, and the real HDR10 clip), `tests/media-hdr-webm-speed.perf.test.ts` (nightly; the real clip converted by the default route inside half of the limit, with ffprobe's frame count equal to the source's 1,797, the SDR tags, a full decode and SSIM 0.99 or more against the lossless tone-mapped frames), `tests/media-empty-audio.test.ts` (a WAV with an empty data chunk and the corpus file `kaitai-small.wav`, shown empty by the RIFF layout and by ffprobe, refused for `.aac` by both engines with status 422 and the message, refused for `.m4a`, `.mp3`, `.opus` and `.ogg` by both engines with status 422 and the message, still converted to FLAC and WAV and read back, and a WAV of one second converted to a decodable `.m4a`, `.mp3`, `.opus` and `.ogg` with packets and the right length, and to AAC, also from one sample), `tests/media-short-audio.test.ts` (WAV files of 1, 2, 15, 16 and 17 samples written by the test converted to `.flac` and to `.ogg` with FLAC audio, then read back by a separate ffmpeg with every decode error fatal: the codec, the sample count and every sample equal to the ones written; the worker engine for one sample; the default `.ogg` read by ffprobe and ffmpeg with the length of the input in its last page), the Linux run described above, and the video and audio parity rows.
- **Revisit when**: the worker host has far more cores than the two assumed, or the time limit changes; a faster VP9 setting is found that keeps the file size (re-run the table); QA wants HDR kept in WebM (the limit then has to allow the 10-bit encode, 175 s on four cores for this clip); an FFmpeg release writes a reader-valid empty `.m4a`, `.mp3`, `.opus` or `.ogg`; or the worker image moves to FFmpeg 9 (the short `.opus` and `.aac` outputs above then reach production).

## 55. HTML pages left open, code points XML cannot carry, and books with no content

- **Status**: Implemented (HTML to Word, ODF and EPUB rows of issue #783, epic #760), decided 2026-10-19 (QA request).
- **Decision**:
  - **An HTML page is read with the structure HTML gives it when an author leaves elements open**, so the text of the page reaches the Word, ODF, EPUB and the other targets written from the document model, and the in-process PDF renderer. A `<head>` ends at the first element that does not belong in a head (anything but `base`, `basefont`, `bgsound`, `link`, `meta`, `noframes`, `noscript`, `script`, `style`, `template` and `title`) or at the first text that is not white space. A second `<html>`, `<head>` or `<body>` start tag is ignored, and what follows it joins the first element. A `<table>` that starts directly in a table, a row group or a row ends that table and starts a sibling. A cell that starts where no row is open (directly in the table or a row group) gets a row. A `<form>` that starts between the rows of a table holds nothing, so the rows after it are the table's. A column group ends at the first start tag that is not a column. Text and elements directly in a list, between the items, are read as ordinary blocks at the place they stand.
  - **Control codes that XML 1.0 has no place for are dropped, and the document is converted.** In the Word, ODF, EPUB and XHTML writers, a line tabulation (U+000B) or form feed (U+000C) becomes a line feed: both are white space in HTML and Unicode and separate lines or pages, so dropping them would join the last word before them to the first after. Any other U+0000 to U+001F but tab, line feed and carriage return is left out of the text (a DOS end-of-file mark U+001A, a NUL). They have no glyph and no meaning in the output, and the PDF writers already drop them. A lone surrogate, U+FFFE and U+FFFF stand for a character that was lost before the writer, so each becomes U+FFFD and the conversion reports it (entry 7) in one line for every lone surrogate (`Replaced a lone surrogate with U+FFFD: …`) and one each for U+FFFE and U+FFFF (`Replaced U+FFFE with U+FFFD: XML 1.0 cannot carry this code point (a lone surrogate or a noncharacter).`), so a text of many distinct lone surrogates cannot fill the warning lines of the conversion. No writer refuses a document for either any more.
  - **A document with no content is written as a book with one empty content document**, as the Word and ODF writers write one empty paragraph (entry 8). A frameset-only page, a page of blanks and a page with an empty body convert to the three targets and to the PDF (entry 51) alike, each with no text. **What the reader leaves out is reported on the result** (entry 7), in the words of the PDF routes (entry 51): an image that cannot be read is `Left out the image "…": <reason>.` (the reference quoted without control codes and cut at 120 characters, at most 50 images listed and the rest counted), and the elements no target carries (`svg`, `object`, `embed`, `iframe`, `canvas`, `video`, `audio`) and the frames of a frameset page are one counted line, `Left out elements that cannot be drawn (N): <tag> x N.`, so a frameset page gives one line, not one per frame. They reach `metadata.warnings` of every conversion that reads HTML into the document model. The reader collected these lines on the model and no writer passed them on, so they were lost.
  - **An unknown element that holds blocks reads as those blocks, and text in a table outside a cell reads as text before the table.** An inline element (an unknown one such as `<noindex>`, or `<font>`, `<span>`, `<a>`) around divisions, lists, tables or paragraphs is split around them, as a browser lays it out: the blocks stay blocks and each piece of inline content between them is in a copy of the element, so its link and formatting carry to every block. Text, and an element that is not table content, in a table, row group or row but outside a cell is moved to just before the table (HTML foster parenting); an inline element left open between a table and its rows, or between the cells of a row, ends when the next row or cell starts.
  - **An id is written once, as an XML name, and a link follows it.** In EPUB, Word and ODF output an id (a bookmark name) occurs once: the first element of a source id keeps it, a later one with the same id gets `-2`, `-3`; a character an XML name lacks (a space) becomes `_` and a name that does not start like one gets a leading `_`; an id the EPUB writer makes up (`h-1`, `footnote-1`) is not taken. A link finds its anchor by the fragment as written, then percent-decoded, as a browser does; a link with no anchor is not a link. A link target is written as a URI reference: a character a URI cannot carry (a space, `[`, `|`, `^`, `<`, a non-ASCII letter) is percent-encoded as UTF-8, and a web address the URL parser refuses (a space inside the host) stays text.
  - **Every element with an `id` is a link target, not only the elements read as blocks** (HTML Living Standard, "the indicated part of the document": the first element whose `id` is the fragment as written, else percent-decoded, else the first `a` whose `name` is that; an `a` named like the `id` of any element is not what a fragment finds, and an `a` with both an `id` and a different `name` is the target of both). The id of a container (`div`, `section`, `article`, `aside`, `header`, `footer`, `nav`, `main`, `figure`, `form`, `details`, `dl`, `blockquote`, the `body`) or of a list, table, row group, row, cell or caption is an anchor at the start of the first paragraph, heading or list item that element produces, in front of the ids of the containers inside it, so nested containers that start at the same paragraph give it several anchors. An element that produces no text (an empty division, an empty paragraph, a named anchor between two paragraphs, a picture left out) anchors at the place it stands: the start of the next paragraph, heading or item, or, at the end of the page, after the last text. Preformatted text and a rule hold no runs, so their ids (and the ids inside preformatted text) stand in a paragraph of their own in front of the block, and after such a block at the end of the page in a paragraph after it. An id on a picture or a `<br>` anchors where it stands. The first of two equal ids is the target (the rule of the previous bullet). In the PDF written by the office suite (entry 51) the page it is handed gives each `div`, `section`, `article`, `aside`, `header`, `footer`, `nav`, `main`, `figure`, `figcaption`, `caption`, `details`, `fieldset`, `legend`, `summary`, `center`, `hgroup` and `label` with an `id`, and each element it is rebuilt without (a `form`), a named anchor of that id where it starts, because the office suite makes the ids of the other elements link targets and not these (24.2 and 26.8).
- **Why**:
  - **The omissions were silent.** Over the 450 saved pages of the real-world sample (govdocs1) converted to Word, ODF and EPUB, 356 pages (the same 356 for each target) now report a warning that none reported before: 9,489 image lines and 87 lines counting images past the listing limit in all three targets, and 39 lines for elements (24 for 8 framesets, 9 for 3 pages with an `object`, 3 each for an `iframe` and an `embed`), at most 51 lines for a page; no byte of the document body, styles or media changes (only the timestamp and identifier parts of the package differ between runs).
  - **The reader dropped the text of pages that browsers show.** The 450 saved pages of the real-world sample (govdocs1) were read by the parse5 HTML parser as the oracle, and the words of each page were compared with the words in the document model (the Word, ODF and EPUB writers share it; the Word and ODF documents converted and kept the empty model, silently). Of the 439 pages with words (the options of drop-down lists left out of the comparison, as forms are not drawn), 26 kept less than 95% of their words, 16 less than half and 9 none: the body sat inside a `<head>` that was never closed (the page opens with `<head>` and the next tag is `<body>`), second `<html>` and `<head>` tags nested the page in the first head, tables held a table or cells with no row, rows sat in a `<form>` or a column group, and text sat in a list outside its items. After the change two pages are below 95% (64% and 80%): their navigation lists sit in an element the reader does not know (`<noindex>`), where the items are read as one paragraph and the words are glued together (`HomeImage`) but kept.
  - **Eight of these pages made the EPUB writer refuse the document** ("no content to put in an EPUB") although they hold text, and the Word and ODF writers wrote an empty document for them, with the text in the Markdown and text results. Ten more pages of the 450 hold no text and no picture (framesets, a page of blanks, a body that only sends the reader elsewhere): they convert to the PDF with one page and no text (pdfinfo and pdftotext), and the EPUB was the only writer that refused them.
  - **The control codes of the sample are the ones real files carry**: a DOS end-of-file mark (U+001A) after the closing tag of two pages, six U+0000 after a list item, and form feeds in typewritten text. All five converted to PDF, Markdown and text and were refused by the three XML writers.
  - **Dropping a control code leaves no trace, and it does not matter**: the PDF writers drop them for the same reason (no glyph), and Word and LibreOffice drop them on import. A replacement mark would put a visible mark at the end of a file for a code the author never saw. A lone surrogate or a noncharacter is different: it stands for a character that existed and was lost, so the mark and the warning of entry 7 say so.
  - **The same tree feeds the PDF routes, so 16 of the 450 pages convert to a different PDF.** The staging for the office suite (entry 51) and the in-process renderer read the tree this change builds. Measured with pdftotext against the words of parse5 (each word counted once), five pages show more of their words (the body of `govdocs1-000190` was 1027 words short and is now complete, `govdocs1-001323` 289 short and now 4, and `-000607`, `-000894`, `-000191` a few) and one shows 14 fewer (`govdocs1-001742`: the staged HTML holds them, and the office suite's table layout puts them past the right edge of the page, where its text is cut off). The other ten differ in layout only (a line break, a hyphenated word, a page).
  - **An empty book is valid.** EPUBCheck 5.4.0 reports no error and no warning for the book (a navigation document that links the one content document by the title, an empty section).
  - **Words were glued and dropped, and the books failed validation.** Over the 450 saved pages (govdocs1) converted to EPUB and read back with parse5 as the oracle (each word counted once), 46 pages lacked words (669 in all) and 30 pages held glued words (`HomeImage`, `TopAbstract`; 152 in all); now 12 pages lack words (30; the options and labels of form controls, which stay undrawn) and 10 hold glued tokens (the options of drop-down lists). EPUBCheck 5.4.0 reported RSC-005 or RSC-020 for 48 of the 450 books (47 messages on 27 books for ids used twice or holding a space, 78 on 29 books for link targets with a space, `[`, `|`, `^` or `<`); it reports none for any of the 450 now, and the 1,239 internal links of the books are the same links. The Word and ODF documents held 253 repeated bookmark names on 21 pages; none now. Every XML part of the 1,350 documents parses (xmllint), no conversion fails that converted before, and the text of the in-process PDF renderer changes on 4 pages by order only (text in a table moves before it).
  - **Links to divisions did not link.** Of the 450 saved pages (govdocs1), 182 hold 2,564 internal links whose fragment names an element in the page (parse5 reading the page by the rule of the standard). Written to Word, ODF and EPUB, 1,239 of them were links (1,238 at the word a browser scrolls to) and 1,325 were plain text, because the id of a division, section, table, row, cell, list or caption, or a named anchor with no text, was not kept. Now 2,102 are links, 2,094 of them at the word a browser scrolls to (the 8 others stand in preformatted text, where the target is the start of the text, or at a word the parser and the writer split differently); of the 462 that are still text, those listed for a sample of pages are links around a picture alone, which no writer links (121 of the 153 listed), and fragments that name an element inside text the writer does not keep. 247 of the 450 EPUB books change; EPUBCheck 5.2.1 reports no message for any of the 247 (nor for the 60 of them read before the change). No conversion fails that converted before. The 302 quality rows of the document family (strict mode, Linux) are equal before and after, 3 of them HTML to PDF rows.
  - **The office suite does not link the ids of containers.** Measured on 24.2 and 26.8 with a link to each element in turn: paragraphs, headings, lists, items, tables, rows, cells, preformatted text, rules, pictures and inline elements are targets of a link in the PDF, `div`, `section`, `article`, `aside`, `header`, `footer`, `nav`, `main`, `figure`, `figcaption`, `caption`, `details`, `fieldset`, `legend`, `summary`, `center`, `hgroup` and `label` are not. With the named anchor the page is given, each is, on the page of its text (qpdf reading the link annotations, pdftotext the words under the destination), also for ids with a space or letters outside ASCII written as is or percent-escaped, and for two equal ids (the first).
- **Trade-offs**:
  - **The reader follows the rules of HTML for the elements above only.** The rest of the tree-building algorithm (the adoption agency for misnested formatting, the form element pointer) is not implemented; a page that depends on it keeps its words but may lose the placement.
  - **A target in preformatted text is at the start of the text.** Preformatted text is one block with no runs, so a link to an id inside it lands at the start of the block, not the line. An id on preformatted text or a rule, or an id waiting in front of one, adds an empty paragraph in front of it in the Word, ODF and EPUB output (one blank line in Word and ODF).
  - **The in-process PDF renderer writes no internal links at all.** A link whose target is an id (of a division or of a heading) is text there, as before; the office suite route (entry 51) writes them, and the in-process route is used when the suite is missing or an orientation is requested.
  - **A form feed is a line break, not a page break, in the Word and ODF output.** Typewritten text that used it to start a page is not paginated there, though its words stay apart; the PDF writers do not paginate on it either.
  - **A NUL or an end-of-file mark inside a word joins the two halves** (`ab` + U+0001 + `cd` is `abcd`).
  - **Forms stay undrawn** (entry 51): the options of a drop-down list and the values of controls are not in the text, in any target.
- **Alternatives rejected**:
  - **Keeping the refusal for control codes** (the previous behaviour): it refused a whole book for a byte at the end of a file that no reader sees.
  - **Replacing control codes with U+FFFD**: the document would show a mark where the source shows nothing.
  - **Writing the code as a character reference** (`&#x1A;`): XML 1.0 does not allow the reference either; XML 1.1 does, and no reader of Word, ODF or EPUB reads it.
  - **A full HTML tree builder (parse5) as the reader**: a larger change to a code path that the in-process renderer and the staging for the office suite share, with limits (nesting depth, element count) that this reader enforces while reading. The cases above are the ones the corpus shows.
  - **Refusing an EPUB with no content with a clearer message**: the other writers convert the same document, and entry 8 asks for an empty valid output.
  - **A field on each block for its targets** (a block-level anchor in the document model): exact for preformatted text and rules, but every writer and every walker of the model (renaming, bookmark lists, chapter lookup) would change for two kinds of block that rarely carry an id; the empty paragraph costs one blank line only on the pages that need it.
  - **Handing the target of preformatted text to the next paragraph**: the link lands after the block, which can be a whole page of text away; a link that lands in the wrong place is worse than the paragraph in front of the block.
  - **Ids of elements the reader leaves out** (`script`, `template`, `noscript`, the head): they are not drawn, so there is nowhere to scroll to; a link to one stays text.
- **Verified by**: `tests/html-implied-structure.test.ts` (nine derived pages and twelve pages of the corpus, each to Word, ODF and EPUB, with the words of parse5 for the page found in order in the words of the document read by saxes and parse5; the four pages with no text; EPUBCheck on the book of a frameset page), `tests/xml-unwritable-characters.test.ts` (every control code, a lone surrogate, U+FFFE and U+FFFF written to the three formats and every XML part of the package checked by xmllint; a line tabulation and a form feed keeping the words on either side apart; at most three warning lines; five pages of the corpus with U+001A, U+0000 and U+000C to the three formats; EPUBCheck on the book of the page with six U+0000; LibreOffice reading the Word and ODF documents of the page with U+001A), `tests/epub-structure.test.ts` (the writer given a document with no content), `tests/pdf-to-docx-structure.test.ts` (the Word writer given each kind of code point), and a run of the 450 pages to the 14 targets of an HTML page before (6264 converted, 36 refused) and after (6297 converted; the 3 PDF refusals of `govdocs1-000455` (nested tables, no office suite on the machine) and of two pages with `&` in a style sheet are the same, and no pair that converted before is refused). The 450 EPUB, Word and ODF files have every XML part well-formed (xmllint, 7156 parts), the EPUB books give EPUBCheck 5.4.0 no error that the old code did not also give (48 of the 450 books have errors, all duplicate `id` values or illegal fragment characters that the source pages carry; 43 of the 48 converted before with the same count, one with one more because the page now holds more, and 5 are pages that the writer refused before), and LibreOffice opened the Word and ODF files of the pages with control codes. `tests/html-division-anchors.test.ts` (parse5 reading a page by the rule of the standard against the link targets of the Word, ODF and EPUB files read by saxes and parse5: 15 container tags, the body, nested containers, empty containers (between paragraphs, at the end, after preformatted text), a table with its row groups, rows, cells and caption, lists, spans, pictures, preformatted text and rules, equal ids, a named anchor beside an id, ids with a space, a non-ASCII letter and percent escapes; the HTML to PDF route with qpdf and pdftotext over 13 container tags, the body, nested containers, an empty division, equal ids and the escaped ids; the page handed to the office suite) and a run of the 450 pages to Word, ODF and EPUB before and after (links and their targets compared with parse5; EPUBCheck 5.2.1 on the 247 books that change). `tests/html-reader-wrappers-and-anchors.test.ts` (parse5 words for pages with unknown wrappers and text in a table, to Word, ODF and EPUB; EPUBCheck, saxes and parse5 on the ids, bookmark names and links of derived pages; link targets; real pages of the corpus).
- **Revisit when**: a page of the corpus loses its words to a tree-building rule not listed here (a misnested formatting element), the options of a drop-down list matter to a caller, the Word and ODF writers carry page breaks for form feeds, a page of the corpus links into preformatted text often enough that the line matters, or the in-process PDF renderer writes internal links.


## 56. Archive rows of the real-file run: stripped rpm payloads, alternate streams, named volumes and methods, lzip and lzop listings

- **Status**: Implemented (archive rows of issue #783, epic #760), decided 2026-10-19 (QA request in issue #783). Extends [41. Archive sources](#41-archive-sources-package-payloads-links-disk-images-lzip-and-lzop).
- **Decision**:
  - **An rpm whose payload has stripped cpio headers converts to the files it installs.** Since rpm 4.12 a payload may be a cpio of `07070X`, the index of the file in the package header as 8 hex digits, and the content; the names, modes and sizes are only in the package header, so 7-Zip lists such a package as empty. The converter takes the payload from the package itself (everything after the metadata header), decompresses it like any other payload (at most three layers, within the package's byte budget), reads the file table from the header (RPMTAG_BASENAMES, DIRNAMES, DIRINDEXES, FILEMODES, FILESIZES or LONGFILESIZES, FILEMTIMES, FILEFLAGS, FILEINODES, FILEDEVICES, FILELINKTOS), and writes an ordinary cpio named `.<path>` for each entry, which then goes through the same 7-Zip listing, vetting and extraction as the payload of every other rpm. Which entries carry content is decided as rpm decides it (lib/rpmfi.c): a regular file has its size unless it is one of several hard links (regular, not ghost, same device and a nonzero inode), when only the last link of the set carries it; a symbolic link carries its target (taken from the header); everything else carries none; a ghost is not in the payload. The links of a set are written with one inode number and the set size as link count, with the content on the last link, which is how a package's own cpio stores them. Every file of the header that is not a ghost must appear in the payload exactly once, an index outside the table, a repeated index, a payload cut short, a payload with no trailer and a hard-link set whose content does not come last are refused as damaged.
  - **Alternate streams of a disk image are left out, and one warning counts them.** 7-Zip lists no alternate stream unless `-sns` is given and extracts them as files named `<file>:<stream>` unless `-sns-` is given. The listing now runs with `-sns` so every stream is seen, flagged by the listing's own `Alternate Stream` property, and the extraction runs with `-sns-` so none is written. Streams are not vetted, not counted against the entry, size and ratio caps and not named in the output; the result carries, after any warnings it already has, `metadata.warnings = ["N alternate streams (extended attributes and resource forks of the files) were left out."]` (`1 alternate stream ... was left out.` for one). There is no switch to keep them. A stream is told by the property and by the shape of its name (a colon in the last path segment), never by the name alone, so a flag forged on a plain name leaves the entry vetted like any other and the drive-letter rule of the entry-name check is untouched: an entry named `C:evil.txt` that is not a stream is still refused as an absolute path, and a stream such as `A:com.apple.provenance` (a volume named with one letter) no longer refuses the image.
  - **The first part of a multi-volume RAR set names the volume that is missing.** The refusal of entry 41 now says `the next volume, <name>, is not part of the upload`. The name is built from the name the caller gave the archive and the numbering the archive declares: a RAR 4.x main header carries a flag for `.partN.rar` numbering (the next number, with the same zero padding) or else the old `.rar`, `.r00`, ... numbering (the first volume is followed by `<base>.r00`); a RAR 5 set is numbered by its name alone, so only a `.partN.rar` name gives one. When the name cannot be derived (no file name, or a name the numbering cannot be read from) the message is the earlier one, which says only that the other parts are missing.
  - **An entry whose compression method 7-Zip does not decode is refused as an unsupported method.** 7-Zip prints `ERROR: Unsupported Method : <entry>`; the refusal is an `UnsupportedArchiveMethodError` (HTTP 422, the type the in-process readers already use) that names the entry and the method the listing gives for it (`the data of 'a.txt' uses the compression method 96, which this 7-Zip build does not decode.`). The line must name an entry of the vetted listing exactly: 7-Zip prints the same words, followed by the cause, for a wrong password, and that line is never claimed.
  - **Archive inspection lists lzip and lzop files.** `inspectArchive` recognises them by their first bytes (`LZIP`; `89 4C 5A 4F 00 0D 0A 1A 0A`) and reads them with the in-process readers of entry 41 under the same size and ratio caps as the conversion: a tar inside is listed by its members (`tar.lz`, `tar.lzo`), any other payload is one entry named like the upload without the compression suffix, with its size and CRC-32. Inspection runs in the web process and holds the payload in memory, so it decodes to at most 64 MiB (not the 500 MB of an unpack); past that the answer is the size-cap refusal of every other archive, and the conversion route is the place for such a file.
