The lzop reader adds up the block sizes first and decodes into one buffer of that size, so the payload is held once.
  - **Bounds on what the rpm reader takes from an untrusted header.** At most 65,535 index entries per header (the limit rpm applies, `HEADER_TAGS_MAX`); at most 64 MiB of header data (rpm accepts up to `HEADER_DATA_MAX`, 256 MiB less one byte), and a header longer than the bytes left in the file is refused before any of it is read; every value must lie wholly inside the header data and every string must end inside it; no table is read with more rows than the archive entry cap (50,000, the same `entry-count` refusal as for an archive with that many entries); every per-file table must have one value per name; the file table keeps each directory name and base name as views of the header and joins one path at a time when an entry is written, so a header that names one 4 KB directory for 50,000 files (ghosts never reach the byte budget) holds that directory once; a path is at most 4,096 bytes (the name limit rpm reads a cpio name with) and must start with a slash; the stripped stream is walked once in 64 KiB pieces with no recursion, an index may appear once, and everything written is charged against the package's byte budget as it is written, so a header of many long paths cannot grow past what a package may unpack to. Names are not rewritten: they go through the listing and the checks of every archive (traversal, absolute names, links, devices, depth, collisions) and the post-extraction walk.
- **Why**:
  - **Stripped payloads are what current rpm writes.** The published sample `hardlinks-ghosts-v6.rpm` (rpm 6) and every package with files over 4 GiB use it. Reading the names from the header is what rpm's own unpacker does, and the converted tree equals what it unpacks: the four files of the sample and the same files for the written packages (hard-link sets of two and three links with a ghost among them, a symbolic link, a directory, an empty file, an accented name, 32-bit and 64-bit sizes, gzip, xz, bzip2 and Zstandard payloads), compared file by file with `rpm2archive` and `tar` on Linux. Where rpm's own cpio writer (`rpm2cpio`) refuses a package over 4 GiB, this route does not need it.
  - **Alternate streams are not part of what a user means by the files of a volume.** They hold macOS bookkeeping (`com.apple.provenance` on every file of an image the disk utility wrote), and a converted archive extracted on another system would carry files named `report.pdf:com.apple.provenance`, which some systems refuse to create. 7-Zip's own documentation names `-sns-` for unpacking without alternate streams (and `-sns` for listing them), and its defaults are the opposite for the two commands (`l` hides them, `x` writes them), which is why the listing and the extraction disagreed. Counting them in a warning keeps the omission visible, as [9. Conversion warnings](#9-conversion-warnings) asks.
  - **Naming the volume or the method turns a guess into an action.** The earlier text asked for "the other parts"; the caller needs to know which file to add (`unrar` says `Cannot find volume <name>`, and 7-Zip prints `Missing volume : <name>` for the file on disk), and for a method the caller needs to know whether the archive is damaged or simply newer than the installed 7-Zip.
  - **Inspection should answer for every source the conversion accepts.** A caller who can convert a `.lz` file but gets "unrecognized archive format" when asking what is in it has no way to choose entries.
- **Trade-offs**:
  - Streams are dropped for every archive 7-Zip lists them in (HFS+ images, and NTFS streams in WIM or 7z files made on Windows), with no way to keep them; a caller who needs them gets the original image. A volume with thousands of attributes makes the listing longer, within the listing buffer bound.
  - A stripped payload is on disk several times at its peak (the package's own payload bytes, the decompressed stream, the rewritten cpio, then the extracted tree), all charged against the one budget of entry 41, so a package whose payload is a large part of the bound is refused as a bomb although each stage alone is under it.
  - The rewritten cpio carries owner 0 and the header's time; the owner and group names of the header are not carried (nothing in the targets uses them).
  - For a RAR 5 set renamed away from `.partN.rar`, and for any set uploaded without a file name, the message cannot name the volume.
  - A package whose header is damaged but whose payload 7-Zip lists as a plain cpio is not touched by this route; only a package that 7-Zip lists as empty is read from its header.
- **Alternatives rejected**:
  - **Linking a package library or running `rpm2archive` in the worker**: the library and the program are copyleft; the format is a few hundred lines of reading with every length bounded, and the programs run only in tests, as the oracle.
  - **Reading the stripped payload entry by entry without a cpio in between**: it would duplicate the vetting, hard-link handling and mode normalisation that the 7-Zip pipeline already has.
  - **Keeping alternate streams under their `<file>:<stream>` names**: the names are not valid on every file system, they would multiply the entry count of an image, and the earlier behaviour refused whole images over a name.
  - **An option to keep them**: no user asked for one, and every setting is a surface to document and test; the original image is the answer for the rare case.
  - **Deciding a stream from its name** (a colon after the first path segment): a real file may be named that way, and a rule that skips such names would be a way to hide an entry from the vetting.
  - **Naming the missing volume as 7-Zip does** (`Missing volume : input.r00`): the archive is staged under a generic name, so the name would be the stage's, not the caller's.
  - **Naming a method when the line does not name an entry of the listing**: the generic refusal stays for that case, because guessing which entry a line is about is how a wrong-password answer would be turned into a method answer.
- **Verified by**: `tests/rpm-stripped-payload.test.ts` (the published rpm 6 package to zip, tar, tar.gz and 7z against the contents worked out from `rpm -qp --dump`, the header table of the sample, packages written by `tests/helpers/rpm-builder.ts` with every payload compression and both size widths compared with `rpm2archive`, payload order, an empty package, entry selection, hostile names, devices, links, a ratio bomb, and the refusal for each bound above), `tests/archive-alternate-streams.test.ts` (a disk image written by the macOS disk utility with a volume named `A`, six streams counted from 7-Zip's own listing, the files equal to a 7-Zip extraction with `-sns-`, a plain `C:evil.txt` still refused), `tests/archive-missing-volume-and-method.test.ts` (the published first volume `rarfile-rar3-old.rar` against `unrar` and 7-Zip, both numberings, entries stored with an unknown method and a 7z with an unknown coder), `tests/archive-inspection-lzip-lzop.test.ts` (the real lzip and lzop fixtures, names compared with `lzip`, `lzop` and `tar`, a lzip bomb), `tests/archive-package-sources.test.ts` and `tests/archive-refusal-reasons.test.ts` (updated for the new answers).
- **Revisit when**: a user needs attributes or resource forks kept (they would go into the output as extended-attribute records of a tar), a package format other than rpm and deb with a payload 7-Zip cannot list is added, an rpm header larger than 64 MiB is found in a real package, or RAR 5 volume naming can be read from the archive.

## 57. Surround layouts: Opus channel mapping, a stereo mix for WMA, and the BS.775 mix of 5.1(side)

- **Status**: Implemented (issue #783, media rows), decided 2026-10-19 (QA request in issue #783: a real file converts, or is refused with a typed answer that names a real limit of the file; found while checking the `mts` rows of the nightly real-file run: `mts` to WebM and `mts` to WMA).
- **Decision**:
  - **A source whose surround channels sit at the sides is written to Opus with its surround channels at the rear, the pair of the same count.** Opus channel mapping family 1 (RFC 7845 section 5.1.1.2) fixes the channel order of mono, stereo, 3.0, quad, 5.0, 5.1, 6.1 and 7.1, which is the order of the Vorbis I specification, and its surround channels are the rear pair. FFmpeg's encoder opens exactly those layouts. AC-3, E-AC-3 and DTS decode to 5.1(side), so every such film track to `.opus`, `.ogg` with Opus, `.weba`, WebM or Matroska stopped with `Invalid channel layout 5.1(side) for specified mapping family -1` (FFmpeg 6.1.1 and 9.0.2). The transcode that stops with that line is started again with `channelmap=channel_layout=5.1` (5.0 for 5.0(side), quad for quad(side)) on that audio output stream only, so a file with a stereo commentary beside a 5.1 mix keeps both. The change moves no sample and mixes nothing: the decoded samples of the real file below are byte for byte the same with and without the relabelling. The written file states mapping family 1 with 4 streams, 2 of them coupled, and the channel mapping `[0, 4, 1, 2, 3, 5]` of the table for 6 channels. Other layouts the family does not list (2.1, 3.1, 4.0, 4.1, 6.0, hexagonal, 7.0, 6.1 with a rear pair, the wide 7.1 layouts) are not changed and stop with the encoder's own error.
  - **A source with more channels than WMA codes is written as stereo.** Windows Media Audio Standard, the only WMA FFmpeg encodes (`wmav2`), codes one or two channels, and the encoder stops with `too many channels: got 6, need 2 or fewer`. A 5.1 file converted to `.wma` with no channel count was refused with a client error (400) for a property the caller never set; the MP3 encoder takes the same source and gets stereo. WMA now gets the ITU-R BS.775-3 stereo mix: left = 0.4142 FL + 0.2929 FC + 0.2929 SL (the centre and the surround channels 3 dB down, the LFE left out, the sum of the row normalised to 1), right the same for the right side. The normalisation is stated (`aresample` with `rematrix_maxval=1.0`) because WMA takes floating-point samples, and for those FFmpeg leaves the mix unnormalised. A caller who names a count above two (`audio.channels: 6`, `audioChannels: "5.1"`) is still refused with the limit named, and so is AC-3 with a source wider than 5.1, where the mix depends on the layout and has no single standard matrix.
  - **The BS.775 mix a caller asks for (`audio.downmix: "itu-r-bs775"`) keeps the surround pair of a 5.1(side) source.** The expression named the rear pair (`BL`, `BR`), which an AC-3 source does not have, and FFmpeg's `pan` takes a name the input lacks as silence, so the downmix of every AC-3 5.1 track had no surround channel in it. The expression now names the channels by position (channels 4 and 5 are the surround pair of a 5.1 mix, at the rear or at the sides); the 7.1 expression names both pairs and is unchanged.
- **Why**:
  - **The channel order is the format.** A player reads the channel mapping of the file, not the label the encoder was given, so a relabelled 5.1(side) track plays its surround channels on the surround speakers, which is where the AC-3 source had them (the Vorbis specification has only one surround pair). A mix instead of a relabelling would have changed the audio.
  - **Measured on tones, one to a channel.** A 1 s file with a sine of its own in each of the six channels of a 5.1(side) layout (400, 600, 800, 80, 1000 and 1200 Hz at half of full scale), written to `.opus` and to Ogg Opus and decoded by a separate ffmpeg: every channel holds its own tone within 0.02 of 0.5 (the 128 kbit/s default; at 64 kbit/s two channels leak 0.09 into each other) and no channel holds another's tone within 30 dB. The real file `samples-00001-2.mts` (AC-3 5.1(side), 448 kbit/s) to WebM has each of its six channels within 1.5 dB of the source's level.
  - **Why not let FFmpeg choose the layout.** With `aformat` listing the eight layouts, FFmpeg chose the nearest by its own rules, and the rules differ by build: 5.1(side) became 5.1 on 6.1.1, 7.1.5 and 9.0.2, but 5.0(side) and quad(side) became 7 channels (a 6.1 layout) on 6.1.1 and the right layout on the others. A relabelling table gives the same file on every build.
  - **Stereo is what a WMA file of Standard profile holds, and the mix is the standard one.** Windows Media Audio Professional codes up to 7.1 and FFmpeg has no encoder for it, so there is no multichannel WMA to write. The MP3 path already brings a surround source to stereo with this matrix (0.4142 of the front channel, 0.2929 of centre and surround), so the same source has the same level in both. Through the float path without the normalisation the left channel came out at 0.483 of full scale for the half-scale test tone (the front channel at gain 1, +7.4 dB over the 0.207 the 16-bit path gives and over full scale for loud material); with it 0.201 after WMA coding, 0.26 dB under the 0.207 of the mix itself.
  - **The explicit downmix lost the surround channels of the most common 5.1 source.** With the half-scale tones the stereo output held 0.146 of the centre and none of the side channels, against 0.146 of each after the change; a viewer hears a film mix with the music and effects of the surrounds missing.
- **Trade-offs**:
  - **Layouts outside the three relabelled ones and the family's eight still fail** with the encoder's error (a page of log, as before), because the nearest listed layout would add or remove channels, which the caller did not ask for. They are rare in files.
  - **FFmpeg's decoder returns the five channels of an Opus 5.0 file in the order front left, front right, rear left, rear right, centre** (measured with FFmpeg 9.0.2, and the same for a native 5.0 source with no change of ours), so the test judges the written channel mapping for 5.0 and the decoded tones for 4 and 6 channels.
  - **A film track written to WMA loses its LFE and its surround separation.** That is what stereo is; a caller who needs more converts to another format.
  - **The Opus default bitrate is 128 kbit/s whatever the channel count**, about 21 kbit/s a channel for 5.1. It is not changed here; the tones above stay within 0.02 at that rate, music is not measured.
  - **The restart costs one failed start** (a few milliseconds) for each audio stream that needs the relabelling; a conversion with other layouts pays nothing.
- **Alternatives rejected**:
  - **`aformat` with the family's layouts on every Opus encode:** build-dependent choice (above), and a filter in every Opus graph.
  - **Mapping family 255 (channels with no defined meaning):** every layout encodes, but a player cannot place the channels, which is what family 1 is for.
  - **A stereo downmix of the surround source for Opus:** the format holds six channels; a downmix is for the caller to ask for.
  - **`-ac 2` for WMA:** the unnormalised float mix above.
  - **Refusing a 5.1 source for WMA (the previous behaviour):** the caller never asked for the count, the MP3 path converts the same file, and the refusal named a property of the encoder that no request option could have avoided except by guessing `audio.channels: 2`.
  - **Downmixing 7.1 to 5.1 for AC-3 the same way:** the mix of side and rear pairs has several conventions; the refusal names the limit and the request option that avoids it.
- **Verified by**: `tests/media-opus-channel-layout.test.ts` (WAV files written by the test in 5.1(side), 5.0(side), quad(side) and 5.1, a tone to a channel, to `.opus` and to Ogg with Opus: ffprobe's codec, channel count and layout, the OpusHead read from the file's bytes against the table of the reference encoder, and, for 4 and 6 channels, the decoded tone of each channel by a separate ffmpeg; an AC-3 Matroska file to WebM; a file with a mono track beside a 5.1(side) track; `samples-00001-2.mts` to WebM), `tests/media-surround-downmix.test.ts` (the tones through WMA against the BS.775 gains within 1.5 dB and the other side's and the LFE tone more than 25 dB down; `samples-00001-2.mts` to WMA as stereo, as long as the source and decoded without error; the explicit BS.775 downmix of a 5.1(side) file within 0.2 dB of the gains; the explicit request for six channels refused with the limit named), `tests/media-audio-targets.test.ts` (8 channels refused by AC-3, written as stereo by WMA, and the explicit counts), `tests/media-audio-subtitles-thumbnails.test.ts` (the pan expression), and runs on Ubuntu 24.04 (FFmpeg 6.1.1) and with FFmpeg 7.1.5 under `ORACLE_STRICT_MODE=1` and on macOS (FFmpeg 9.0.2), red on `main` before the change.
- **Revisit when**: a real file with a layout outside the three relabelled ones is refused; QA wants a multichannel default bitrate for Opus; AC-3 gets a stated mix for 7.1; FFmpeg's Opus encoder lists the side layouts (the restart then never triggers and the rule can go); or FFmpeg gains a WMA Professional encoder.

## 58. PDF page operations: when a page count is needed, loop checks beside the rewrite, and the measured floor of the rows

- **Status**: Implemented (issue #695, epic #759), decided 2026-10-20 (QA request). QA kept per-job network isolation and rejected a helper that holds one namespace for several programs (see the alternatives).
- **Decision**:
  - **Page deletion counts the pages of the document only for a specification that names page 1.** Any other specification leaves page 1 and so leaves a page, whatever the length of the document, so the second `qpdf --show-npages` run, which told an emptied document from a result, is not started. A specification that names page 1 (`1`, `1-3`, `-2`) is counted beside the edit as before and refused when it names every page.
  - **Compression asks whether the page tree loops beside the rewrite, not before it.** The rewrite (Ghostscript, or qpdf for `archive`) and `qpdf --show-pages` start together; only a document qpdf cannot list discards the first rewrite, mends the tree (entry 53) and rewrites. Ghostscript starts from the launcher (entry 31) like `qpdf` does.
  - **Watermark and merge give pdf-lib 500 objects between two yields to the event loop.** The defaults (100 objects read, 50 written) put a zero-delay timer, which Node holds for a millisecond, every few objects; the work between two yields is still a few milliseconds.
  - **Every program that handles user bytes keeps a network and a user namespace of its own** (`unshare -r -n` in front of each run), as before. Nothing about the isolation changes.
- **Why**:
  - **Compression.** The check of the page tree before the rewrite was a qpdf run of 2.35 ms in front of a Ghostscript run of 56 ms; beside the rewrite, ours / reference rose from 0.92 to 0.97 to 0.98 in the parity bench.
  - **Delete.** A second program costs 0.16 ms through the launcher (a `--show-npages` beside the edit: 2.77 against 2.61 ms) and 0.37 ms in the whole operation; in a loop of alternating single calls, skipping it took delete from 0.90 to 0.96 of the reference to 1.01. In the parity bench the row moves little (head against base 1.00 to 1.04): the wrapper below is most of its gap.
  - **Watermark.** The save of the stamped document took 1.6 ms of the 2.2 ms of the operation, about 1.3 ms of it waiting for a zero-delay timer (the font and its character map put the document past 50 objects, and the profile of the process is 63 percent idle); the operation takes 0.9 ms now (2.1 to 2.3 ms before). Head against base in the parity bench: 2.1 to 2.2; ours / reference 1.24 to 1.27 before and 2.6 to 2.7 now. Merging ten three-page files went from 8.2 to 5.5 ms.
  - **Where the other rows stand.** On the 4-core test VM (Ubuntu 24.04, Node 24.22, libuv 1.52.1) an extraction of a 20 KB, three-page PDF takes 2.3 to 2.5 ms, and the time goes to: the `unshare -r -n` in front of the program, 0.27 ms for a program that does nothing and 0.39 to 0.46 ms for `qpdf` (15 to 16 percent; 0.52 ms between the `execve` of `unshare` and the `execve` of `qpdf` in a trace); the fork of Node, a plain `clone(SIGCHLD)` that copies the page tables (`spawnSync` of `/bin/true` takes 0.35 ms at 44 MB resident, 0.84 at 160, 1.26 at 261 and 2.34 at 463, about 4.7 ms per gigabyte; libuv uses neither `vfork` nor `posix_spawn` on Linux, and no option of `spawn` changes the cost); and `qpdf` itself, whose start takes 1.26 ms of the 2.14 ms of an extraction (`qpdf --version`, which does nothing; it links 18 libraries, GnuTLS among them) against 0.85 ms of page work, which the reference command pays as well. The steps in the process are cheap by comparison: the header test and the encryption check 4.5 microseconds, a private directory with its input and its removal 14 to 42, the input file 12, the ZIP of three small parts 24, the resident size 2. The CI runner enables user namespaces, so its pdf-ops rows carry the wrapper: base ratios of 0.82 to 0.93 there, 0.73 to 0.96 on the test VM with the namespaces enabled.
  - **Parity bench of the family, head against base** (user namespaces enabled, 24 interleaved pairs per row and run, three runs of the head and two of the base; the test VM is shared, so the ranges are wide): protect 0.98 to 1.01, decrypt 1.0, unlock 0.98 to 1.03, split 0.99 to 1.01, extract 0.98 to 0.99, delete 1.00 to 1.04, reorder 0.97 to 1.00, rotate 0.96 to 0.99, compress 1.06 to 1.07, watermark 2.1 to 2.2, merge 0.90 to 1.01. Ours / reference:

    | Row | Base | Head |
    |---|---|---|
    | protect | 0.89 to 0.90 | 0.87 to 0.90 |
    | decrypt | 0.91 to 0.93 | 0.88 to 1.02 |
    | unlock | 0.92 to 0.96 | 0.85 to 0.95 |
    | split | 0.82 to 0.94 | 0.77 to 0.80 |
    | extract | 0.80 to 0.84 | 0.82 to 0.84 |
    | delete | 0.73 to 0.79 | 0.78 to 0.81 |
    | reorder | 0.79 to 0.83 | 0.76 to 0.82 |
    | rotate | 0.79 to 0.83 | 0.78 to 0.82 |
    | compress | 0.92 | 0.97 to 0.98 |
    | watermark | 1.24 to 1.27 | 2.6 to 2.7 |

  - **Output flags, measured on the extraction (time against ours, bytes):**

    | Flags | Time | Bytes |
    |---|---|---|
    | `--object-streams=generate --compress-streams=y` (ours and the reference) | 1.00 | 17,680 |
    | `--object-streams=preserve` or `disable` | 0.93 | 19,872 |
    | `generate` with `--stream-data=preserve` or `--compress-streams=n` | 0.95 | 19,457 |
    | `preserve` with `--stream-data=preserve`, or none of the flags | 0.93 to 0.94 | 19,872 |
    | ours with `--deterministic-id` | 1.02 | 17,680 |
    | ours with `--compression-level=1` | 1.00 | 17,742 |
    | ours with `--recompress-flate` | 1.32 | 17,680 |

    Packing the objects costs 0.18 ms (7 percent) and saves 11 percent of the bytes, and the bytes are a parity row of the same family.
  - **Ruled out by measurement.** Output to standard output or to a file: 2.271 against 2.275 ms. AES-256 (revision 6) costs 3.0 ms in `qpdf` on this file (encryption 4.9 ms, decryption 5.4 ms, a copy 1.9 ms) and AES-128 0.1 ms; the build in the distribution has the GnuTLS provider only (`QPDF_CRYPTO_PROVIDER=native` is refused), and the reference command derives the same key.
- **Trade-offs**:
  - **The rows of split, extract, delete, reorder, rotate, protect, decrypt and unlock stay below the reference** (0.77 to 1.0 in the table above) with the isolation as it is: the wrapper, the fork and the start of `qpdf` are what the reference does not pay or pays once. They stay in `bench/parity-gaps.json` with issue #695 and no tolerance is changed.
  - A deletion that names page 1 still runs a second program.
- **Alternatives rejected**:
  - **A launcher helper started under `unshare -r -n` that serves several programs from one namespace.** Measured: it removes the wrapper of each program and took head against base to 1.04 to 1.24 on these rows (ours / reference 0.85 to 0.96). QA rejected it: concurrent runs would share one loopback and one user namespace, and every program that handles user bytes keeps its own.
  - **`vfork` or `posix_spawn` from the worker**: libuv offers neither on Linux (entry 31).
  - **Skipping the loop check of protect and unlock.** The check cannot be dropped: `qpdf --encrypt` and `--decrypt` do not read the pages, and the encrypted copy cannot be read back without the password. A page-touching option that does not change the output does not exist; `--pages .` would rewrite the page tree of the document.
  - **Incremental update for rotate and protect.** The `qpdf` command line has no incremental writer (its help names none; `--replace-input` rewrites the whole file), and an appended update would keep the unpacked objects and the content of removed pages (entry 31).
  - **A page count read in process for deletion.** The packed object streams of a modern file hide `/Count`; reading them needs the inflater and a second page-tree walk next to `qpdf`'s own, to save a program in a case (a specification that names page 1) that is rare.
  - **No object streams, a lower compression level, `--stream-data=preserve`**: faster by 0.1 to 0.2 ms, 10 to 12 percent more bytes (table above).
- **Verified by**: `tests/pdf-ops-launcher.test.ts` (one program for a deletion that leaves page 1 and two for one that names it), `tests/pdf-delete-count-abort.test.ts`, `tests/pdf-page-ops.test.ts`, `tests/pdf-page-tree.test.ts` (compression of a ring on the Ghostscript profiles returns the page and its text, read by Poppler), `tests/pdf-compress.test.ts`, the quality rows of the `pdf-ops` family (`--quality-only`) and its speed rows against the base.
- **Revisit when**: a program start that costs less than the wrapper and the fork is available (a native job runner that gives each job its own namespaces, or libuv starting programs with `vfork` or `posix_spawn` on Linux); the CI runner stops allowing user namespaces; a specification that names page 1 becomes the usual deletion.

## 59. Pictures to PostScript: one document of every page, a page of its own size, compressed samples

- **Status**: Implemented (picture rows of issue #783, epic #760), decided 2026-10-20 (QA request in issue #783: output that is wrong or refused although the input is fine).
- **Decision**:
  - **A picture converted to PostScript (`ps`) is a PostScript document whose pages are the pages of the source.** A multi-page TIFF or HEIF sequence (or the pages a request selects) is one file with `%%Pages: n`, one `%%Page` per page, and not a ZIP of one file per page. An EPS is one figure and stays one file per page in a ZIP.
  - **Every page sets its size.** `<< /PageSize [w h] >> setpagedevice` with the size of the picture in points (entry 65: the stored print density, one pixel to the point when the picture states none); an EPS keeps its bounding box and calls no page device. Before, an interpreter drew the picture on its default page (612 x 792 points): a larger picture came out cut off, and the scan of the real-world corpus came out blank.
  - **The samples are Flate-compressed ASCII85** (`LanguageLevel 3`), not hexadecimal: three bytes of RGB were six characters, so one 3,307 x 4,677 page weighed 94 MB.
  - **The print density of the source is applied since entry 65** (a 400 dpi scan is an A4 page, as in the PDF it converts to). Before it, a page had the picture's pixel count as its size in points.
  - **A Word template whose body is empty converts to XPS** (`lo-tdf120761_zorder.dot`): no change was needed. The in-process reader refuses the document ("holds no text": its words are in a header and a frame), the dispatcher retries it through the office suite's plain text, which holds them, and the XPS carries the one line. The sample is pinned for `xps` with the suite's own PDF as the oracle.
- **Why**: the standard converters write one document of all pages for a multi-page TIFF (three pages, 11.8 MB for the corpus file; `tiff2ps` 3.9 MB), and a PostScript file is made to be printed as one job. A page device per page is what the structuring conventions ask for and what keeps a viewer or printer from guessing. The corpus file now converts to 175 KB, and each page drawn by the interpreter equals the page libtiff decodes, sample for sample.
- **Trade-offs**:
  - **Pages kept the picture's pixel count as their size in points** (3,307 x 4,677 points for an A4 scan) until entry 65, which reads the density (595 x 842 points for the scan, as `tiff2ps` writes it) for the PDF writer and this one alike.
  - **Level 3 interpreters only**: the Flate filter is Level 3 (1997). A Level 2 device cannot read the samples; a Level 2 file would be 5 to 6 times larger with run-length coding for photographs.
  - A page is converted as one document and its page section is moved into the joined file, so every page is in memory twice at the end; the aggregate page pixel budget of the multi-page rules bounds it.
  - Compression of a photograph costs about one second more for a 24 megapixel picture (2.5 s against 1.6 s) and halves the file (76 MB against 146 MB).
- **Alternatives rejected**:
  - **A ZIP of one PostScript file per page** (the previous output): a PostScript file is a document; the ZIP was declared as such and no consumer of the extension opens it.
  - **Gray and one-bit samples for scans**: the compression already removes the cost (the three pages are 175 KB), and a second sample layout is a second code path to verify.
  - **Passing the original CCITT data through** (what `tiff2ps` does for such a file): only for one compression and one photometric reading, and the page would no longer follow the resize, background and orientation options.
  - **Reading the density in this change**: it moves the PDF writer too, so it was decided apart (entry 65).
- **Verified by**: `tests/image-multipage-postscript.test.ts` (the three-page CCITT Group 4 scan of the corpus: the interpreter draws three pages of the scan's 3,307 x 4,677 pixels at its 400 dpi and every sample equals the page libtiff decodes, the pages differ from each other, the file is smaller than ImageMagick's; a request for pages 2 to 3; a picture larger than the default page, to `ps` and to `eps` drawn with the bounding box), `tests/image-tiff-tag-order.test.ts` (the two pages of the real TIFF in one document, the second equal to libtiff's decode; EPS still one file per page), `tests/image-postscript.test.ts` (ASCII85 against the specification's encoding, line length, every length of a short run, the refusals), and the existing EPS and PS pixel tests, which now read the Flate and ASCII85 filters with the reader of `tests/helpers/postscript-samples.ts`.
- **Revisit when**: a Level 2 consumer is reported, or a multi-page source with pages of unlike sizes needs a page device per size beyond what a document already holds.

## 60. qpdf jobs from a runner that holds libqpdf loaded, each in namespaces of its own

- **Status**: Implemented (issue #695, epic #759), decided 2026-10-20 (QA approval). It follows entry 58, which records the rows before it.
- **Decision**:
  - **The qpdf jobs of the PDF operations run in a child of `qpdf-runner`** (`native/qpdf-runner`, about 900 lines of C, built like `cad-convert` in the worker image and in CI) instead of starting the `qpdf` program. The runner opens the `libqpdf` of the distribution package once (`dlopen`; nothing of qpdf is linked in) and, for each job, forks a child that calls `qpdfjob_run_from_argv`, the public C entry point of the command line, with the arguments the program would get. The worker talks to it through a pipe (`src/lib/conversions/pdf-postprocess/qpdf-runner.ts`); the callers (`runQpdf`, the decryption for the readers, the edit gate) did not change except for the name of the function they call.
  - **Every job still has a user namespace and a network namespace of its own**, made in the child after the fork and before the library reads a byte of the document, with the mapping `unshare -r -n` makes. A child that cannot make them runs nothing and says so; the job then runs the way it always did (the `qpdf` program behind `unshare`), including the typed refusal of strict isolation. No child serves a second job, and no state is shared between jobs (see the security properties).
  - **The runner declines, and the usual way answers**, for a program that is not `qpdf`, a job that asks for no isolation or for namespaces beyond the user and network ones, a standard input past 32 KB or one its pipe cannot hold (a host over its budget of pipe memory gives a new pipe one page; the runner never waits for room in it), an output limit past 1 GiB, an `nproc` limit, a request past what the runner reads (a frame past 4 MiB, a string past 64 KB, more than 4096 arguments or environment entries, a NUL in a string, a working directory that is not absolute: the worker refuses these before it sends them), the 65th job at a time, and any frame it cannot take; and whenever it is off (`QPDF_RUNNER=off`), missing, not yet started (it starts in the background on first use, in 3 ms) or dead. A job that was in a runner that died is run the usual way.
  - **The results are the program's.** The same arguments give the same bytes on standard output and in files, the same exit status and the same text on standard error (so the matchers that read the loop sentence and the out-of-range message are unchanged). The one difference: for an argument qpdf does not know, the program prints a pointer to its help after the message and the library entry point does not; the message and the status are the same.
  - **Time limit, output cap, cancel, limits.** The runner kills the process group of a job at its time limit and when the output (standard output and error together) passes its cap, on a cancel from the worker, and with the runner itself (`PR_SET_PDEATHSIG`). Those apply to a job whose first process has exited as well: the first process stays unreaped (`waitid` with `WNOWAIT`) until the job is finished, so its group can still be signalled and its id is not reused, and a job whose streams something it started keeps open is ended a second after the first process, with that process's result, unless its limit or a cancel comes first. When a job ends the group is killed, so a process that closed the streams and stayed is not left behind; it applies the address-space, file-size and CPU limits of the request, and no core dump. The errors it reports are those of `executeSandboxedBinary` (`SandboxedProcessError` with the status, signal and text, `SandboxedTimeoutError`, `SandboxedBufferLimitError`, `SandboxedMemoryLimitError`).
- **Why**:
  - **Where the time went** (extraction of a 20 KB, three-page PDF, medians of 400 jobs on the 4-core test VM, the runner timing itself): the worker reaches the runner in 0.09 ms (the checks, the input file, the pipe), the runner parses and makes the pipes in 0.01 and forks in 0.05, the child starts in 0.07, closes descriptors in 0.01, makes its namespaces and maps in 0.11, applies limits, directory and environment in 0.002, runs the job in 0.80 (the page work: the program takes 0.85 ms of work and 1.26 ms to start), the runner sees the exit after 0.06, and the reply reaches the worker 0.07 later: 1.25 ms against 2.5 to 3.0 for the program started behind `unshare` and 2.5 for the reference command. A runner holds 6.8 MB and its resident size did not change over 300 jobs.
  - **Preloading more does not help.** Running a job inside the runner before it serves (to load pages and bind symbols) changed the extraction by less than the noise (1.23 to 1.32 ms without, 1.24 to 1.26 with), so the runner does not.
  - **Parity bench of the family** (user namespaces enabled, 24 interleaved pairs per row and run, three runs; the testbed is shared, so the ranges are wide). Ours / reference, and the head against the tree of entry 58 (two runs) and against the base of the change (one run), a ratio above 1 being the head faster:

    | Row | Ours / reference | Head / tree of entry 58 | Head / base |
    |---|---|---|---|
    | protect | 1.08 to 1.20 | 1.33 to 1.35 | 1.62 |
    | decrypt | 1.14 to 1.24 | 1.29 to 1.35 | 1.43 |
    | unlock | 1.02 to 1.26 | 1.25 to 1.27 | 1.29 |
    | split | 1.58 to 1.64 | 2.05 | 2.03 |
    | extract | 1.90 to 1.95 | 2.27 to 2.30 | 2.37 |
    | delete | 1.52 to 1.93 | 2.35 to 2.41 | 2.46 |
    | reorder | 1.69 to 1.83 | 2.37 to 2.43 | 2.47 |
    | rotate | 1.35 to 2.03 | 2.00 to 2.72 | 2.61 |
    | compress | 0.95 to 0.98 | 1.01 to 1.03 | 1.08 |
    | merge, watermark | above 2 | 0.99 to 1.02 | 0.98 to 2.8 |

    In a loop that times twenty calls of one side and then twenty of the other, as the bench does, with the reference command beside it: protect 1.26, decrypt 1.26, unlock 1.16, split 1.90, extract 2.08, delete 2.23, reorder 2.13, rotate 2.23 (the tree of entry 58 measured 0.79 to 0.98 there).
  - **Compression stays just under the reference** (0.95 to 0.98): 56 of its 58 ms are Ghostscript, which the runner does not start (it stays behind the wrappers it has had); the qpdf runs beside it are the part that got faster.
  - **Same bytes, 79 PDFs.** Ten argument sets of the operations (page extraction, rotation, lossless recompression, `--show-npages`, `--show-pages`, `--check`, the JSON export of the objects and of the encryption, decryption, a page selection that empties the file) plus a split into one file per page, on the 79 PDFs of the public pdf-ops corpus and the repository, each run by the program and by the runner with `--deterministic-id`: 869 runs, 60,285,951 bytes of output and 1,143 split files compared, no difference in a byte, a status or a message; 47 runs failed (damaged, encrypted or empty files) and failed alike. The quality gate of the family (`--quality-only`, full corpus, runner on): 2,197 rows at or above the reference, none failing.
- **Security properties** (each is read from the job's own process or from outside in `tests/qpdf-runner.test.ts` with a stand-in library, not taken from the runner):
  - **Namespaces per job.** Three concurrent jobs are in three different user namespaces and three different network namespaces, none of them the worker's or the runner's; a job sees the loopback alone and the mapping `0 <user> 1` with `setgroups` denied.
  - **Fail closed.** With a filter that denies `unshare` on the runner, no job runs: a job that would create a marker file creates none, the runner answers `setup failed`, and the program runs the job with its own sandbox.
  - **The seccomp filter is the container's and nothing else.** A child inherits the filter, the capabilities and the limits of the runner; a job under a filter that denies one call is denied it, and a job under none shows no filter. The calls the runner and its children make (`unshare` with the user and network flags, `clone` without namespace flags, `close_range`, `prctl`, `setsid`, `signalfd4`, `mremap`, `madvise`, `pipe2`, `setrlimit`, `wait4`, `poll`, `kill`) are allowed by `docker/seccomp-worker.json`, which denies `ptrace` and `process_vm_readv` and answers `clone3` with `ENOSYS`; the runner adds no filter and removes none. (Not run inside Docker here: the container check, `scripts/verify-worker-container.sh` check 8, runs `qpdf-runner --self-test` in the image.)
  - **Nothing is shared between jobs.** A child is forked for one job and exits with it. The buffers that hold requests, outputs and the data in flight are mapped `MADV_DONTFORK`, so no child holds a copy; the argument block of a job (paths, passwords) is wiped when its child exists and each frame is wiped when it is handled. A scan of a running job's whole memory finds none of another job's secret (a control that holds the secret twice is found), a job cannot open the memory of a sibling or of the runner through `/proc/<pid>/mem`, and the runner and every child are not dumpable. A child is the process group leader of a session of its own, so a kill reaches everything it started.
  - **The random numbers are not shared.** Six encryptions of one document by six children of one runner give six different outputs and six different key strings: the library does not repeat its random numbers after the fork.
  - **Bounds.** Time limit (a sleeping job is killed at 300 ms and its process is gone), output cap, file-size limit (`SIGXFSZ` becomes the buffer-limit error), cancel (the process is dead when the caller hears of it), 64 jobs at a time, a frame of at most 4 MiB, strings of at most 64 KB, a standard input of at most 32 KB that is written without blocking into a pipe whose capacity was checked first; the pipes keep the size the system gives them (at most 256 KiB of pipe memory per job, 16 MiB at 64 jobs, of the 64 MiB a user may hold before new pipes shrink; 256 KiB pipes measured the same for 50 MB of output, 49.7 to 51.4 ms). A request it cannot take, a frame of a type it does not know and a frame past 4 MiB (dropped as it arrives) are answered `rejected` and the frames after them are served; only a length too short to hold a type and an id ends the runner (status 2), since nothing then says where the next frame starts. The runner ends with its input and takes its jobs with it; a killed runner takes them too.
  - **Checked by tools.** `gcc -fanalyzer` reports nothing; 3,000 runs of mutated and random frames unprivileged and 12,000 as root against a build with AddressSanitizer and UBSan ended with status 0 or 2 every time, with no report and no hang (`native/qpdf-runner/fuzz-frames.py`); the differential tests pass against that build.
  - **What stays as it was.** The runner is an ordinary process of the worker's user, as the launcher helper of entry 31 is: it holds in-flight requests and outputs, and a process of the same user that could read its memory is not stopped by a namespace (it is stopped by the dumpable flag and by the container's `ptrace` rules). The library's own initialisers (GnuTLS) run in the runner, on no document. A library that starts a thread at load would make `unshare` fail in the children, and then every job runs the program.
- **Trade-offs**:
  - **A native program in the image and in CI** (a C compiler in one build stage and in the CI image; the check is `qpdf-runner --check`), and a second path for the same job. Both are tested against each other, and `QPDF_RUNNER=off` is the switch.
  - **The library must match.** The runner tries `libqpdf.so.34` down to `.27`; `qpdfjob_run_from_argv` is part of the library's C API from version 10.6. The version it loaded is reported and the differential test compares it with `qpdf --version`.
  - **Stderr banner for a usage error** (above), a standard input of at most 32 KB (the passwords and argument files the product sends are a few bytes), 64 jobs at a time (the worker runs a handful).
  - **Compression stays 2 to 5 percent under the reference**, bound by Ghostscript.
- **Alternatives rejected**:
  - **A helper that serves several programs from one namespace** (entry 58): QA kept one namespace per job.
  - **Keeping the Node helper of entry 31 and removing its wrapper.** The helper cost 0.08 to 0.14 ms per program in wake-ups and held 50 MB; the runner needs none of it for qpdf.
  - **Children kept warm and reused.** A child that has read one document holds it in memory; every job gets a fresh fork.
  - **A thread or a worker thread per job.** A thread cannot make a user namespace for itself and shares the memory of the worker.
  - **qpdf compiled to WebAssembly in the worker, a static build of the program**: a dependency or a build outside the pinned engine set (entry 31), and the first does not isolate.
  - **Linking libqpdf and calling the C++ interface.** It would tie the build to the development files and the compiler ABI of each release; the C entry point is the stable surface.
- **Verified by**: `tests/qpdf-runner.test.ts` (namespaces, environment, limits, descriptors, session, seccomp inheritance, no memory of other jobs, time, output and file-size limits, cancel, 40 overlapping jobs, runner stop and kill, declined jobs, the denied `unshare`, a job that leaves a descendant holding its streams (ended at its limit, on a cancel and after the grace) or one that closed them (killed when the job ends), a standard input its pipe cannot hold, the limits of the worker's frames and a NUL, frames the runner cannot read or must skip, a reply of 16 MiB read in 64 KiB chunks and 40 rounds of random bytes), `tests/qpdf-runner-qpdf.test.ts` (the real library against the program on every PDF of the repository, files of a split, decryption with passwords on standard input, unreadable files, the loop sentence, the out-of-range message, different encryptions per job, the self test, and the PDF operations of the product read back by `qpdf --check`, `pdfinfo` and `pdftotext`), `scripts/verify-worker-container.sh` (check 8), and the `pdf-ops` speed and quality rows.
- **Revisit when**: the container check fails on a host (the runner is then off there, and the rows return to entry 58); a libqpdf release changes the entry point; Ghostscript jobs are worth the same treatment (a runner mode that forks, makes the namespaces and executes the program would remove the two wrappers of a 56 ms run); a document-model path starts qpdf with a standard input past 32 KB.

## 61. PDF streams that declare more than a stream may decode: JBIG2 pages, and Poppler output past its limit

- **Status**: Implemented (issue #783, epic #763), decided 2026-10-20 (QA request: a PDF bomb ends quickly in a typed, named limit refusal, never in an untyped failure after tens of seconds; found by the nightly real-file run 39316523318).
- **Decision**:
  - **A PDF with a JBIG2 stream that declares a page decoding to more than 64 MiB is refused with a 413 before any Poppler tool starts.** The page size is read from the first bytes of the stream (the page information segment, ITU-T T.88 section 7.4.8: width and height, 32-bit each) and the decoded size is ceil(width / 8) bytes a row; the limit is the one every other decoded stream has (`MAX_STREAM_INFLATE_BYTES`). The answer is a `DecompressionLimitError` that names the object, the declared page, the decoded size and the limit. It applies to text, page-image, SVG, PostScript and OCR page rendering, which all read the file with Poppler. An A3 page scanned at 1,200 dpi is 35 MB and passes.
  - **Poppler output past the sandbox's output limit is a 413 as well.** The tools write their result to files, so what fills the pipe is diagnostics; a run that prints without end is one that reads a stream decoding to far more than a page holds. It is a `PayloadLimitError` (413), not the sandbox's untyped error (which was a 500). This covers the streams the size check cannot read.
- **Why**: `bench/realworld` file `pdf-pdfjs-bomb_giant.pdf` (123 KB, three JBIG2 content streams that declare 100,000 x 100,000 pixels) ended 24 s (Apple silicon) to 44 s (CI runner) after the start with `SandboxedBufferLimitError`: Poppler decodes each stream to 1.25 GB, reads the bytes as page-content operators and prints one diagnostic per token (5.9 GB of stderr in 120 s). Nothing in the file said so but the first 30 bytes of each stream, and the in-process reader already refuses a stream that would decode past its budget with the same 413, so the answer of the native route now matches it. A minimal derived file (one 30-byte stream) shows the same behaviour.
- **Trade-offs**:
  - **Only a stream whose first filter is JBIG2Decode is read.** Behind a wrapper (`ASCIIHexDecode`, `FlateDecode`) the segments are not in the file as they are; such a bomb still ends in the output limit, now as a 413 (about 1 s for the hex-wrapped form), not as a quick size refusal.
  - **A PDF that is encrypted with an empty user password** shows ciphertext, so its JBIG2 streams are not read; a request with a password is read after decryption.
  - The file is read once more from the job directory (the search for the filter name is a byte scan; a PDF with no JBIG2 stream costs a read and a scan). The search is one forward pass whatever the number of names (a 100 MB file made of the name alone takes 3 ms, a 2 MB one took 2.7 s with the first version, which searched forward and back from every name), and looks at 4,096 names at most; beyond them the output limit above answers.
  - An image of a very large JBIG2 page (above 64 MiB decoded, 536 megapixels) is refused although Poppler could draw it.
- **Alternatives rejected**:
  - **`pdftotext -q`**: it silences the messages that tell a wrong password and a damaged file from a good one, and the decode (23 s here) is still paid.
  - **A smaller output limit for Poppler alone**: the diagnostics start only after the 1.25 GB decode, so the run is not shorter.
  - **A time limit alone**: the run ends after 45 s in a `CpuTaskTimeoutError`; the size is known after 30 bytes.
  - **Listing the file as a known failure of the real-file run**: the product's answer was wrong, not the evidence.
- **Verified by**: `tests/pdf-jbig2-bomb.test.ts` (the page size read from the header bytes of the real file, 100,000 x 100,000, against T.88 and the arithmetic of the row; a scanned A4 page and a page behind a segment before it; PDFs made of a 30-byte stream refused for text and page images within 5 s; the real file; the hex-wrapped form ending in the typed output limit; files of 2 and 20 MB made of the filter name, alone or with a stream keyword or an object each time, read in a bounded number of bytes and names). Of the 1,260 PDFs in the corpus cache, 9 name JBIG2; the guard refuses only the bomb, reads the pages of seven scans (0.3 to 1.1 MB decoded each) and finds no size in the one that is encrypted.
- **Revisit when**: the size of a JBIG2 page is judged with the other limits of a request in one place (page area times pages), a wrapper filter must be read by the guard, or Poppler gets a decode limit of its own.

## 62. PDF text threads: a warm replacement before the old thread is retired

- **Status**: Implemented (issue #672, epic #759), decided 2026-10-20 (QA request); extended the same day by the follow-up on the work of a thread's start (the "follow-up" items below). The thread lifetime of 100 documents is unchanged and stays: QA kept per-job isolation (decision of 2026-10-19) and rejected a longer lifetime (see the alternatives).
- **Decision**:
  - **A text thread that has read 80 documents starts its replacement.** The replacement is a new thread that reads one fixed page written for this purpose (`pdf-text-warmup.ts`: three lines in two standard fonts), which loads the reader library and its font data, and then reports that it is ready. It takes no document before that, and then joins the pool behind the idle threads.
  - **The old thread is retired when it has read 100 documents and the replacement is ready.** If the replacement is still starting at 100, the old thread reads on; it is retired at 200 documents at the latest, or at 100 when no replacement could be started (the next document then starts a thread, as before).
  - **A replacement that is still starting does not count against the pool limit** and is stopped with the pool (`shutdownPdfTextThreads`).
  - **The speed jobs time the bundle the service runs.** The service runs the text thread from `dist/pdf-text-worker.js` (`build:worker` in `Dockerfile.worker` runs `build:pdf-text-worker`); with no bundle the thread runs the TypeScript source through the loader, which transpiles every module at every thread start. The `parity speed` job of a pull request and the nightly `bench-parity-speed` and `bench-ab-speed` jobs run `npm run build:pdf-text-worker` for the checkout under test and for the base (when the base has the script) before any row is timed.
  - **A thread that runs the bundle starts with no `execArgv` of its parent.** A thread inherits the `--import` of its parent, and under the loader of a benchmark or development process the bundle's own imports went through it: the first document took 450 ms instead of 125 ms. The service has no such flag, so nothing changes there.
  - **A new thread collects its old generation first at 128 MB** (`--initial-old-space-size=128`; the heap limit of 1 GB is unchanged). V8 takes the first threshold from the heap limit; at 1 GB a thread did a full collection every 23 documents. The flag is read when a thread's heap is made and is process-wide, so the host sets it only from before a text thread is created until that thread is online (its heap exists) and then sets `--initial-old-space-size=0`, which gives V8's computed size again. Threads of other modules (the CPU pool, the RAW decoder) started after that have the default; one started in the few milliseconds while a text thread starts shares the 128 MB, which is harmless. Overlapping starts keep it set until the last one is online, and a thread that fails or ends before it is online gives it back.
  - **A new thread loads pdf-lib when it reads the first page with images** (follow-up), not at start-up. Only the reader of the images in a document (`pdf-text-images.ts`) uses it, and loading it costs 27 ms of a new thread's start. The replacement of a thread that has read a document asking for images loads it before it reports ready (`workerData.images`, `preloadImageReader()`), so a conversion that needs images (DOCX, HTML, Markdown) does not wait for it. The native image library (sharp, 11 ms) still loads with the thread: `image-input-limits.ts` imports it and is shared by every image conversion, so it would need splitting.
  - **The isolation does not change.** The replacement has read no document of a user, and the old thread is retired as before, so everything a document left in the old thread ends with it. Documents still share a thread with at most 99 (up to 199) others, as before.
- **Why**:
  - **The variance of the row was one event.** A thread is retired after 100 documents, and the first document after that waited for a new thread: 210 ms (source mode, as the benchmark runs) or 125 ms (compiled worker) against 4 to 5 ms for a document. The row reads seven documents per call, so every 14th call took 280 ms instead of 35 ms. Over 100 calls on the 4-core Linux VM: mean 52 ms, median 34 ms, 7 calls over 100 ms (all of 210 to 260 ms above the rest), coefficient of variation 1.16; the calls without that start had a coefficient of variation of 0.23 and the 21 calls after it were 12 ms slower. The reference (one process per document) had 0.03 to 0.08.
  - **Starting the thread ahead hides that wait.** Same calls with the replacement: no call over 100 ms (largest 79 to 95 ms), mean 40 ms, median 37 ms. On the benchmark row (24 to 102 interleaved pairs, three runs each) the coefficient of variation of ours fell from 0.47 to 0.50 to 0.13 to 0.14, and the lower end of the interval of the speed ratio against the reference rose from 0.33 to 0.34 to 0.70 to 0.72 (median ratio 0.73 to 0.74 and 0.75 to 0.76; head against base 1.00 to 1.04).
  - **The time of a document, where it goes** (thread of 2,000 documents, 3.77 ms per document): reading the text 2.03 ms (54 percent), reading the operator list and scanning it 1.23 ms (33 percent), the bounded look at the streams before the reader 0.18 ms, opening the document 0.25 ms, closing it 0.05 ms. Reading the text and the operator list is 87 percent of the time. A thread that has read more than 120 documents reads a document of the row in 3.5 ms, the same as the reference's process (3.8 ms), a thread of its first 20 documents in 8 ms, and one of 20 to 80 documents in 4.0 to 4.6 ms.
  - **Follow-up: where a replacement's start goes, by phase** (the bundle, a 4-core Linux VM, Node 24, median of 15 to 25 starts, from `new Worker` to the ready message): 116 to 125 ms, made of the thread itself to its first line 11 ms; the bundle's own requires 52 ms (pdf-lib 27.5, sharp 10.8, Node's modules 0.4, the rest the bundle's code); loading the reader 25 to 28 ms (the native canvas module it requires takes 1.3 of them); opening the first document 30 to 38 ms (the reader's worker module and the standard font data); reading its page 10 to 12 ms (text 7.3 to 8.3, operator list 2.9 to 3.1); the stream check 1.0; closing 0.5. With pdf-lib loaded by the first page with images: 74 to 87 ms. The reading thread's CPU is the same figure, so the saving is CPU, not waiting.
  - **Follow-up: a document of a thread, by its position on the thread** (replacement threads of 700 to 2,100 documents, mean thread time per document): positions 1 to 5: 12.2 to 16.0 ms; 6 to 10: 7.0 to 10.1; 11 to 20: 5.1 to 6.5; 21 to 100: 4.1 to 5.0. The excess over the steady 4.1 to 5.0 ms is about 90 ms per thread, in about four of the 14 calls of a row, which is where most of the coefficient of variation of the row comes from. In the first documents the time is in the reader's text and operator-list code (getTextContent 13.7 ms against 2.8 steady, the operator list 6.8 against 1.6), not in opening or closing the document.
  - **Follow-up: what warms a replacement** (experiments, not shipped; 286 calls of the row per run, coefficient of variation of the calls; the host varied from run to run, so the figures of one comparison are from runs taken alternately): the standard-font warm-up page read 1, 15 or 40 times: 0.33 to 0.37, unchanged (the first five documents stay at 14.3 to 16.0 ms); the seven benchmark documents read once: 0.23 to 0.24 in three runs of three, against 0.28 to 0.35 without; a generated document of three pages with an embedded font program read eight times: 0.21 against 0.28 in a quiet pair of runs, and in six alternating runs 0.286 against 0.313 (lower in five of six), median call 43.8 against 45.3 ms, slowest call 97 against 107 ms. The reader's code is warmed by reading documents that carry font programs, which the standard-font page does not.
  - **Follow-up: the row on the VM is not moved by the experiments above**: the parity speed row, interleaved against the base, three runs of the code with the generated warm-up and the code cache: head against base 1.019, 0.989 and 1.014 (no change in speed), ratio against the reference 0.70 to 0.76, coefficient of variation of ours 0.19, 0.16 and 0.12 (0.21 before). The nightly run of 2026-10-20 on the CI runner, which includes the first version, measured the row at 1.20 [0.99, 1.38] over 15 pairs with a coefficient of variation of 0.164 (the reference 0.079).
- **Trade-offs**:
  - **The row stays below the reference.** Timed as the service runs it (the bundle, no inherited `execArgv`, the 128 MB threshold) the ratio is 0.83 to 0.85 (interval 0.76 to 0.87, three runs of 54 to 60 pairs), against 0.64 to 0.67 for the same code with the 1 GB default threshold and 0.75 to 0.76 for the source through the loader. The coefficient of variation of ours is 0.18 to 0.19, above the 0.15 aim: the thread is faster now, and the first version put the cause in the 100 to 125 ms in which a replacement starts (loading the reader takes 105 ms, 78 ms of it the native canvas module the reader requires without using it for text). The follow-up measured both claims and corrects them: the native canvas module takes 1.3 ms in a new thread, and the thread that reads is not slowed while the replacement starts (4.09 ms per document at positions 80 to 100 of a thread, 4.16 at 40 to 80, on the 4-core VM). What is slow is the first documents of the new thread, which are read by cold code (see the follow-up measurements). Each thread lives only 100 documents, so about 20 percent of the documents are read in the first 20 documents of a thread.
  - **Up to 48 MB more resident memory per thread**: peak resident memory of a process reading 300 documents on one thread at a time was 310 MB with the threshold and 262 MB without (333 MB at 256 MB). The flag is a threshold, not a limit. Measured through the host with the bundle (700 documents, the first 100 left out, 4 runs each, on a VM under a load of 25 from other jobs, so the figures are high): mean 7.57 to 7.68 ms per document without the threshold and 5.93 to 6.31 with it (median 5.85 to 6.12 against 4.52 to 4.89); peak resident memory of the process 323 to 338 MB without and 382 to 399 MB with it (about 60 MB for the two threads that overlap at a hand-over).
  - **Up to two threads per pool thread exist for about 20 documents of each 100**, one of them idle, each with the reader library and its heap loaded.
  - **A replacement that sits unused for 15 seconds is stopped**, and started again by the next document past 80, so a pool with under one document per second starts a thread every 15 seconds between its 80th and 100th document.
- **Alternatives rejected**:
  - **A longer warm-up** (one to 80 reads of the fixed page; two or six rounds of the benchmark's own documents): no change in the median call (35.6 to 38.9 ms against 35.3 to 36.9 ms for one read); the benchmark documents lowered the first 20 documents from 8.0 to 5.7 ms and lengthened the start by as much.
  - **Why the bundle was slower than the source.** The same reader file ran 4.55 ms per document in the bundle and 4.15 ms from the source (three workers of 160 documents; the profile is 15 percent slower in every function of the reader, with no hot spot). The bundle's threads did 13 full collections in 300 documents and the source's 6, and the threshold above brings the bundle to 3.87 ms. Heap limit of the thread against ms per document (bundle): 256 MB 4.97, 1 GB 4.70, 4 GB 3.97; young generation limit of 16 to 64 MB: no change. Other causes checked and ruled out:
    - **A different build of the reader.** A trace of the files opened shows both modes read `legacy/build/pdf.mjs` and `legacy/build/pdf.worker.mjs` of the same package (the source path opens `pdf.mjs` twice, once more for the loader).
    - **The bundler lowering private members.** The bundle is built with `--packages=external`, so the reader is not in it: it holds only this repository's code (209 KB), which has no `#private` member, and a search for the lowering helpers (`__privateGet`, `__privateAdd`) finds none with `node24`, `esnext`, `es2017` or `es2015` as the target; its one `WeakMap` is a table of glyph names. The lowering of private members to weak collections, which costs garbage-collector time when many instances exist, therefore does not apply.
    - **The target and the syntax form.** On a quiet VM `node24`, `es2017` and `es2022` gave 4.4 to 4.6 ms per document, and a minified bundle 4.56; the same set (with `esnext` and `es2015`) measured again while another job loaded the VM (load 6 to 8) gave 5.2 to 8.1 ms in a spread wider than any difference between the targets, so those runs rank none of them.
    - **Also checked:** a transformed copy of the reader, `require` instead of `import()`, a zero stack trace limit, an eval or a file entry.
  - **A heap limit of 4 GB for the thread:** the same speed-up, but the limit is what stops a hostile document from taking the process's memory.
  - **The flag in `NODE_OPTIONS`:** V8 refuses it there.
  - **Not loading the native canvas module in a new thread** (the first version counted 78 ms of a 125 ms start for it): the reader requires it when its Node utilities are evaluated, before any option is read, so it would take a hook on the module loader of every thread (`module.registerHooks`, whose hooks a thread does not inherit). Measured in the follow-up with the hook in place: the module takes 1.3 ms in a new thread and a start is 115.9 ms with the hook against 114.7 ms without, so the 78 ms were not the module. Not done.
  - **Reader and engine settings:** the baseline compiler for every function, no lazy feedback allocation, a 16 MB minimum young generation, no lazy compilation, a single GC thread: 35.3 to 36.3 ms against 35.3 ms, and 38.5 ms for the single GC thread.
  - **The on-disk compile cache of Node** (`NODE_COMPILE_CACHE`, or `module.enableCompileCache(directory)` in the thread; measured in the first version and again in the follow-up): the first document of a new thread 100 ms instead of 130 ms, and a replacement's start 60 to 74 ms instead of 86 (with pdf-lib lazy), 25 ms of CPU, nothing for the following documents. It puts a directory whose content is executed code, written by the worker, among the files the service has to protect (the engine checks the cache by the source's hash and its own version, not by a signature). That trade is not worth 25 ms of a start that does not move the row. Not shipped.
  - **A startup snapshot of the thread, or a code cache of the bundle by `vm.Script`** (follow-up): Node's documentation describes a snapshot as the state a whole process is started from (`--snapshot-blob`, with an entry built into it), and nothing in the options of a worker thread names one, so a thread cannot start from it. A `cachedData` for the bundle covers only this repository's 209 KB of code; the reader is loaded by the module loader, which the compile cache covers for every module without code of ours.
  - **Reader options** (follow-up): `isOffscreenCanvasSupported` (false in Node by default), `disableFontFace` (true in Node) and `useWorkerFetch` (false) are documented defaults that the thread already has, `standardFontDataUrl` is set, and a canvas factory is only used for drawing. None of them stops the module the reader requires when it is evaluated.
  - **Warming a replacement with the benchmark's own documents** (follow-up): coefficient of variation 0.23 to 0.24 against 0.28 to 0.35 (see above), but those files are the documents the row times and the fixtures of the tests, and shipping them in the service to warm its threads would tune the service to the benchmark. Not shipped.
  - **A generated warm-up document with an embedded font program** (follow-up; a TrueType writer of about 165 lines and a three-page document read eight times, the replacement started after 60 documents instead of 80): the coefficient of variation of the calls fell by about 9 percent (0.313 to 0.286 over six alternating runs, lower in five), the median call by 3 percent, and the row against the base did not change (1.019, 0.989, 1.014). That is within the noise of the VM, and a font generator and an earlier, longer-lived replacement (idle restarts in a pool that takes under 2.7 documents per second) are not worth it. Not shipped.
  - **Reusing the thread and reloading the reader's modules** to drop their state: the heap, the realm and every global the reader changed would stay shared, which is less than a new thread gives.
  - **More documents per thread, or no retirement by count.** Lifetimes of 300 and 1,000 documents measured 0.86 to 0.88 (coefficient of variation 0.13 to 0.15) and 0.91 to 0.94 (0.08 to 0.09) against the reference when timed from the source. QA rejected it: the lifetime bounds what a defect of the reader can accumulate across documents of different users.
  - **Timing the bundle with the loader of the benchmark process inherited by the thread:** the row reads 0.70 to 0.72 with a coefficient of variation of 0.17 to 0.20, because every thread start costs 450 ms; the service never runs that way.
  - **Starting the replacement when the pool is idle:** the pool has no way to know when the next document comes, and the benchmark's reference runs are the idle time.
- **Sources** (fetched on 2026-10-20 unless marked):
  - The bundler's documentation of content types, "Private member performance": the transform of `#name` members uses weak collections, engines may not perform well with large ones, and many instances of such classes may cost the garbage collector. It applies to code the bundler transforms below the syntax level; the reader is not bundled here.
  - The engine's post on faster class features: initialising class fields, private ones included, was much slower than ordinary assignment until v9.7 made it go through inline caches. Node 24 has a newer engine.
  - The reader's project discussion of its modern and legacy builds (discussion 17989): the legacy build is the one for runtimes that lack newer language features, and the one the package documents for Node. The code imports the legacy build in both modes.
  - The documentation of worker threads: `resourceLimits` bound the engine's heap only, not buffers; V8 options are not accepted in `execArgv`, and an explicit `execArgv`, an empty one included, replaces the inheritance from the parent.
  - The documentation of the module compile cache: the cache is written when the process is about to exit, from the compiled code, into a directory.
  - The post on the engine's baseline compiler: it lets the engine tier up much more eagerly for short-lived code (the flag that compiles every function with it is not named there).
  - Barrett, Bolz-Tereick, Killick, Mount and Tratt, "Virtual Machine Warmup Blows Hot and Cold", OOPSLA 2017 (arXiv 1602.00602): benchmarks on common JIT-compiled VMs often fail to reach a steady peak, so warm-up cannot be assumed to end.
  - The documentation of Node's modules (fetched 2026-10-20): `module.enableCompileCache(directory)` persists the engine's code cache of CommonJS and ES modules in a directory, affects the current instance only (a worker thread calls it itself or inherits `NODE_COMPILE_CACHE`), and writes entries when the instance is about to exit; caches are not reusable across Node versions. `module.registerHooks` runs synchronous hooks in the thread that loads the modules, and they are not inherited by worker threads unless registered through a preloaded file.
  - The documentation of Node's command line and of its `v8` module (fetched 2026-10-20): `--build-snapshot` and `--snapshot-blob` build and start a process from a snapshot of an entry script, with a single entry point and a subset of built-in modules that is known to serialise; nothing is said of worker threads. `v8.setFlagsFromString` after the engine has started "may result in unpredictable behavior".
  - The reader's API documentation (fetched 2026-10-20), document parameters: `isOffscreenCanvasSupported` (false in Node), `disableFontFace` (true in Node; `useSystemFonts` is forced off with it), `useWorkerFetch` (false in Node), `standardFontDataUrl`, and the canvas factory. The reader's own source for Node (the package's `legacy/build/pdf.mjs`, read on 2026-10-20): the block `if (isNodeJS)` requires the optional canvas package when the module is evaluated, before it tests `globalThis.DOMMatrix` and `Path2D`, so no document option can skip it. The reader's issue 21720 (closed 2026-08-06) records that the import fails when that optional package is absent.
  - Not verified: that the reference tool extracts text without a rendering device (its source could not be fetched).
  - Not verified: the practice of restarting a pooled worker after a number of requests, with a random spread so that the workers do not restart together, which process managers for web services document; the page could not be fetched.
- **Verified by**: `tests/pdf-text-host.test.ts` (the replacement starts after 80 documents with the warm-up request, takes no document while it starts, is given the next document after the old thread is retired, the old thread is retired at 200 when the replacement never reports, at 100 when none could be started, and a starting replacement is stopped with the pool), `tests/pdf-text-host-replacement.test.ts` (on real threads: 200 documents read the same text across the hand-over, two threads were started, the first one has ended; the warm-up page is read by `pdftotext` and by the reader line by line, the same as its source), `tests/pdf-text-thread-start.test.ts` (on real threads: pdf-lib is not loaded after a text-only document and is loaded by the first page with images; a replacement told to expect images has it loaded when it reports ready and one that is not told does not), `tests/pdf-text-host.test.ts` (the replacement is told whether the old thread has read a document that asks for images), `tests/ci-parity-workflow.test.ts` (the bundle is built for the checkout and for the base before the speed rows of a pull request and of the nightly jobs, and `build:worker` builds it for the image), `tests/pdf-text-host.test.ts` (the bundle starts with an empty `execArgv`, the source keeps the loader it registers; the old-generation threshold is set before each thread is made, given back when the thread is online, fails or ends, kept while another is still starting, and the heap limit stays 1 GB), `tests/pdf-text-host-threshold.test.ts` (on real threads: a probe thread builds 160 MB of live objects and reports how far in its first full collection came; with the default that is at 5 to 9 percent of the work, with the flag at 70 to 83 percent, and the test compares probes with each other because counts of collections depend on the host. A probe made while a text thread starts has the threshold, and probes made after the text thread is online are nearer the default than the flagged one), the quality rows of `document/pdf-text->txt` and `document/pdf-structure->docx` (unchanged: character error rate 0, word F1 1), and the throughput row `document/pdf-text->txt/throughput` of the parity bench.
- **Revisit when**: QA changes the isolation decision for the text thread, the reader stops requiring its canvas module at import or `image-input-limits.ts` is split so that sharp loads on demand (11 ms more off a start), a content-general warm-up beats the noise of the row (read documents with font programs bring the coefficient of variation of the calls from 0.28 to 0.35 down to 0.23 to 0.24 at best, which shows what is left, but a generated one measured within noise), QA accepts a code cache of the reader (25 ms of a start), the cause of the slower reader in the bundle is found, the reader loads its document and font tables in a way that a new thread can take from an immutable snapshot, or the pool takes documents so slowly that the idle replacement is stopped before it is used.

## 63. Flaky unit tests of the shard run: event-loop budgets, scaling ratios, temporary directories, big fixtures and the shard plan

- **Status**: Implemented (flake rows of issue #692, epic #763), decided 2026-10-20 (QA request); limits for programs, liveness checks and the numbering guard added 2026-10-23 (QA request).
- **Decision**:
  - **The event-loop budget cases measure CPU time of the loop thread, not the lateness of a timer.** A timer fires every 5 ms and the case records how much CPU time the thread used since the previous tick (`process.threadCpuUsage()`); the largest value is the longest stretch of work the loop did without turning. The bound is unchanged (`EVENT_LOOP_BLOCK_BUDGET_MS` plus one tick), the synchronous controls still have to exceed it.
  - **A scaling comparison takes extra passes whenever its ratio is over the bound** (up to `MAX_SCALING_PASSES` in all, inside the same time budget). Before, a ratio above 1.5 times the bound was refused after the first three passes as clearly super-linear.
  - **A test that lists a temporary directory before and after a conversion runs the conversion with a `TMPDIR` of its own.** The archive-copy check of the 7z route and the pass-directory checks of the two-pass media tests did not; the RAW decode test already did.
  - **The over-cap encrypted ZIP is written in one pass by a script that implements the traditional ZIP cipher**, not staged as 50,001 files for 7-Zip to pack. 7-Zip lists every entry and decrypts one, so the fixture is still read by an independent tool.
  - **The shard plan records durations from real runs.** Every `tests` shard writes a JSON report and uploads it as `test-report-<shard>`; `scripts/ci-test-shards.mjs --update` merges the reports, drops the entries of files that are no longer sharded, and a test fails when more than 5% of the sharded files have no entry.
  - **A test file that declares an oracle case has a default limit of 60 s** (`tests/helpers/cli-test-timeout.ts`, applied by `tests/helpers/oracle-test.ts` while the file's imports load). It covers the oracle cases and the plain cases around them, which reach the same programs through helpers. A limit the file sets itself afterwards, or a case names, replaces it. The limit stays finite, so a program that hangs still fails the case.
  - **Liveness of a process is asked of `isProcessRunning` / `waitUntilStopped`, never of a signal of 0.** A signal of 0 succeeds on a zombie. `tests/process-liveness-checks.test.ts` fails on `process.kill(pid, 0)` and `kill -0` in the test sources; 13 call sites in 10 files moved to the helper.
  - **A fixture does not outlive a timer the test races.** The process-state zombie fixture ends when its stdin closes, not after 5 s. The case of the job pool that needs a child too slow for a 1 ms deadline puts that job first, on a child that has converted nothing.
  - **The numbering of this document is checked on every pull request** by `scripts/guard-policy-decisions.mjs` (`npm run guard:policy-decisions`, a step of the `changes` job, which documentation-only pull requests also run): no two headings with one number, every heading has a row in the contents and every row a heading, no number twice in the contents, a row's title and link equal its heading's, rows in numeric order within a group. The committed document had 11 faults when the check was written (five entries without a row, a row for 29 that named entry 30, a stale title for 26, rows 49 and 46 out of order, a number twice).
- **Why**:
  - **Timer lateness measures the host.** On a 4-vCPU Linux machine with the thread pinned to two cores that 24 busy loops also used, the level-19 zstd case of the budget file failed 3 times in 8 (118.1, 107.7 and 130.9 ms against 105; the failure on the runner read 112.3), and 3 of 3 under 40 busy loops on one core (259.7, 156.4 and 251.8 ms). The same case through the CPU-time measure passed 30 of 30 under the first load, and 3 of 3 under the second. On the laptop the loop thread held the loop for 0.3 to 6.4 ms in every encode case; the synchronous encoders held it for 430 to 496 ms.
  - **A ratio of 13.09 (0.5 ms -> 7.0 ms) was final.** Work that grows linearly read 13x on a loaded runner; the helper refused anything above 12 (1.5 times 8) at once, so the best of three passes could not recover. The reader's work on 100 KB is 0.4 ms. The failure did not reproduce here: the old test passed 80 of 80 runs (8 busy loops; the 30 heaviest files running beside it), and about 100 measurements of the CPU-time ratio never passed 7.3. The cause of the inflated passes is therefore not known; the fix does not depend on it. Quadratic work stays over the bound on every pass (a reader made quadratic on purpose read 20.3x after the 12 passes), and the replay of the 13.2x case on a virtual clock fails without the change.
  - **Listings of a shared directory race.** A noise process that creates and removes `easyconvert-*.7z` or `easyconvert_pass_*` entries in the shared directory made the old checks fail 5 times in 5; with a private `TMPDIR` the same checks passed 30 of 30.
  - **The fixture's cost grew with its entry count.** Staging 50,001 files, packing them, rejecting the archive and removing the files took 8.6 s on an Apple-silicon laptop (staging 3.3, packing 2.0, extraction refusal 0.3, the `afterEach` removal 2.9) and 2 s on the test VM; on the CI runner the test took 24 s and its hook passed 10 s. The one-pass fixture takes 0.5 s on the VM and the hook removes one file.
  - **A flat second per unrecorded file unbalanced the shards.** 191 of 849 files (22%) had no entry; the six jobs of the last main run took 4:48 to 8:26. Weighed by their time on a machine of the runner's size, the heaviest shard is 1.19 times the mean (1.31 with the old table).
  - **The zombie fixture and the child-survival case were not reproduced.** 200 runs of the old fixture (100 idle, 100 beside 48 busy processes) and 92 runs of the child-survival check (12 as the test, 80 straight from a shell, 40 of them beside 48 busy processes) all passed.
